Biometric Payment Computer Vision: A Deep Architecture Master Guide

Version: 2026-10-03 — expanded advanced edition
Purpose: A single-source technical reading document for understanding the computer-vision and biometric architectures that can sit behind a biometric payment system—from pixels and convolutions to YOLO26, ResNet/ArcFace, DETR, SAM 3, liveness, vector matching, and production payment decisioning.

This document is intentionally long. It is written as a technical book rather than a checklist or curriculum. The goal is not merely to tell you which models exist, but to make the internal logic of each architecture understandable enough that you can reason about design choices rather than memorize names.

How to Read This Book as One Story, Not 97 Separate Definitions

The easiest way to get lost in computer vision is to learn vocabulary in isolation: convolution, ResNet, FPN, CSP, DETR, ArcFace, SAM, HNSW. Each definition can be correct while your mental model remains fragmented. This book is better understood as a sequence of engineering problems. A new architecture usually exists because an older one solved one bottleneck and exposed the next.

The story begins with representation. A camera gives us pixels, but payment logic cannot reason directly over millions of RGB values. CNNs introduced a powerful inductive bias: learn local filters, reuse them over the image, and build progressively more semantic representations. Deeper CNNs then ran into optimization problems; residual connections made depth practical. Efficient convolutions, normalization, bottlenecks, and feature reuse then became the vocabulary from which later detectors were built.

Once useful visual features existed, the next question became localization: not merely what is in the image? but where is it? Early systems repeatedly classified proposed image regions. R-CNN made deep features useful for detection but was expensive. Fast R-CNN shared computation. Faster R-CNN learned the proposals themselves. SSD and YOLO pushed toward one-stage detection: predict objects directly from shared feature maps. The YOLO lineage is therefore not a random list of version numbers. Darknet-53, FPN/PAN, CSP, C3/C2f, anchor-free heads, improved assignment, better losses, attention modules, and finally end-to-end heads are successive answers to recurring questions: how do we preserve useful gradients, detect objects at multiple scales, reduce duplicated computation, assign ground truth more intelligently, and remove post-processing bottlenecks?

For a biometric system, generic detection is only the front door. After locating a face, the problem changes from localization to identity representation. Face detectors such as RetinaFace and SCRFD are specialized because alignment matters: the recognition network should not waste capacity relearning arbitrary head rotation and crop geometry. ResNet-style backbones then convert the aligned face into an embedding. FaceNet established the geometric framing; SphereFace, CosFace, ArcFace and later quality-aware objectives refined the geometry so identities separate cleanly on a hypersphere. At this point the model does not output a person’s name. It outputs a vector whose geometry makes verification possible.

A separate architectural branch asks whether dense convolutional prediction is the only way to detect things. Transformers replace local convolutional bias with token interactions through attention. DETR reformulates detection as set prediction and uses object queries plus bipartite matching, eliminating many hand-designed detector mechanisms. Deformable DETR and later descendants address DETR’s inefficiencies. SAM then builds on the transformer/foundation-model direction but changes the question again: rather than only predicting boxes and fixed classes, it makes segmentation promptable and, in later generations, extends the system with video memory, concept-driven detection, presence reasoning, and tracking.

That leads to another critical distinction: visual understanding is not biometric trust. YOLO can tell you where a face is. SAM can tell you which pixels or tracked object correspond to a prompted concept. ArcFace-style recognition can tell you how similar two identity embeddings are. None of those facts proves that a live authorized account holder is presenting themselves right now. That is why presentation-attack detection, sensor trust, image quality, multi-frame consistency, cryptographic template protection, and secure transaction binding enter the story.

Finally, the system stops being a model-selection problem and becomes a risk and systems-engineering problem. A similarity score has to be calibrated. A 1:N search has retrieval uncertainty that differs from a 1:1 comparison. Edge deployment changes latency, privacy, and attack surfaces. Quantization may shift score distributions. A vector database may retrieve candidates but must not become the biometric decision-maker. A risk engine combines signals, while a payment authorization layer makes the consequential decision under explicit policy and cryptographic controls.

So as you read, keep this chain in mind:

pixels
  -> learned visual features
  -> localized objects/faces
  -> normalized face geometry
  -> identity embeddings
  -> calibrated biometric comparison
  -> liveness + sensor trust
  -> multi-frame / retrieval / quality reasoning
  -> risk policy
  -> cryptographically bound authorization
  -> payment

And keep a second, historical chain beside it:

hand-designed / repeated-region vision
  -> shared CNN features
  -> one-stage dense detection
  -> feature pyramids + better gradient flow
  -> anchor-free / quality-aware detection
  -> transformer set prediction
  -> promptable foundation segmentation/tracking
  -> multimodal, security-aware production systems

The chapters below deliberately revisit some concepts later. Those revisits are not accidental duplication. The first pass gives you the architectural map; the advanced pass returns after you already know the vocabulary and derives the mechanics more deeply. Think of it as learning the city once by roads, then revisiting it through the electrical grid, water system, and building foundations.

1. The Mental Model: A Biometric Payment System Is Not One AI Model

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous—
This chapter1. The Mental Model: A Biometric Payment System Is Not One AI Model
Next2. Computer Vision Foundations: Pixels, Tensors, Features, and Receptive Fields

A recurring mistake in discussions about biometric payment is to ask something like:

“Should the system use YOLO, CNN, or SAM?”

That question mixes different abstraction levels.

A CNN is a general architectural primitive or family of neural networks.

YOLO is an object-detection architecture family that uses many CNN-style primitives and, in recent variants, attention modules.

SAM 3 is a promptable detection/segmentation/tracking foundation model built around transformer mechanisms and a DETR-like detector.

ArcFace is primarily a training loss/objective for face embeddings, not a detector and not a complete face-recognition architecture.

ResNet is a neural-network backbone architecture based on residual learning.

A payment system may use several of these ideas simultaneously because they solve different problems.

A useful decomposition is:

camera/sensor
    |
    v
capture integrity + quality assessment
    |
    v
person / face localization
    |
    +----> scene understanding / segmentation / tracking
    |
    v
face landmarks + alignment
    |
    +----> presentation attack detection / liveness
    |
    v
face embedding network
    |
    v
biometric matching
    |
    v
identity confidence
    |
    v
transaction risk engine
    |
    v
authorization policy
    |
    v
payment rail

Each block answers a different question:

Stage Question
Detection Where is a face/person?
Segmentation Which pixels belong to the object?
Tracking Is this the same visual object across frames?
Alignment Can I normalize the face geometry before recognition?
Liveness/PAD Is this a genuine live presentation rather than an attack artifact?
Recognition What identity information does this face encode?
Verification Does the biometric match the claimed account?
Identification Which enrolled person is the best candidate?
Risk Should this transaction be trusted given all signals?
Authorization Should money actually move?

The crucial engineering rule is:

A biometric model should never be treated as the payment authorization engine.

An embedding similarity score is a noisy statistical measurement. It is not a cryptographic proof, and it is not a complete fraud decision.

1.1 Recognition, verification, and identification are different

Suppose a camera sees a face and a neural network outputs a vector:

[ 0.012, -0.441, 0.037, 0.811, ... ]

That vector is a face embedding.

Now there are at least two ways to use it.

Verification: 1:1

The person first claims an identity:

"I am account A."

The system compares the captured embedding only to account A’s registered template.

capture ---> embedding E_query
                    |
                    v
              compare with
                    |
                    v
            E_account_A

This is 1:1 biometric verification.

Identification: 1:N

The person provides no identity claim.

The system searches a gallery:

E_query
   |
   +--> Alice template
   +--> Bob template
   +--> Carol template
   +--> ...
   +--> Nth enrolled person

This is 1:N identification.

The difference is not cosmetic. If your gallery contains millions of users, the probability that someone happens to produce a relatively high impostor similarity becomes an important systems problem. Candidate retrieval, calibration, demographic performance, fraud controls, and second-factor policy become more critical.

A payment system that says:

camera -> nearest face in 50 million identities -> pay

has a very different risk profile from:

authenticated account/device intent
       +
live face verification against that one account
       +
risk checks
       =
authorization

1.2 Detection is not recognition

If YOLO predicts:

class = face
bbox = [x1, y1, x2, y2]
confidence = 0.99

the model has answered:

“I think there is a face at this location.”

It has not answered:

“This face belongs to Razi.”

A detector is trained to be invariant to identity. In fact, for the class face, it is useful if every person’s face maps to the same semantic category. A biometric recognizer has almost the opposite goal: it must preserve subtle identity-discriminative differences while ignoring nuisance variations such as lighting, pose, expression, and camera properties.

That difference explains why a practical pipeline often contains both:

face detector -> aligned crop -> face recognition backbone -> embedding

2. Computer Vision Foundations: Pixels, Tensors, Features, and Receptive Fields

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous1. The Mental Model: A Biometric Payment System Is Not One AI Model
This chapter2. Computer Vision Foundations: Pixels, Tensors, Features, and Receptive Fields
Next3. Convolutional Neural Networks from First Principles

Before YOLO, ResNet, ArcFace, or SAM makes sense, you need a precise model of what a neural network sees.

Humans see:

a face, eyes, expression, glasses, a person standing in front of a terminal.

A neural network begins with numbers.

An RGB image is a tensor.

For an image of height H, width W, and three channels:

X∈ℝH×W×3 X \in \mathbb{R}^{H \times W \times 3}

or, in common deep-learning frameworks, perhaps:

X∈ℝ3×H×W X \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times H \times W}

For a training batch of B images:

X∈ℝB×C×H×W X \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times C \times H \times W}

where:

  • B = batch size
  • C = number of channels
  • H = height
  • W = width

A 640×640 RGB image therefore starts conceptually as:

[3, 640, 640]

before batching.

Pixel values may originally be integers in [0,255], then be normalized into [0,1], [-1,1], or standardized using channel means and standard deviations.

2.1 A channel is not necessarily “a color”

At the input:

channel 0 = red
channel 1 = green
channel 2 = blue

After the first convolution, channels no longer mean colors.

Suppose a convolution produces 64 output channels:

[3, 640, 640]
      |
      v
Conv2D
      |
      v
[64, 320, 320]

Those 64 channels are learned feature maps.

One may become strongly activated by vertical edges. Another may react to color transitions. Another may respond to diagonal textures. Another may eventually participate in representing eyes or corners.

Do not interpret a hidden channel too literally as a human-named concept. The network learns a distributed representation. A semantic property can be encoded across many channels.

2.2 Spatial resolution versus semantic richness

Deep vision networks often gradually transform:

large spatial map + few channels
                |
                v
smaller spatial map + more channels

For example:

3 x 640 x 640
      |
      v
64 x 320 x 320
      |
      v
128 x 160 x 160
      |
      v
256 x 80 x 80
      |
      v
512 x 40 x 40
      |
      v
1024 x 20 x 20

Why?

Early layers preserve exact spatial information.

Deep layers have seen larger regions of the image and can represent more abstract semantics.

This creates a fundamental detection tradeoff:

  • high-resolution feature maps are useful for small-object localization;
  • low-resolution deep features carry stronger semantics and broad context.

FPN and PAN-style architectures exist largely because we want both.

2.3 Stride

A stride-2 operation roughly halves spatial dimensions:

640 x 640
   |
stride 2
   v
320 x 320

After repeated downsampling:

640 / 2 = 320
320 / 2 = 160
160 / 2 = 80
80  / 2 = 40
40  / 2 = 20

Detector documentation often names feature levels according to effective stride:

P3 = stride 8
P4 = stride 16
P5 = stride 32

For a 640×640 input:

P3 -> 80 x 80
P4 -> 40 x 40
P5 -> 20 x 20

This notation will repeatedly appear throughout YOLO, FPN, RetinaFace, and related detectors.

2.4 Receptive field

A neuron in a deep feature map is influenced by some region of the original image. That region is its receptive field.

A 3×3 convolution sees only a 3×3 local neighborhood at its own layer. But stacking convolutions grows the effective receptive field:

input pixels
   |
3x3 conv
   |   sees local region
3x3 conv
   |   now depends on a larger input region
3x3 conv
   |   larger still
...

The network gradually builds local patterns into increasingly global structures.

A useful conceptual progression:

pixels
  -> edges
  -> corners/textures
  -> motifs
  -> parts
  -> object-level structures

This hierarchy is one reason CNNs became so powerful for vision.

2.5 RGB, YUV/YCbCr, HSV, and why color representation matters

RGB stores three additive color channels. It is natural for displays and is the most common representation passed into general-purpose vision models.

Video pipelines, however, frequently use YUV-family or YCbCr representations. Conceptually they separate:

  • Y — luminance/brightness-like information;
  • chroma channels — color-difference information.

This is useful because human perception is more sensitive to luminance detail than chroma detail, so video codecs can subsample chroma aggressively. A camera stream may therefore physically arrive as formats such as NV12/YUV420 and only later be converted to RGB.

That conversion is not free. A low-latency kiosk pipeline should ask whether preprocessing copies and color conversions dominate model time.

HSV reorganizes color into approximately:

  • hue;
  • saturation;
  • value/brightness.

HSV is useful for classical image processing because illumination and color can sometimes be separated more intuitively, but neural networks do not automatically benefit from HSV input. The training and deployment color pipeline must match.

A subtle production bug is:

training: RGB
deployment camera library: BGR

The model still runs, but its statistics are wrong.

2.6 Normalization versus standardization

These words are often used loosely.

Simple range normalization:

x′=x/255 x' = x/255

maps 8-bit pixels into [0,1].

Standardization uses a mean and standard deviation:

x′c=xc−μcσc x'_c=\frac{x_c-\mu_c}{\sigma_c}

usually per channel.

For example, an ImageNet-pretrained backbone may expect a particular RGB mean/std convention. A face-recognition model may expect pixels transformed into [-1,1].

Preprocessing is part of the model specification. Using the wrong normalization can reduce accuracy even if the neural-network weights are identical.

2.7 Effective receptive field is not the same as theoretical receptive field

The theoretical receptive field tells you which input pixels can influence a deep activation. The effective receptive field describes where influence is actually concentrated.

Even when a deep neuron can theoretically depend on most of the image, gradients/influence are often concentrated nearer the center. Architectural components such as SPP/SPPF, dilation, FPN fusion, and attention change how context is aggregated.

This matters because simply calculating a large theoretical receptive field does not guarantee the model uses global context strongly.

3. Convolutional Neural Networks from First Principles

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous2. Computer Vision Foundations: Pixels, Tensors, Features, and Receptive Fields
This chapter3. Convolutional Neural Networks from First Principles
Next4. Modern CNN Building Blocks: BatchNorm, Residuals, Bottlenecks, Depthwise Convolution

A convolutional neural network uses learnable kernels that are applied spatially.

Consider a single-channel 3×3 patch:

X=[x11x12x13x21x22x23x31x32x33] X = \begin{bmatrix} x_{11} & x_{12} & x_{13}\\ x_{21} & x_{22} & x_{23}\\ x_{31} & x_{32} & x_{33} \end{bmatrix}

and a learned 3×3 kernel:

K=[w11w12w13w21w22w23w31w32w33] K = \begin{bmatrix} w_{11} & w_{12} & w_{13}\\ w_{21} & w_{22} & w_{23}\\ w_{31} & w_{32} & w_{33} \end{bmatrix}

One output position is essentially a weighted sum:

y=∑i=13∑j=13xijwij+b y = \sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}x_{ij}w_{ij} + b

The same learned weights are reused across spatial positions.

This is weight sharing.

3.1 Why weight sharing matters

Imagine a vertical edge.

If the model learns a useful detector for a vertical edge in the top-left of an image, the same visual pattern should still matter in the center or bottom-right.

A fully connected network would need to independently learn many spatially duplicated relationships.

A convolution encodes a useful inductive bias:

local visual patterns can matter regardless of absolute position.

That reduces parameter count and improves statistical efficiency.

3.2 Multi-channel convolution

Real convolutions operate across channels.

If the input has C_in channels and the layer has C_out kernels, each kernel spans all input channels:

K∈ℝCout×Cin×kh×kw K \in \mathbb{R}^{C_{out} \times C_{in} \times k_h \times k_w}

For example:

input:  64 x 80 x 80
kernel: 128 filters, each 64 x 3 x 3
output: 128 x 80 x 80

Each output channel represents a learned combination of local structures across all input channels.

3.3 Padding

Without padding, a convolution reduces spatial size.

For one dimension:

O=⌊I+2P−D(K−1)−1S+1⌋ O = \left\lfloor \frac{I + 2P - D(K-1)-1}{S} + 1 \right\rfloor

where:

  • I = input size
  • P = padding
  • D = dilation
  • K = kernel size
  • S = stride
  • O = output size

A common 3×3 convolution uses:

kernel = 3
stride = 1
padding = 1

so height and width are preserved.

3.4 Strided convolution

If stride becomes 2:

kernel 3x3
stride 2
padding 1

the convolution learns features while downsampling.

Modern detectors often prefer strided convolution over old-style max pooling for much of the network because the downsampling itself becomes learnable.

3.5 1×1 convolution is not useless

A 1×1 convolution seems strange because it does not combine neighboring pixels spatially.

Its power is channel mixing.

Suppose:

input: 256 x 40 x 40

A 1×1 convolution can turn it into:

output: 128 x 40 x 40

At every spatial position, it learns a linear combination of the 256 input channels.

This is useful for:

  • reducing channels before expensive operations;
  • increasing channels afterward;
  • fusing concatenated features;
  • implementing bottlenecks;
  • constructing CSP branches;
  • projection layers.

Much of modern CNN architecture engineering is really about controlling where expensive spatial convolution happens and where cheap 1×1 channel mixing is enough.

3.6 Dilation

A dilated convolution inserts gaps between sampled positions.

A 3×3 kernel with dilation 2 can cover a larger effective region without increasing the number of kernel weights.

Conceptually:

x . x . x
. . . . .
x . x . x
. . . . .
x . x . x

This increases receptive field while preserving spatial resolution.

Dilated/atrous convolution became especially important in dense prediction and segmentation systems.

3.7 Parameters versus FLOPs

Two quantities are frequently confused.

Parameters

How many learnable numbers are stored.

For a normal convolution:

params≈CoutCinKhKw \text{params} \approx C_{out} C_{in} K_h K_w

ignoring bias.

FLOPs

How much arithmetic is required during inference/training.

A model can have modest parameter count but still be computationally expensive if it repeatedly applies those parameters over very large spatial maps.

This is why architectural placement matters.

A 3×3 convolution at 160×160 can be far more expensive than a similar convolution at 20×20.

4. Modern CNN Building Blocks: BatchNorm, Residuals, Bottlenecks, Depthwise Convolution

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous3. Convolutional Neural Networks from First Principles
This chapter4. Modern CNN Building Blocks: BatchNorm, Residuals, Bottlenecks, Depthwise Convolution
Next5. What Object Detection Actually Solves

YOLO’s named modules—Darknet-53, CSP, C3, C2f, C3k2—are combinations of a smaller vocabulary.

Understanding the vocabulary first makes the named modules much easier.

4.1 Activation functions

A stack of only linear operations is still mathematically equivalent to one linear operation.

Neural networks therefore need nonlinearities.

Historical CNNs often used ReLU:

ReLU(x)=max(0,x) \operatorname{ReLU}(x) = \max(0,x)

Modern YOLO implementations commonly use SiLU, also known as Swish:

SiLU(x)=xσ(x) \operatorname{SiLU}(x) = x \sigma(x)

where σ is the sigmoid function.

SiLU is smooth and allows small negative outputs rather than clipping everything below zero.

A common Ultralytics convolutional unit is conceptually:

Conv2D -> BatchNorm -> SiLU

4.2 Batch normalization

BatchNorm normalizes activations and then applies learned scale and shift parameters.

A simplified form:

x̂=x−μσ2+ϵ \hat{x} = \frac{x-\mu}{\sqrt{\sigma^2+\epsilon}}

y=γx̂+β y = \gamma \hat{x} + \beta

Why it became popular:

  • stabilizes optimization;
  • allows more aggressive learning rates in many settings;
  • reduces sensitivity to activation scale;
  • works well in CNN pipelines.

At deployment, convolution and BatchNorm parameters can often be fused algebraically into one convolution for faster inference.

4.3 Residual connections

This is the central idea behind ResNet and also appears throughout detection architectures.

Instead of requiring a block to learn:

H(x) H(x)

directly, learn a residual:

F(x)=H(x)−x F(x)=H(x)-x

and output:

y=F(x)+x y=F(x)+x

Diagram:

x ----------------------------+
|                              |
v                              |
Conv -> Norm -> Act -> Conv ---+
                               |
                               v
                             y = F(x)+x

If the optimal transformation is close to identity, the block can learn a small residual rather than reconstructing identity from scratch.

Residual connections also provide short paths for gradient propagation.

This made substantially deeper networks easier to optimize.

The original ResNet paper by He et al. explicitly framed deep-network layers as learning residual functions relative to their inputs rather than unreferenced mappings.

4.4 The residual bottleneck

ResNet-50/101/152 commonly use a bottleneck structure:

1x1 reduce channels
      |
      v
3x3 spatial processing
      |
      v
1x1 expand channels
      |
      + residual

For example:

256 channels
     |
1x1 -> 64
     |
3x3 -> 64
     |
1x1 -> 256
     |
+ shortcut

Why bottleneck?

Because a 3×3 convolution over 256 channels is expensive. Reduce the channel dimension, perform the spatial operation in the smaller latent width, then expand.

The term bottleneck therefore usually refers to a narrower intermediate representation.

4.5 Concatenation versus addition

Two common ways to merge features:

Addition

A ----+
      +--> A + B
B ----+

Channels stay the same.

Used heavily in residual connections.

Concatenation

A ------+
        +--> [A ; B]
B ------+

Channels are stacked.

If A and B each have 128 channels:

concat -> 256 channels

CSP-style architectures use concatenation heavily because they intentionally preserve separate feature/gradient paths and fuse them later.

4.6 Depthwise separable convolution

A standard convolution jointly mixes spatial and channel information.

A depthwise separable design breaks it into:

  1. depthwise convolution — one spatial kernel per channel;
  2. pointwise 1×1 convolution — mix channels.

Standard convolution cost roughly scales as:

HWCinCoutK2 H W C_{in} C_{out} K^2

Depthwise + pointwise roughly scales as:

HWCinK2+HWCinCout H W C_{in}K^2 + H W C_{in}C_{out}

This can be much cheaper.

Architectures designed for phones/edge devices—MobileNet and MobileFaceNet are classic examples—exploit this heavily.

YOLO11’s classification branches also use lighter depthwise-separable operations in places to improve efficiency, according to current Ultralytics architecture documentation.

4.7 Pooling

Max pooling

Select the largest value in a neighborhood:

y=max(x1,…,xn) y = \max(x_1,\ldots,x_n)

It emphasizes whether a strong feature exists somewhere locally.

Average pooling

Average the neighborhood.

Global average pooling

Reduce each entire feature map to one number:

C x H x W
   |
global average over H,W
   v
C

This often appears near classification or embedding heads.

4.8 Upsampling

FPN-style feature fusion requires making a low-resolution map larger.

Example:

20 x 20
   |
2x upsample
   v
40 x 40

Then it can be combined with a 40×40 feature map from an earlier stage.

Nearest-neighbor upsampling itself has no learned parameters. It is often followed by learned convolutional processing.

Transposed convolution

A transposed convolution is a learned upsampling operator related to the transpose of the matrix representation of convolution. It is sometimes misleadingly called “deconvolution”; it does not generally invert a convolution.

Conceptually:

low-resolution feature map
       |
transposed convolution
       v
higher-resolution learned feature map

Advantages:

  • learned upsampling;
  • can jointly increase resolution and transform channels.

Risks:

  • checkerboard artifacts if kernel/stride geometry is poorly chosen;
  • more compute than simple interpolation + convolution in some deployments.

Modern segmentation decoders often compare transposed convolution with nearest/bilinear upsampling followed by ordinary convolution.

4.9 BatchNorm, LayerNorm, and GroupNorm are not interchangeable

BatchNorm

Normalizes a channel using statistics computed across batch/spatial samples. It works extremely well in conventional CNN training when batches are sufficiently representative.

LayerNorm

Normalizes features within each sample/token rather than depending on batch statistics. This is why transformers heavily favor LayerNorm: token processing should not depend on having a large image batch.

For token x with feature dimension d:

x̂=x−μfeaturesσfeatures2+ϵ \hat{x}=\frac{x-\mu_{features}}{\sqrt{\sigma^2_{features}+\epsilon}}

followed by learned affine scale/shift.

GroupNorm

Divides channels into groups and normalizes within each group.

64 channels
  -> 8 groups
  -> 8 channels/group

GroupNorm is useful when batch size is very small, as can happen in high-resolution detection/segmentation training where BatchNorm estimates become unstable.

The choice affects both optimization and deployment behavior. BatchNorm has running statistics and can often be fused into convolution at inference; LayerNorm/GroupNorm remain active normalization operations.

4.10 Why architecture diagrams look more complicated than the math

A diagram may show:

C3k2 -> SPPF -> C2PSA -> Upsample -> Concat -> C3k2

This looks like a collection of unrelated names.

But each block decomposes into primitives:

convolution
normalization
activation
split
residual add
concatenate
pool
attention
MLP
upsample

A useful learning habit is:

Whenever you see a named architecture block, recursively expand it until you reach operations you already understand.

That is exactly what the later YOLO and SAM sections do.

Connection Bridge — From Feature Extraction to Detection

Chapters 2–4 built the machinery that can turn pixels into hierarchical features. That machinery by itself still answers a classification-style question: what patterns exist? Detection adds geometry. The model must now preserve enough spatial information to say where an object is while still learning features abstract enough to say what the object is. That tension—semantic abstraction versus spatial precision—is why detector architectures soon need multi-scale feature maps, pyramids, specialized heads, and localization losses.

The YOLO story that follows should therefore be read as an evolving answer to one problem: how can a network share expensive visual computation once, then convert the resulting features into many accurate object predictions fast enough for real-time use?

5. What Object Detection Actually Solves

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous4. Modern CNN Building Blocks: BatchNorm, Residuals, Bottlenecks, Depthwise Convolution
This chapter5. What Object Detection Actually Solves
Next6. The YOLO Family: Historical Evolution and Core Idea

Image classification maps an image to a label:

image -> "person"

Object detection must output a set:

{(bi,ci,si)}i=1N \{(b_i, c_i, s_i)\}_{i=1}^{N}

where:

  • bib_i is a bounding box;
  • cic_i is a class/category;
  • sis_i is a confidence score.

For example:

[
  {class: person, box: [40, 31, 250, 620], score: 0.98},
  {class: face,   box: [91, 50, 170, 147], score: 0.96},
  {class: phone,  box: [310, 320, 410, 510], score: 0.88}
]

That creates three subproblems:

  1. representation — extract useful image features;
  2. localization — predict where each object is;
  3. classification — predict what each object is.

There is also a hidden fourth problem:

  1. assignment — during training, decide which model prediction is responsible for which ground-truth object.

And a historical fifth:

  1. duplicate suppression — at inference, remove multiple predictions for the same object.

DETR and NMS-free YOLO designs are partly about redesigning problems four and five.

5.1 Bounding-box parameterizations

A box can be represented as:

x1, y1, x2, y2

or:

center_x, center_y, width, height

or distances from a reference point:

left, top, right, bottom

Modern anchor-free detectors frequently use distance-to-boundary style representations.

A reference location p might predict:

l = distance to left boundary
t = distance to top boundary
r = distance to right boundary
b = distance to bottom boundary

Then the model reconstructs the box.

5.2 Intersection over Union

To measure geometric overlap between a predicted box B_p and ground truth B_g:

IoU=|Bp∩Bg||Bp∪Bg| IoU = \frac{|B_p \cap B_g|}{|B_p \cup B_g|}

If they perfectly overlap:

IoU = 1

If they do not overlap:

IoU = 0

IoU is both:

  • an evaluation concept;
  • a component of many box regression losses;
  • historically an important ingredient in label assignment and NMS.

5.3 Precision and recall

Suppose the detector outputs 100 face boxes.

If 90 are real faces and 10 are false detections:

Precision=TPTP+FP Precision = \frac{TP}{TP+FP}

If the image dataset actually contained 120 faces and the detector found 90:

Recall=TPTP+FN Recall = \frac{TP}{TP+FN}

A system can increase precision by being conservative, but may lose recall.

For biometric capture, false negatives and false positives affect different UX/security failure modes.

5.4 Average Precision and mAP

Object-detection benchmarks often evaluate Average Precision across confidence thresholds and IoU criteria.

COCO commonly reports:

AP@[0.50:0.95]

meaning AP averaged over IoU thresholds from 0.50 to 0.95.

Do not confuse:

detector mAP

with:

face-verification TAR/FAR

They evaluate different systems and should not be directly compared.

6. The YOLO Family: Historical Evolution and Core Idea

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous5. What Object Detection Actually Solves
This chapter6. The YOLO Family: Historical Evolution and Core Idea
Next7. YOLOv1: Detection as a Single Regression Problem

YOLO began with Joseph Redmon and collaborators’ 2016 paper “You Only Look Once: Unified, Real-Time Object Detection.” The defining move was to treat detection as a unified prediction problem from the full image instead of a pipeline that first generated candidate regions and then classified them.

Primary source: Redmon et al., YOLO, CVPR 2016.

The original slogan is architectural:

one network evaluation predicts boxes and class probabilities for the whole image.

That is why YOLO became associated with one-stage detection.

Before one-stage detection became dominant for real-time use, region-based methods had a conceptual pipeline closer to:

image
  |
region proposals
  |
crop/align region features
  |
classify each proposal
  |
refine boxes

YOLO instead moves toward:

image
  |
shared feature extractor
  |
dense prediction
  |
boxes + classes

The exact implementation changed enormously across generations.

So when somebody says:

“YOLO architecture”

you should immediately ask:

Which generation and which implementation lineage?

There is no single monolithic YOLO architecture in 2026.

6.1 The YOLO name is now an ecosystem, not a single linear codebase

Historically relevant lines include:

  • YOLOv1 — Redmon et al.
  • YOLOv2 / YOLO9000 — Redmon & Farhadi
  • YOLOv3 — Redmon & Farhadi
  • YOLOv4 — Bochkovskiy, Wang, Liao
  • YOLOv5 — Ultralytics
  • YOLOv6 — Meituan
  • YOLOv7 — Wang, Bochkovskiy, Liao
  • YOLOv8 — Ultralytics
  • YOLOv9 — Wang, Yeh, Liao
  • YOLOv10 — Tsinghua-associated research line
  • YOLO11 — Ultralytics
  • YOLO12 — attention-centric research line
  • YOLO26 — current Ultralytics family released in 2026

You should therefore avoid statements such as:

“YOLOv10 evolved into YOLO11 and then YOLO26.”

That is too simplistic because different versions come from different teams, codebases, and research lineages.

A better statement is:

The broader YOLO family shares the real-time one-stage detection tradition, while individual generations borrow, modify, or independently rediscover architectural ideas such as CSP connections, feature pyramids, decoupled heads, anchor-free regression, attention, and end-to-end assignment.

Current Ultralytics documentation explicitly describes its own architectural progression from YOLOv3/YOLOv5-style blocks through YOLOv8, YOLO11, and YOLO26: Ultralytics YOLO Architecture Guide.

7. YOLOv1: Detection as a Single Regression Problem

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous6. The YOLO Family: Historical Evolution and Core Idea
This chapter7. YOLOv1: Detection as a Single Regression Problem
Next8. YOLOv2 / YOLO9000: Anchors, Multi-Scale Training, Darknet-19

The original YOLO divides the image into a grid.

Historically, a cell is responsible for detecting an object when the center of that object’s ground-truth box falls into the cell.

Conceptually:

+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
|    |    |    |    |    |    |    |
+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
|    |    |    |FACE|    |    |    |
+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
|    |    |    |  x |    |    |    |
+----+----+----+----+----+----+----+
...

Each grid position predicts box coordinates, confidence, and class probabilities.

The original YOLO formulation is useful pedagogically because it exposes the core insight:

Do not scan the image with a classifier. Predict object geometry and semantics directly from the whole image.

7.1 Why this was fast

Earlier region-based systems often repeated expensive processing for many proposals.

YOLO shared almost all image computation.

The network sees global context once and emits many detections.

This creates the characteristic real-time advantage.

7.2 Why early YOLO struggled with localization

A coarse grid constrains spatial assignment.

Multiple small nearby objects can compete for limited prediction capacity.

Early YOLO also had relatively crude box parameterization and loss design by modern standards.

That produced a recurring pattern in early comparisons:

  • very fast;
  • strong semantic/background reasoning;
  • more localization errors than slower proposal-based systems.

Many later YOLO innovations can be understood as attacks on these limitations.

8. YOLOv2 / YOLO9000: Anchors, Multi-Scale Training, Darknet-19

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous7. YOLOv1: Detection as a Single Regression Problem
This chapter8. YOLOv2 / YOLO9000: Anchors, Multi-Scale Training, Darknet-19
Next9. YOLOv3 and Darknet-53 in Detail

YOLOv2, described in YOLO9000: Better, Faster, Stronger, introduced a collection of improvements rather than one isolated trick.

Primary source: Redmon & Farhadi, YOLO9000 / YOLOv2.

Important ideas included:

  • Batch normalization;
  • a stronger Darknet-19 backbone;
  • anchor boxes;
  • dimension clustering;
  • direct location prediction;
  • fine-grained features;
  • multi-scale training;
  • joint classification/detection training for YOLO9000.

8.1 Why anchor boxes existed

Objects have different aspect ratios:

person: tall and narrow
car:    wide and short
ball:   roughly square

Instead of predicting every possible shape from an unconstrained starting point, anchor-based detection begins from reference templates.

At a feature-map location:

anchor A -> tall/narrow
anchor B -> wide
anchor C -> square-ish

The network predicts offsets relative to an anchor.

That injects prior information into box prediction.

8.2 Dimension clustering

YOLOv2 used clustering over training-set box dimensions to choose anchor priors better suited to the dataset instead of hand-selecting arbitrary shapes.

This is an important conceptual transition:

use the empirical box-shape distribution to define priors.

Later anchor-free approaches move away from this requirement altogether.

8.3 Multi-scale training

Convolutional detectors can often operate on different input sizes.

YOLOv2 exploited this by changing training resolution periodically.

The model therefore learned to operate under different speed/accuracy tradeoffs.

That was a useful demonstration that a fully convolutional detector need not be tied to one exact input shape.

8.4 Darknet-19

Darknet-19 was the backbone used by YOLOv2.

The name means approximately:

  • Darknet framework/code family;
  • 19 convolutional layers in the backbone architecture.

By YOLOv3, this would be replaced by Darknet-53, which is much more relevant to the architecture lineage you asked about.

Connection Bridge — Why Darknet-53 Is a Turning Point

YOLOv1 proved that detection could be treated as a single learned prediction problem. YOLOv2 improved resolution handling, anchors, and backbone design. YOLOv3 is where several ideas that later become familiar across the YOLO family line up at once: a much deeper residual backbone, predictions at multiple scales, and a clearer separation between representation and detection heads.

Darknet-53 matters because it inherits the lesson of ResNet: making a network deeper is useful only if gradients can actually train it. The next chapters—FPN/PAN, SPP, CSP, C3/C2f and C2PSA—are all descendants of the same systems question: how do we move information and gradients through a deep detector without wasting computation or destroying spatial detail?

9. YOLOv3 and Darknet-53 in Detail

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous8. YOLOv2 / YOLO9000: Anchors, Multi-Scale Training, Darknet-19
This chapter9. YOLOv3 and Darknet-53 in Detail
Next10. Feature Pyramids: FPN, PAN/PANet, P3/P4/P5 and Multi-Scale Detection

YOLOv3 was published as “YOLOv3: An Incremental Improvement” by Joseph Redmon and Ali Farhadi in 2018.

Primary source: YOLOv3 paper.

It introduced or consolidated several ideas that became deeply influential:

  • Darknet-53 backbone;
  • residual connections;
  • prediction at three scales;
  • logistic objectness/class predictions;
  • anchor-based box prediction.

Current Ultralytics architecture documentation still uses YOLOv3 as a useful historical baseline for explaining the evolution of its model blocks.

9.1 Why Darknet-53 is called “53”

Darknet-53 is a 53-convolutional-layer feature extractor.

Its rough design is:

Conv
Conv stride 2
Residual blocks x1

Conv stride 2
Residual blocks x2

Conv stride 2
Residual blocks x8

Conv stride 2
Residual blocks x8

Conv stride 2
Residual blocks x4

The exact channel progression grows as spatial resolution shrinks.

A simplified shape sketch for a 416×416 image:

416x416
   |
   v
208x208
   |
   v
104x104
   |
   v
52x52
   |
   v
26x26
   |
   v
13x13

Deeper stages contain many more channels.

9.2 Darknet residual block

A typical conceptual block uses:

x
|\
| 1x1 Conv (channel reduction)
|      |
|    3x3 Conv
|      |
+------+
   add
    |
    v
output

This is similar in spirit to ResNet: the transform learns a residual relative to identity.

Why 1×1 then 3×3?

The 1×1 convolution controls channel width and reduces expensive spatial computation.

9.3 Darknet-53 versus ResNet

They share the residual-learning idea but are not the same architecture.

ResNet families use standardized “basic” and “bottleneck” blocks and were designed primarily around image classification.

Darknet-53 was optimized in the YOLO ecosystem for detection speed/accuracy and uses a different arrangement of convolutional blocks.

The broader lesson is more important than memorizing layer counts:

Backbone design is the representation engine.

The detector head can only localize/classify as well as the backbone exposes useful features.

9.4 Three-scale prediction

YOLOv3 predicts at three feature resolutions.

For a 416×416 input, historically this is approximately:

13 x 13   -> large objects
26 x 26   -> medium objects
52 x 52   -> smaller objects

For a 640×640 modern input, the analogous stride levels are:

P5: 20 x 20  (stride 32)
P4: 40 x 40  (stride 16)
P3: 80 x 80  (stride 8)

Why does high resolution help small objects?

A tiny face may occupy only a few pixels on a 20×20 feature map.

At 80×80, the same region spans more spatial cells and retains finer localization information.

9.5 YOLOv3’s multi-scale feature fusion

YOLOv3 takes deep semantic features, upsamples them, and combines them with earlier higher-resolution features.

This is conceptually related to FPN-style top-down fusion.

deep 13x13 semantic feature
          |
       upsample
          v
        26x26
          |
          + concatenate with earlier 26x26 feature
          |
          v
     prediction processing

Then a similar process reaches the higher-resolution scale.

The key idea is:

deep layers know what; shallow layers remember where.

Feature pyramids try to have both.

10. Feature Pyramids: FPN, PAN/PANet, P3/P4/P5 and Multi-Scale Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous9. YOLOv3 and Darknet-53 in Detail
This chapter10. Feature Pyramids: FPN, PAN/PANet, P3/P4/P5 and Multi-Scale Detection
Next11. SPP and SPPF: Expanding Receptive Field Cheaply

The Feature Pyramid Network (FPN) paper by Lin et al. formalized a top-down architecture with lateral connections for constructing semantically strong feature maps at multiple scales.

Primary source: Feature Pyramid Networks for Object Detection.

A CNN naturally creates a bottom-up pyramid:

C3: high resolution, weaker semantics
  |
  v
C4: medium resolution
  |
  v
C5: low resolution, stronger semantics

The problem is that the high-resolution layers are good for localization but semantically weaker.

FPN creates a top-down semantic path:

        C5
        |
       P5
        |
     upsample
        v
C4 ---> + ---> P4
              |
           upsample
              v
C3 ----------> + ---> P3

The arrows from C4 and C3 are lateral connections.

10.1 Why the pyramid matters in a payment camera

Suppose a camera frame contains:

  • the full customer body;
  • a face;
  • eyes;
  • a phone;
  • a QR code;
  • a card;
  • other people in the background.

These objects occupy radically different fractions of the image.

A single feature resolution is inefficient because:

  • low resolution is excellent for big objects;
  • high resolution is necessary for small objects.

A pyramid lets one model service all scales.

10.2 P3, P4, P5 notation

The P levels generally correspond to pyramid outputs.

A common mapping:

P3 -> stride 8
P4 -> stride 16
P5 -> stride 32

For input 640:

P3 -> 80 x 80
P4 -> 40 x 40
P5 -> 20 x 20

Each cell in a stride-8 map is associated with an 8-pixel step in the input coordinate system.

The effective receptive field is larger than merely 8×8 because deep convolutions accumulate context.

10.3 PAN / PANet bottom-up augmentation

The original Path Aggregation Network (PANet) introduced bottom-up path augmentation to shorten information flow from lower layers to deeper feature levels.

Primary source: Path Aggregation Network for Instance Segmentation.

In modern YOLO explanations, the neck is often described as an FPN + PAN style structure.

Top-down FPN:

P5
 |
upsample
 |
+ P4
 |
upsample
 |
+ P3

Then bottom-up path:

P3 fused
 |
stride-2 conv
 |
+ P4 fused
 |
stride-2 conv
 |
+ P5 fused

This creates bidirectional information exchange:

  • high-level semantics travel toward fine resolutions;
  • detailed localization information travels back toward coarser levels.

Current Ultralytics YOLO architecture documentation explicitly describes YOLO11/YOLO26 necks as top-down FPN followed by bottom-up PAN-style fusion.

10.4 Why concatenate after up/downsampling

Suppose:

A = deep semantic feature, 40x40
B = backbone lateral feature, 40x40

After spatial sizes match:

concat(A, B)

preserves both representations.

A convolutional block after concatenation learns how to fuse them.

This is different from simply adding them.

Concatenation lets downstream layers choose which channels to retain rather than forcing immediate element-wise summation.

11. SPP and SPPF: Expanding Receptive Field Cheaply

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous10. Feature Pyramids: FPN, PAN/PANet, P3/P4/P5 and Multi-Scale Detection
This chapter11. SPP and SPPF: Expanding Receptive Field Cheaply
Next12. CSPNet: Cross Stage Partial Networks and Gradient Flow

Spatial Pyramid Pooling (SPP) gives a model access to context at several pooling scales.

A simplified block:

                  +--> MaxPool 5x5 --+
input --> reduce -+--> MaxPool 9x9 --+--> concat --> fuse
                  +--> MaxPool13x13 --+
                  +-------------------+

The output contains information from progressively larger neighborhoods.

This helps the network reason about context without stacking many more expensive convolutions.

11.1 SPPF: Spatial Pyramid Pooling Fast

Ultralytics uses SPPF in several generations.

Instead of running three independent large pooling kernels, repeatedly apply a 5×5 max pool:

x
|
+------------------------------+
|
5x5 pool -> y1
|          |
|          +-------------------+
5x5 pool -> y2
|          |
|          +-------------------+
5x5 pool -> y3
|
concat[x, y1, y2, y3]
|
1x1 fuse

Repeated 5×5 pooling produces effective receptive fields corresponding roughly to larger pooling windows while reusing computation.

Current Ultralytics architecture documentation describes SPPF as mathematically corresponding to the older (5, 9, 13) SPP receptive fields but with a cheaper sequential implementation.

YOLO26 additionally uses a residual shortcut around SPPF in its current architecture.

12. CSPNet: Cross Stage Partial Networks and Gradient Flow

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous11. SPP and SPPF: Expanding Receptive Field Cheaply
This chapter12. CSPNet: Cross Stage Partial Networks and Gradient Flow
Next13. C3, C2f, C3k, and C3k2: The Ultralytics Backbone Evolution

CSPNet means Cross Stage Partial Network.

Primary source: CSPNet: A New Backbone that can Enhance Learning Capability of CNN.

The paper’s central motivation was not merely “split the channels to save compute.”

It explicitly discusses duplicate gradient information and how network stages can be reorganized to improve computational efficiency while maintaining or improving learning capability.

12.1 A normal stage

Imagine a deep block:

x
|
Block 1
|
Block 2
|
Block 3
|
Block 4
|
y

Every channel goes through every expensive block.

Gradient information follows heavily overlapping paths through the same transformations.

12.2 CSP idea: split the flow

CSP-style design splits feature flow:

                  +--> heavy transformation path --+
x --> split ------+                                +--> concat/fuse
                  +--> partial/bypass path --------+

One portion receives deeper processing.

Another portion takes a shorter path.

They are fused at the end of the stage.

12.3 Why this can help

Three related benefits:

1. Less duplicate computation

Not every channel must pass through every expensive block.

2. More diverse gradient paths

The two routes expose the fused output to different transformation histories.

3. Better efficiency/accuracy tradeoff

The CSPNet paper reports reduced computation with comparable or improved performance in its evaluated settings.

This idea became highly influential in real-time detection.

12.4 CSP is a design pattern, not one fixed block

This is important.

“CSP” does not mean one exact module.

It is a strategy:

split
process part
preserve another path
merge later

Therefore:

  • CSPDarknet;
  • C3;
  • C2f;
  • C3k2;
  • C2PSA

can all embody partial/split-path ideas while having different internal processing.

13. C3, C2f, C3k, and C3k2: The Ultralytics Backbone Evolution

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous12. CSPNet: Cross Stage Partial Networks and Gradient Flow
This chapter13. C3, C2f, C3k, and C3k2: The Ultralytics Backbone Evolution
Next14. C2PSA: Position-Sensitive Attention Inside YOLO11 and YOLO26

Current Ultralytics documentation gives a particularly useful lineage:

YOLOv3: Bottleneck
   |
YOLOv5: C3
   |
YOLOv8: C2f
   |
YOLO11/YOLO26: C3k2

Reference: Ultralytics YOLO Architecture Explained.

The names look arbitrary until we expand them.

13.1 C3

A conceptual C3 block:

                     +--> Conv --> Bottleneck x N --+
input --> split-ish -+                              +--> concat --> Conv
                     +--> Conv ---------------------+

In the implementation described by Ultralytics:

  • one branch goes through repeated bottlenecks;
  • one branch bypasses those bottlenecks;
  • both branches are concatenated;
  • a final convolution fuses them.

If hidden width is c, the final fusion sees roughly two c-channel paths.

This follows the CSP principle.

13.2 C2f

YOLOv8 uses C2f.

The key change is richer reuse of internal outputs.

Conceptually:

input
  |
Conv -> split -> a, b
               |   |
               |   +--> Bottleneck1 -> c
               |                     |
               |                     +--> Bottleneck2 -> d
               |                                         |
               +-----------------------------------------+
                                                         |
concat [a, b, c, d, ...]
            |
          Conv
            |
          output

Rather than only fusing the start and final transformed branches, C2f concatenates multiple intermediate bottleneck outputs.

This gives the fusion layer access to features at multiple transformation depths.

According to current Ultralytics docs, if there are n bottlenecks, C2f sends n+2 feature tensors into the fusion convolution.

13.3 Why “faster”?

The exact efficiency benefit depends on implementation, tensor widths, memory movement, and hardware.

The conceptual intention is to preserve strong gradient/feature reuse while simplifying the CSP bottleneck structure and maintaining favorable speed.

Never infer performance from a module’s name alone. Benchmark on the deployment hardware.

13.4 C3k

C3k is a C3-style variant with configurable convolution kernel sizes.

Why care about kernel size?

A larger kernel has a larger direct spatial receptive field.

But larger kernels also cost more.

Modern architecture design therefore tries to place kernel capacity selectively rather than everywhere.

13.5 C3k2

YOLO11 and YOLO26 use C3k2.

Current Ultralytics documentation describes C3k2 as a subclass/variant of C2f whose internal repeating unit can switch between:

  • ordinary Bottleneck;
  • C3k block;
  • in some configurations attention-enhanced internals.

At the outer level the pattern still resembles:

project/split
    |
multiple internal transformations
    |
concatenate intermediate paths
    |
fuse

The significance is not the name itself.

The significance is that YOLO architecture evolution increasingly optimizes:

  • gradient paths;
  • channel reuse;
  • kernel placement;
  • compute distribution.

13.6 The naming trap

Do not memorize:

C3 good, C2f newer, C3k2 newest.

Instead ask:

  1. How is the input split?
  2. Which branch is transformed?
  3. Which intermediate outputs are preserved?
  4. Where are channels concatenated?
  5. What kernel sizes are used?
  6. Are residual shortcuts active?
  7. What is the hidden expansion ratio?
  8. Where is the block placed spatially?

Those questions transfer to almost every CNN architecture.

14. C2PSA: Position-Sensitive Attention Inside YOLO11 and YOLO26

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous13. C3, C2f, C3k, and C3k2: The Ultralytics Backbone Evolution
This chapter14. C2PSA: Position-Sensitive Attention Inside YOLO11 and YOLO26
Next15. Anchor-Based vs Anchor-Free Detection

The term you wrote as C2SPA is, in current Ultralytics naming, C2PSA.

Current architecture reference: Ultralytics YOLO Architecture Guide.

C2PSA is a CSP-like module whose active branch passes through PSABlock modules.

A conceptual structure:

input
  |
1x1 Conv
  |
split ---------------------------+
  |                               |
  |                            bypass
  v                               |
PSABlock x N                       |
  |                               |
  +--------------- concat --------+
                  |
                1x1 Conv
                  |
                output

14.1 What PSABlock contains

Current Ultralytics documentation describes PSABlock as containing:

  1. multi-head attention;
  2. a feed-forward network;
  3. residual connections around those subcomponents.

Conceptually:

x
|\
| attention
|   |
+---+ -> x1
        |\
        | FFN
        |  |
        +--+ -> y

This is recognizably transformer-like.

The FFN itself is implemented using convolutional projections in the YOLO module context.

14.2 Why add attention to a CNN detector?

Convolution has strong local inductive bias.

Attention allows a feature at one spatial position to dynamically interact with distant positions.

Consider a face partly occluded by a phone.

Local convolution may see:

skin texture + phone edge

Attention can help associate distant contextual structure:

left eye
right eye
face contour
body position

depending on what representations the model learns.

This does not mean attention automatically makes detection better. It adds modeling capacity at computational cost.

The architectural challenge is to place attention where the spatial map is small enough that cost remains reasonable.

That is one reason attention often appears deeper in a detector rather than at the full input resolution.

14.3 Why CSP-wrap the attention

Instead of sending the entire tensor through attention:

all channels -> expensive attention

a split-path design can roughly do:

some representation -> attention
some representation -> bypass
then fuse

This retains a direct convolutional/identity information route and reduces expensive processing.

The same design philosophy appears repeatedly:

use expensive global reasoning selectively, preserve efficient local paths elsewhere.

14.4 C2PSA is not “the transformer inside YOLO”

That would be an oversimplification.

YOLO11/26 remain heavily convolutional architectures with:

  • strided convolutions;
  • CSP-like blocks;
  • SPPF;
  • feature-pyramid fusion;
  • convolutional detection heads.

C2PSA injects an attention module into that ecosystem.

A better description:

YOLO11/26 are CNN-dominant real-time detectors augmented with strategically placed self-attention.

That hybridization is becoming increasingly common in computer vision.

Connection Bridge — From Backbone Design to Prediction Design

The previous chapters mostly changed how features are built and fused. From this chapter onward, the center of gravity moves to how predictions are represented and trained. Anchor boxes were an effective engineering scaffold: start from predefined shapes and learn offsets. But they introduced hyperparameters, matching rules, duplicated candidates, and a growing amount of machinery around what should conceptually be a simple question: where are the four sides of the object?

Anchor-free detectors simplify that representation, but simplification at inference does not make training trivial. Once anchors disappear, the difficult question becomes which spatial predictions should be treated as responsible for each ground-truth object? That is why decoupled heads, IoU-family losses, label assignment, quality-aware scoring, and eventually end-to-end/NMS-free designs follow naturally.

15. Anchor-Based vs Anchor-Free Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous14. C2PSA: Position-Sensitive Attention Inside YOLO11 and YOLO26
This chapter15. Anchor-Based vs Anchor-Free Detection
Next16. Decoupled Detection Heads: Classification vs Localization

Anchor boxes were an important historical step, but they also introduced engineering complexity.

To understand why modern YOLO models moved toward anchor-free heads, compare the two formulations.

15.1 Anchor-based prediction

Assume one spatial location has three anchors:

anchor 1:  tall
anchor 2:  wide
anchor 3:  square

For each anchor, the model may predict:

objectness
class scores
x/y offset
width/height transform

So one location produces multiple candidate boxes.

The anchor prior acts like:

“Start from this plausible shape, then adjust it.”

This helps optimization, especially in earlier detector designs.

But anchors create questions:

  • How many anchors?
  • What aspect ratios?
  • What sizes?
  • Which scale gets which anchors?
  • Which ground-truth box should assign to which anchor?
  • Do anchor statistics transfer across datasets?

If your training set changes from street scenes to close-up faces, the box-shape distribution changes.

15.2 Anchor-free prediction

Anchor-free detectors remove predefined box templates.

A spatial position or “anchor point” can directly predict geometric distances:

              top
               ^
               |
        +-------------+
 left <-|      o      |-> right
        +-------------+
               |
               v
             bottom

The location o predicts:

(l,t,r,b) (l,t,r,b)

relative to itself.

The box is reconstructed from those four distances.

“Anchor-free” does not mean there is no spatial reference point. It means there is no hand/precomputed anchor rectangle with a predefined width/height template.

15.3 Why anchor-free became attractive

Advantages include:

  • fewer box-shape hyperparameters;
  • simpler transfer to datasets with different aspect distributions;
  • a cleaner geometric formulation;
  • compatibility with modern label-assignment schemes;
  • easier decoupling of box regression and classification.

Current Ultralytics documentation describes YOLOv8, YOLO11, and YOLO26 as anchor-free.

15.4 Anchor point versus object query

Do not confuse YOLO anchor-free points with DETR object queries.

YOLO-style dense detector:

feature map locations
o o o o o o
o o o o o o
o o o o o o
...

Each location can generate predictions.

DETR:

learned query embeddings
q1 q2 q3 ... qN

Queries are learned latent slots that attend globally to image features.

Both can be called “anchor-free” in a broad sense, but their computational representations are very different.

16. Decoupled Detection Heads: Classification vs Localization

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous15. Anchor-Based vs Anchor-Free Detection
This chapter16. Decoupled Detection Heads: Classification vs Localization
Next17. IoU, GIoU, DIoU, CIoU, Box Regression, and Distribution Focal Loss

Object detection contains at least two objectives:

  1. determine what the object is;
  2. determine where it is.

These objectives benefit from different features.

Classification may care about:

eyes
face shape
texture
semantic parts
context

Localization may care intensely about:

left boundary
right boundary
top/bottom edge
geometric extent

A fully shared prediction head forces the same terminal feature representation to service both.

A decoupled head splits them.

fused feature map
        |
   +----+----+
   |         |
   v         v
box branch  class branch
   |         |
 l,t,r,b   class logits

Modern Ultralytics YOLO detectors use decoupled heads.

16.1 Why “decoupled” does not mean unrelated

The branches usually share:

  • the backbone;
  • the neck;
  • the feature map entering the head.

Only later prediction processing diverges.

So the architecture is:

image
 |
shared backbone
 |
shared multi-scale neck
 |
P3/P4/P5 features
 |
+--> regression processing
|
+--> classification processing

They share perception but specialize near the final output.

16.2 The classification/localization alignment problem

There is a subtle issue.

A location can be semantically confident but geometrically poor.

Example:

Prediction A:
class confidence = 0.99
IoU with object   = 0.50

Prediction B:
class confidence = 0.87
IoU with object   = 0.92

If ranking uses only class confidence, A may be ranked above B even though its geometry is worse.

Research such as TOOD (Task-Aligned One-stage Object Detection) explicitly studies this mismatch between classification and localization and proposes task-alignment strategies.

Primary source: TOOD paper.

This is one reason modern detector training and assignment logic becomes more sophisticated than ordinary classification.

17. IoU, GIoU, DIoU, CIoU, Box Regression, and Distribution Focal Loss

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous16. Decoupled Detection Heads: Classification vs Localization
This chapter17. IoU, GIoU, DIoU, CIoU, Box Regression, and Distribution Focal Loss
Next18. Label Assignment: Why Training a Detector Is More Than Drawing Boxes

A detector must train box coordinates.

The naive approach is ordinary coordinate regression:

L=|x−x̂|+|y−ŷ|+|w−ŵ|+|h−ĥ| L = |x-\hat{x}| + |y-\hat{y}| + |w-\hat{w}| + |h-\hat{h}|

But geometric overlap is what actually matters.

IoU-derived losses therefore became common.

17.1 IoU loss

IoU(Bp,Bg)=|Bp∩Bg||Bp∪Bg| IoU(B_p,B_g)=\frac{|B_p\cap B_g|}{|B_p\cup B_g|}

One can optimize something like:

LIoU=1−IoU L_{IoU}=1-IoU

Problem:

if boxes do not overlap, IoU is zero, and ordinary IoU gives weak information about how the prediction should move toward the target.

17.2 Generalized IoU

GIoU adds information from the smallest enclosing box.

Intuitively it penalizes how much empty enclosing area exists beyond the union.

This provides a signal even when boxes do not overlap.

17.3 Distance IoU

DIoU additionally considers distance between box centers.

A simplified intuition:

good:
pred center nearly equals GT center

bad:
same overlap quality,
but centers far apart

This makes optimization more geometrically informative.

17.4 Complete IoU

CIoU extends the idea to include:

  • overlap;
  • center distance;
  • aspect-ratio consistency.

Its purpose is to better reflect what a “good box” means.

Different YOLO generations use different combinations of IoU-style losses and regression strategies.

Do not treat “YOLO loss” as one timeless equation.

17.5 Distribution Focal Loss: predict a distribution rather than one scalar

YOLOv8 and YOLO11 use DFL-style box regression in current Ultralytics implementations.

The idea is related to Generalized Focal Loss research, which represents box boundary values as distributions over discrete bins rather than forcing a direct single scalar.

Primary source: Generalized Focal Loss.

Suppose a boundary distance is around 6.4.

Instead of directly outputting:

6.4

the model can represent probabilities over bins:

bin 0: 0.00
bin 1: 0.00
...
bin 6: 0.60
bin 7: 0.40
...

Then expectation gives:

6(0.60)+7(0.40)=6.4 6(0.60)+7(0.40)=6.4

The distribution carries a richer learning signal than a single unconstrained scalar.

17.6 Why a distribution can help

Localization contains ambiguity.

A boundary can fall between discrete spatial positions.

Learning neighboring probabilities can provide smoother optimization.

In Ultralytics YOLOv8/YOLO11, reg_max=16 is associated with this discretized distance representation.

17.7 Why YOLO26 removes DFL

Current YOLO26 documentation says its detection head is DFL-free, using reg_max=1 in the current architecture.

Reference: Ultralytics YOLO26.

This simplifies the head:

  • fewer box-regression outputs;
  • less decoding complexity;
  • simpler export/inference.

The important engineering lesson is not that DFL was “wrong.”

It is:

An architectural technique is valuable only while its accuracy benefit justifies its runtime/training/deployment complexity.

YOLO26’s authors report that with their changed training recipe and architecture, they can remove DFL while preserving a favorable accuracy/latency tradeoff.

18. Label Assignment: Why Training a Detector Is More Than Drawing Boxes

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous17. IoU, GIoU, DIoU, CIoU, Box Regression, and Distribution Focal Loss
This chapter18. Label Assignment: Why Training a Detector Is More Than Drawing Boxes
Next19. NMS and the Move Toward End-to-End NMS-Free Detection

Assume one image contains one face.

The detector might produce thousands of candidate predictions across P3/P4/P5.

Which predictions should be trained as positive?

This is the label assignment problem.

It is easy to underestimate how important this is.

18.1 Why every nearby prediction cannot simply be positive

Suppose a face overlaps 30 candidate points.

If all 30 are trained equally as positives:

  • the model may emit many duplicate boxes;
  • classification and localization targets become inconsistent;
  • optimization wastes capacity.

If only one arbitrary point is positive:

  • supervision may be too sparse;
  • training becomes unstable;
  • small objects can receive too little positive coverage.

Assignment algorithms determine who is responsible.

18.2 Historical fixed assignment

Older anchor systems used rules based on:

  • IoU with anchors;
  • object center location;
  • anchor-shape compatibility.

These were relatively static.

18.3 Dynamic assignment

Modern detectors often use prediction quality during training.

A candidate may be good when:

  • class score is high;
  • geometry overlaps target well;
  • it is spatially close to the object;
  • a combined alignment metric is high.

This makes assignment itself adaptive.

18.4 Task-Aligned Assignment

TOOD introduced task alignment ideas that strongly influenced modern detection ecosystems.

A simplified alignment score can combine classification and localization:

t=sαuβ t = s^\alpha u^\beta

where:

  • ss is classification confidence;
  • uu is localization quality such as IoU;
  • α,β\alpha,\beta control emphasis.

The exact implementation varies.

The principle is:

positive assignments should reward predictions that are jointly good at what and where.

18.5 Small objects are difficult to assign

Small faces are especially challenging.

If an object spans very few feature-map locations:

big object -> many candidate positive points
small face -> maybe very few

Small errors in center position or downsampling can dramatically change which points are valid.

This motivates special label-assignment techniques for small objects.

YOLO26 introduces STAL — Small-Target-Aware Label Assignment according to current Ultralytics documentation.

Its purpose is to improve positive label coverage for small targets.

That can be relevant for biometric systems where the face may be distant in the initial frame.

19. NMS and the Move Toward End-to-End NMS-Free Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous18. Label Assignment: Why Training a Detector Is More Than Drawing Boxes
This chapter19. NMS and the Move Toward End-to-End NMS-Free Detection
Next20. YOLOv4, YOLOv5, YOLOv6, YOLOv7, YOLOv8, YOLOv9, YOLOv10, YOLO11, YOLO12

Dense detectors naturally produce duplicates.

Suppose four nearby locations all predict the same face:

box A score .98
box B score .94
box C score .91
box D score .73

They overlap heavily.

Traditional inference applies Non-Maximum Suppression (NMS).

19.1 Basic greedy NMS

  1. Sort predictions by score.
  2. Select the highest-score box.
  3. Remove lower-score boxes whose IoU with it exceeds a threshold.
  4. Repeat.

Example:

A score .98 -> keep
B IoU(A,B)=.88 -> suppress
C IoU(A,C)=.82 -> suppress
D IoU(A,D)=.21 -> maybe keep

NMS is simple and effective.

19.2 Why NMS is conceptually awkward

The neural network itself does not produce the final set of detections.

Instead:

network prediction
       |
       v
external heuristic algorithm
       |
       v
final prediction

This creates several drawbacks:

  • extra latency;
  • deployment complexity;
  • threshold tuning;
  • potentially awkward accelerator/export support;
  • training/inference mismatch.

DETR’s set-prediction philosophy attacked this from a different direction.

More recent YOLO research also increasingly targets NMS-free output.

19.3 One-to-many versus one-to-one assignment

Dense YOLO training traditionally benefits from one-to-many supervision:

one GT object
   |
   +--> positive prediction A
   +--> positive prediction B
   +--> positive prediction C
   +--> positive prediction D

This gives abundant training signal.

But for end-to-end NMS-free inference you want something closer to:

one GT object
   |
   +--> exactly one final prediction

This is one-to-one assignment.

The tension:

  • one-to-many is often easier/stronger for training;
  • one-to-one creates duplicate-free inference.

Modern end-to-end YOLO designs try to get benefits of both.

19.4 YOLOv10 consistent dual assignments

YOLOv10 explicitly proposed consistent dual assignments for NMS-free end-to-end YOLO detection.

Primary source: YOLOv10: Real-Time End-to-End Object Detection.

Conceptually, training includes:

shared features
    |
    +--> one-to-many supervision
    |
    +--> one-to-one supervision

The one-to-many branch supports rich supervision.

The one-to-one branch learns the final duplicate-free output behavior.

This idea is highly relevant for understanding YOLO26’s dual-head design, though the specific implementation/training recipe is its own.

Connection Bridge — Stop Thinking of “YOLO” as One Continuous Architecture

From this point, “YOLO” is better treated as a design tradition than a single clean lineage. Different teams adopt the name while borrowing from the same ecosystem of ideas: CSP-style gradient paths, FPN/PAN multi-scale fusion, anchor-free prediction, decoupled heads, improved assignment, re-parameterization, attention, and end-to-end objectives. Version numbers alone therefore tell you less than the internal modules and training strategy.

When reading YOLO11 or YOLO26, ask four questions instead of memorizing the name: What backbone builds features? How does the neck fuse scales? What head representation produces predictions? What training objective and assignment strategy teach that head? Those four questions generalize to almost every detector you will encounter.

20. YOLOv4, YOLOv5, YOLOv6, YOLOv7, YOLOv8, YOLOv9, YOLOv10, YOLO11, YOLO12

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous19. NMS and the Move Toward End-to-End NMS-Free Detection
This chapter20. YOLOv4, YOLOv5, YOLOv6, YOLOv7, YOLOv8, YOLOv9, YOLOv10, YOLO11, YOLO12
Next21. YOLO26 in Detail

This section is not meant to imply a single linear genealogy. It is a map of important ideas.

20.1 YOLOv4: CSPDarknet53 + SPP + PAN

YOLOv4 was described in 2020 by Bochkovskiy, Wang, and Liao.

Primary source: YOLOv4: Optimal Speed and Accuracy of Object Detection.

A frequently cited high-level architecture is:

Backbone: CSPDarknet53
Neck:     SPP + PAN
Head:     YOLOv3-style detection

The paper also systematized:

  • Bag of Freebies — training techniques that improve accuracy without necessarily increasing inference cost;
  • Bag of Specials — architectural/post-processing additions with some inference cost.

Examples of ideas associated with YOLOv4 include:

  • Mosaic augmentation;
  • CIoU loss;
  • CSP connections;
  • SPP;
  • PAN;
  • Mish activation in the original design;
  • self-adversarial training and various regularization/augmentation choices.

20.2 What “Bag of Freebies” means

Suppose you improve training with data augmentation.

The deployed graph does not get bigger.

That is “free” at inference.

Examples:

mosaic augmentation
mixup
label smoothing
better optimizer schedule

This distinction is valuable in production:

training cost and inference cost are different optimization budgets.

20.3 YOLOv5: Ultralytics engineering lineage

YOLOv5 did not originate as a conventional peer-reviewed YOLO paper in the same way as v1-v4.

It became extremely influential as an engineering implementation.

Historically important components include:

  • CSP/C3-style backbone blocks;
  • PAN/FPN-style neck;
  • SPPF;
  • anchor-based head in original versions;
  • extensive deployment/export tooling.

In current Ultralytics architecture documentation, YOLOv5 is the bridge between YOLOv3’s bottleneck backbone and newer C2f/C3k2 blocks.

20.4 YOLOv6: deployment-oriented reparameterization

YOLOv6, from the Meituan research ecosystem, emphasizes industrial real-time detection and hardware efficiency.

Important ideas include:

  • EfficientRep-style backbone;
  • Rep-PAN style neck;
  • decoupled detection heads;
  • reparameterization ideas inspired by RepVGG.

Structural reparameterization means training with a richer multi-branch block and algebraically fusing branches into a simpler inference block.

Conceptually:

TRAIN:
3x3 conv branch
1x1 conv branch
identity branch
      |
      v
sum

DEPLOY:
equivalent single 3x3 conv

This is a powerful general design pattern:

make training expressive, make inference simple.

20.5 YOLOv7: E-ELAN and gradient-path design

YOLOv7 was introduced by Wang, Bochkovskiy, and Liao.

Primary source: YOLOv7 paper.

A major architectural idea is E-ELAN (Extended Efficient Layer Aggregation Network).

The authors focus strongly on gradient-path planning and how architecture topology affects learnability as networks scale.

A simplified mental model:

input
 | \
 |  \ multiple transformation groups
 |   \
 +---- aggregate

The exact graph is more complex than CSP, but the philosophical connection is clear:

how information and gradients travel matters as much as nominal layer count.

YOLOv7 also explored:

  • compound model scaling;
  • trainable bag-of-freebies;
  • planned reparameterized convolution.

20.6 YOLOv8: C2f + anchor-free + DFL

YOLOv8 marks a clean modern Ultralytics architecture.

High-level:

backbone:
Conv + C2f stages + SPPF

neck:
FPN/PAN style + C2f

head:
anchor-free
decoupled
DFL box regression

The most important conceptual transitions are:

  1. C3 -> C2f for richer intermediate feature reuse;
  2. anchor-based -> anchor-free prediction;
  3. decoupled head;
  4. DFL-style distributional box regression.

For learning modern YOLO internals, YOLOv8 is an excellent reference point because YOLO11 and YOLO26 are easier to understand as modifications of this general pattern.

20.7 YOLOv9: GELAN + PGI

YOLOv9 introduced two major ideas:

  • GELAN — Generalized Efficient Layer Aggregation Network;
  • PGI — Programmable Gradient Information.

Primary source: YOLOv9: Learning What You Want to Learn Using Programmable Gradient Information.

The paper starts from an information-flow argument:

deep transformations lose information.

During training, the gradient may therefore be based on degraded intermediate representations.

PGI introduces auxiliary/reversible-style paths intended to provide more reliable gradient information during optimization without necessarily burdening deployed inference in the same way.

The concept is worth remembering:

training architecture can contain information paths that exist primarily to improve gradient quality.

This resembles the broader pattern we saw with reparameterization and dual-head training: training-time graph design and inference-time graph design do not have to be identical.

20.8 GELAN

GELAN combines efficient layer aggregation with architecture generality.

It is heavily concerned with:

  • parameter utilization;
  • gradient flow;
  • scalable composition.

Do not reduce GELAN to a specific sequence of Conv calls. Its research contribution is the network-topology strategy.

20.9 YOLOv10: end-to-end detection

YOLOv10’s headline innovation is NMS-free end-to-end detection through consistent dual assignments.

It also performs holistic efficiency-driven architectural redesign.

Key concepts:

one-to-many head -> strong training supervision
one-to-one head  -> duplicate-free inference target

This pushes YOLO toward a property historically associated with DETR:

the model itself produces the final detection set.

20.10 YOLO11: C3k2 + C2PSA

Current Ultralytics architecture guide identifies three major changes relative to YOLOv8:

  1. replace C2f with C3k2;
  2. add C2PSA after SPPF;
  3. lighten classification branches using depthwise-separable convolutions.

The detection head remains:

  • anchor-free;
  • decoupled;
  • DFL-based (reg_max=16).

So YOLO11 is not a complete paradigm change.

It is a careful refinement of:

CNN multi-scale detector
+
better CSP-like block
+
selective attention
+
head efficiency

20.11 YOLO12: attention-centric YOLO research

YOLOv12 was proposed as an attention-centric real-time detector.

Primary source: YOLOv12: Attention-Centric Real-Time Object Detectors.

Its motivating question is:

Can attention become the dominant modeling mechanism without destroying the latency advantage associated with YOLO?

This is important because the field is converging:

  • CNN detectors borrow attention;
  • transformer detectors borrow multi-scale sparse/local operations;
  • deployment architectures increasingly become hybrids.
