YOLO12 is not simply “YOLO11 plus more attention”; it comes from a separate research effort and should be studied on its own terms.

20.12 YOLO naming is not chronological superiority

Never assume:

YOLO12 > YOLO11 > YOLO10 > YOLO9

for every task.

Different models optimize:

  • GPU latency;
  • CPU latency;
  • edge accelerators;
  • memory;
  • parameter count;
  • small-object AP;
  • training cost;
  • export simplicity;
  • licensing ecosystem;
  • supported tasks.

For biometric payment, a small specialized face detector may outperform a huge general detector on exactly the operational metric you care about.

21. YOLO26 in Detail

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous20. YOLOv4, YOLOv5, YOLOv6, YOLOv7, YOLOv8, YOLOv9, YOLOv10, YOLO11, YOLO12
This chapter21. YOLO26 in Detail
Next22. Face Detection Is a Different Problem from Generic Object Detection

As of the date of this document, YOLO26 is the current Ultralytics family released in 2026.

Primary official sources:

  • Ultralytics YOLO26 documentation
  • Ultralytics architecture guide
  • Paper referenced by Ultralytics: Ultralytics YOLO26: Unified Real-Time End-to-End Vision Models, arXiv:2606.03748.

The architecture is easiest to understand as:

                 IMAGE
                   |
                   v
             BACKBONE
  Conv -> C3k2 -> Conv -> C3k2 ...
                   |
                  SPPF
             + residual shortcut
                   |
                 C2PSA
                   |
                   v
                  NECK
          top-down FPN path
                   |
         bottom-up PAN path
                   |
         P3       P4       P5
          \        |        /
           \       |       /
            v      v      v
               DETECT
        /                    \
one-to-many head        one-to-one head
      |                       |
 NMS default              NMS-free

21.1 Backbone

The current Ultralytics architecture guide says YOLO26 retains:

  • C3k2;
  • C2PSA;

from YOLO11-style backbone design.

So the core representation engine remains hybrid convolution + selective attention.

A simplified backbone shape flow for 640 input:

Input 3 x 640 x 640
   |
Conv stride 2
   v
P1-ish: 320 x 320
   |
Conv stride 2
   v
P2-ish: 160 x 160
   |
C3k2
   |
Conv stride 2
   v
P3: 80 x 80
   |
C3k2
   |
Conv stride 2
   v
P4: 40 x 40
   |
C3k2
   |
Conv stride 2
   v
P5: 20 x 20
   |
C3k2
   |
SPPF
   |
C2PSA

Channel counts depend on model scale (n, s, m, l, x).

21.2 Compound scaling

Ultralytics models use scale configurations that adjust:

  • depth;
  • width;
  • maximum channels.

Conceptually:

n = nano
s = small
m = medium
l = large
x = extra-large

A shared architecture template can therefore instantiate different compute budgets.

Depth multiplier changes how many repeated blocks survive scaling.

Width multiplier changes channel counts.

This is far more maintainable than manually designing five independent networks.

21.3 SPPF residual shortcut

YOLO26 changes SPPF by enabling a residual connection in the deepest representation when shapes allow it.

Instead of:

x -> SPPF -> y

think:

x ----------------+
|                 |
v                 |
SPPF              |
|                 |
+------ add ------+
        |
        v
        y

This preserves a direct information/gradient path around the pooling transformation.

The general theme again:

add expressive transformation without forcing every signal through it.

21.4 C2PSA

After spatial pooling, deep features pass through C2PSA.

Why place attention there?

At P5-like deep resolution, spatial dimensions are relatively small.

For 640 input:

20 x 20 = 400 positions

Attention over 400 positions is much cheaper than attention over:

640 x 640 = 409,600 positions

So deep placement creates global interaction at manageable cost.

21.5 Neck: FPN + PAN

YOLO26 retains multi-scale feature fusion.

Top-down:

deep P5 semantics
    |
upsample
    |
concat P4
    |
process
    |
upsample
    |
concat P3

Bottom-up:

fused P3
   |
stride-2
   |
concat fused P4
   |
process
   |
stride-2
   |
concat P5

Result:

P3 -> small objects
P4 -> medium objects
P5 -> large objects

These all feed detection.

21.6 DFL-free regression

YOLO26 removes Distribution Focal Loss from the head.

Current docs explicitly describe:

reg_max = 1

for the architecture-level comparison.

Why care operationally?

An embedded payment terminal may have:

  • constrained CPU/NPU;
  • strict latency budget;
  • strict export format;
  • deterministic deployment requirements.

A simpler regression head can improve deployment even if training becomes more sophisticated elsewhere.

21.7 Dual-head architecture

This is one of YOLO26’s most important ideas.

Current Ultralytics docs describe:

One-to-many head

Default path.

Produces dense traditional-style outputs and uses NMS.

The docs describe an output form approximately:

(N, nc + 4, 8400)

for a common 640-style configuration, where nc is number of classes.

This branch typically prioritizes accuracy.

One-to-one head

Activated for end-to-end NMS-free inference (nms=False in current APIs).

The docs describe up to 300 final detections:

(N, 300, 6)

The one-to-one head is trained to emit a final sparse set without an external NMS stage.

21.8 Why keep both?

Because the goals conflict.

One-to-many supervision gives many positives per target:

ground truth
 |  |  |  |
 p1 p2 p3 p4

This is optimization-friendly.

One-to-one inference wants:

ground truth
 |
 p1

for a clean final set.

Dual-head training lets the architecture support both modes.

21.9 Progressive Loss

Current YOLO26 documentation describes Progressive Loss as shifting training emphasis toward the inference-time head.

Conceptually imagine training progress tt:

early training:
more help from dense one-to-many objective

later training:
increasing emphasis on final one-to-one behavior

The exact implementation should be read from the paper/code for reproduction, but the conceptual reason is intuitive:

  • early optimization benefits from abundant supervision;
  • final deployment behavior needs the sparse end-to-end head to become strong.

21.10 STAL: Small-Target-Aware Label Assignment

Small objects have sparse spatial support.

YOLO26 introduces STAL to maintain positive assignment coverage for small targets.

For a biometric terminal, imagine:

customer approaches camera

frame 1: face = 18 pixels high
frame 2: face = 35 pixels
frame 3: face = 80 pixels
frame 4: face = 160 pixels

If the system tracks a person before close-range verification, small-face detection quality can matter.

However, do not assume generic YOLO26 face detection is automatically preferable to SCRFD/RetinaFace. Specialized face detectors have training objectives and landmark outputs designed for this exact domain.

21.11 MuSGD

YOLO26’s training recipe uses MuSGD, described by Ultralytics as a hybrid combining SGD with Muon-style optimization.

The current training guide says Muon-inspired updates are applied to matrix-like weights—including reshaped convolutional filters—while retaining SGD behavior where appropriate.

This matters mainly for training dynamics, not inference architecture.

Once weights are trained, the optimizer is gone.

This distinction is crucial:

optimizer -> exists during training only
model graph -> exists during inference

Do not put “MuSGD” in an inference architecture diagram as if requests flow through it.

21.12 Objects365 pretraining -> COCO fine-tuning

Current YOLO26 training documentation says released base models use:

  1. Objects365 v1 pretraining;
  2. COCO fine-tuning;
  3. 640×640 training resolution in the documented recipe.

This is a reminder that architecture alone does not create model quality.

Performance is a function of:

architecture+data+objective+assignment+augmentation+optimizer+training schedule \text{architecture} + \text{data} + \text{objective} + \text{assignment} + \text{augmentation} + \text{optimizer} + \text{training schedule}

A smaller architecture trained on better-matched face data may beat a larger general model on biometric-terminal capture.

21.13 YOLO26 supports more than boxes

Current Ultralytics family supports task-specific heads for:

  • object detection;
  • instance segmentation;
  • semantic segmentation;
  • depth estimation;
  • classification;
  • pose/keypoints;
  • oriented bounding boxes.

That does not mean one checkpoint magically outputs every task at once.

Different task variants use related architecture families with appropriate heads/training.

21.14 P2 and P6 variants

Current YOLO26 documentation also mentions architecture-only P2/P6 variants.

Why does P2 matter?

Normal P3 minimum stride:

stride 8

P2:

stride 4

gives a higher-resolution detection feature map.

That can help very small objects at extra compute/memory cost.

For distant-face detection, this is conceptually relevant.

But again: a specialized face detector may still be a better system choice.

21.15 YOLO26 architecture in expanded form

Here is the conceptual graph with each named component translated:

RGB IMAGE
   |
   v
[stride-2 convolution]
   |
   v
[stride-2 convolution]
   |
   v
[C3k2]
   |   split feature paths
   |   repeated bottleneck/C3k transforms
   |   preserve intermediate features
   |   concatenate
   |   fuse
   v
[stride-2 convolution] --------------------> P3 source
   |
   v
[C3k2]
   |
[stride-2 convolution] --------------------> P4 source
   |
   v
[C3k2]
   |
[stride-2 convolution] --------------------> P5 source
   |
   v
[C3k2]
   |
   v
[SPPF + residual]
   |   repeated max-pooling for receptive field
   |   concatenation
   |   fuse
   v
[C2PSA]
   |   split path
   |   multi-head attention
   |   FFN
   |   residuals
   |   concat/fuse
   v
deep semantic P5 feature
   |
   +--> upsample -> concat P4 -> C3k2
   |                     |
   |                     +--> upsample -> concat P3 -> C3k2 ---> P3 fused
   |                                                |
   |                                                v
   |                                           stride-2
   |                                                |
   |                                      concat P4 fused
   |                                                |
   |                                              C3k2 ---> P4 fused
   |                                                |
   |                                           stride-2
   |                                                |
   +-------------------------------------- concat deep P5
                                                    |
                                                  C3k2 ---> P5 fused

P3 fused ----+
P4 fused ----+--> detection heads
P5 fused ----+

training:
   one-to-many + one-to-one supervision

inference:
   default dense head + NMS
          OR
   one-to-one NMS-free head

If you understand every arrow in that graph, the phrase “YOLO26 architecture” is no longer magical.

21.16 What YOLO26 would do in our biometric system

Possible roles:

Role A — person detector

frame -> people boxes

Useful for:

  • deciding which person is at the payment zone;
  • queue/scene awareness;
  • detecting multiple people;
  • privacy masking;
  • tracking candidates.

Role B — custom face detector

Train YOLO26 on face boxes.

Possible, but compare it against specialized face detectors.

Role C — anti-spoof object cue detector

Potential custom classes:

phone screen
printed photo
paper boundary
mask
camera display

Useful only as one signal. Attackers can bypass object-level heuristics.

Role D — pose/keypoints

Could help reason about:

  • orientation;
  • body/face approach;
  • active liveness gestures.

Role E — segmentation/depth variants

May provide environmental cues.

But a biometric-payment architecture should avoid using a giant general model just because it can technically perform many tasks.

Production engineering asks:

What is the cheapest, most reliable component for this exact decision?

Connection Bridge — Detection Ends Where Biometrics Begins

A generic detector can draw a rectangle around a face, but a payment system cannot stop at face detected. The next task is to construct an identity representation that remains stable despite pose, illumination, expression, camera changes, and time. That requires tighter localization, facial landmarks, alignment, and a recognition backbone trained for identity geometry rather than ordinary object classes.

This is why RetinaFace and SCRFD appear after YOLO rather than as mere competing YOLO variants. They solve a more specialized input-conditioning problem for the recognition stack. Their landmark predictions allow the face to be normalized before ResNet/IResNet-style networks convert it into an embedding. Detection supplies the crop; alignment standardizes the coordinate system; recognition supplies the identity geometry.

22. Face Detection Is a Different Problem from Generic Object Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous21. YOLO26 in Detail
This chapter22. Face Detection Is a Different Problem from Generic Object Detection
Next23. RetinaFace: Face Detection + Landmarks + Dense Supervision

A general object detector and a face detector solve related localization problems, but a production biometric system usually benefits from treating face detection as a specialized task.

A generic detector might output:

person
car
phone
chair
dog
...

A face detector can devote its entire capacity to the manifold:

human face

including extremely difficult variations:

  • tiny faces;
  • side profiles;
  • partial occlusion;
  • extreme lighting;
  • masks/glasses;
  • motion blur;
  • crowded scenes;
  • varied skin tones;
  • varied camera focal lengths.

More importantly, face-detection pipelines often output landmarks required by recognition.

bbox
left eye
right eye
nose
left mouth corner
right mouth corner

Those landmarks support geometric normalization.

22.1 Why not simply crop the YOLO box?

Suppose the detector returns two images of the same person:

image A: head upright
image B: head tilted 23 degrees

A recognition network can learn some rotation invariance.

But forcing the network to internally solve every possible nuisance transform wastes capacity and increases intra-class variation.

Instead:

detect face
   |
detect landmarks
   |
estimate geometric transform
   |
warp/crop to canonical pose
   |
recognize

The recognizer receives a more standardized input distribution.

This is one reason landmark-aware detectors remain useful even when generic object detection is excellent.

22.2 Detection confidence is not biometric quality

A face can be trivially detectable but useless for recognition.

Example:

face detection confidence: 0.999
face crop: 23 x 23 pixels
heavy motion blur
eyes closed
extreme side pose

The detector is correctly saying:

“there is definitely a face.”

It is not saying:

“this face contains enough identity signal for high-assurance verification.”

A production pipeline should therefore include a separate quality assessment stage.

Potential signals:

  • minimum inter-eye distance;
  • blur;
  • exposure;
  • pose;
  • occlusion;
  • crop truncation;
  • sensor saturation;
  • face size;
  • landmark confidence.

23. RetinaFace: Face Detection + Landmarks + Dense Supervision

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous22. Face Detection Is a Different Problem from Generic Object Detection
This chapter23. RetinaFace: Face Detection + Landmarks + Dense Supervision
Next24. SCRFD: Compute Redistribution for Efficient Face Detection

RetinaFace: Single-stage Dense Face Localisation in the Wild was introduced by Deng et al.

Primary source: RetinaFace paper.

RetinaFace is important because it treats face localization as a multi-task problem rather than “just predict a rectangle.”

The paper combines:

  • face classification;
  • bounding-box regression;
  • five facial landmarks;
  • additional dense face supervision in the research design.

23.1 High-level architecture

Conceptually:

image
  |
backbone
  |
multi-scale feature pyramid
  |
detection heads at multiple scales
  |
  +--> face / background classification
  +--> bounding-box regression
  +--> five landmark regression

This should already look familiar after studying YOLO.

The recurring detector pattern is:

backbone -> pyramid -> heads

The difference lies in task-specific supervision and head design.

23.2 Why multi-scale detection is critical for faces

Faces have an unusually extreme scale distribution.

A surveillance-like frame may contain:

near face: 300 px
far face:   20 px

A detector that only reasons at one feature resolution will struggle.

RetinaFace uses multi-scale feature maps so small and large faces receive appropriate spatial representations.

This is exactly the same underlying problem that motivated FPN.

23.3 Five landmarks

Common five-point layout:

left_eye       right_eye

         nose

left_mouth     right_mouth

These points can be regressed relative to the detection anchor/reference.

The landmarks matter downstream because they let you estimate a similarity transform.

23.4 Multi-task loss

A simplified conceptual RetinaFace objective:

L=Lcls+λ1Lbox+λ2Llandmark+λ3Ldense L = L_{cls} + \lambda_1 L_{box} + \lambda_2 L_{landmark} + \lambda_3 L_{dense}

The exact paper formulation has implementation details, but the key idea is that one backbone is forced to learn representations useful for:

  • face existence;
  • precise face geometry;
  • landmark structure.

The auxiliary tasks regularize and enrich the learned features.

23.5 Why landmarks can improve hard detection

Consider a blurry small region.

A pure binary detector only asks:

face? yes/no

Landmark supervision asks the representation to encode:

where should the eyes be?
where is the nose?
what facial geometry is plausible?

Even if landmarks are imperfect at tiny scales, this structured supervision can encourage stronger face-specific features.

23.6 RetinaFace in a payment pipeline

A reasonable pipeline:

camera
  |
RetinaFace
  |
bbox + landmarks
  |
quality gate
  |
alignment
  |
recognition

The detector is not responsible for identity.

That clean separation is desirable.

24. SCRFD: Compute Redistribution for Efficient Face Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous23. RetinaFace: Face Detection + Landmarks + Dense Supervision
This chapter24. SCRFD: Compute Redistribution for Efficient Face Detection
Next25. Face Alignment: Landmarks, Similarity Transforms, and Why Alignment Matters

SCRFD comes from the paper Sample and Computation Redistribution for Efficient Face Detection.

Primary source: SCRFD paper.

SCRFD is particularly relevant to payment terminals because the design question is not simply:

“Can we detect faces accurately?”

It is:

“Can we allocate limited computation intelligently enough to detect hard faces in real time?”

24.1 The compute allocation problem

Suppose you have a fixed compute budget:

1 billion operations

You can distribute that budget across:

  • early high-resolution stages;
  • deep semantic stages;
  • neck;
  • detection heads.

An architecture copied from general object detection may allocate compute poorly for face-scale statistics.

SCRFD investigates how computation should be distributed for efficient face detection.

24.2 Sample redistribution

Training datasets contain easy and hard examples at different scales.

The effective supervision distribution matters.

A detector may waste optimization on abundant easy faces while under-serving tiny/hard faces.

“Sample redistribution” concerns how training assignments/samples contribute to learning.

24.3 Computation redistribution

The network can redistribute FLOPs among stages.

Why does this matter?

A small face is destroyed early if high-resolution processing is too weak.

Conversely, spending huge computation at full resolution is expensive.

Efficient architecture design is a constrained optimization problem:

maxaccuracysubject toFLOPs/latency/memory budget \max \text{accuracy} \quad \text{subject to} \quad \text{FLOPs/latency/memory budget}

SCRFD explores this specifically for face detection.

24.4 Why SCRFD is attractive for edge deployment

For a payment kiosk, you may want:

  • fast CPU inference;
  • mobile NPU inference;
  • low thermal load;
  • predictable latency;
  • good small-face recall.

A compact specialized face detector can therefore be superior to a large general-purpose model even if the general model has better COCO mAP.

Never optimize a production system against the wrong benchmark.

25. Face Alignment: Landmarks, Similarity Transforms, and Why Alignment Matters

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous24. SCRFD: Compute Redistribution for Efficient Face Detection
This chapter25. Face Alignment: Landmarks, Similarity Transforms, and Why Alignment Matters
Next26. ResNet from First Principles

Face recognition works better when nuisance geometry is normalized.

Suppose the detector returns landmarks:

pi=(xi,yi) p_i = (x_i,y_i)

for:

left eye
right eye
nose
left mouth
right mouth

We define canonical target points:

qi=(ui,vi) q_i = (u_i,v_i)

in the standardized recognition crop.

We seek a transform TT minimizing:

∑i∥T(pi)−qi∥2 \sum_i \|T(p_i)-q_i\|^2

25.1 Similarity transform

A 2D similarity transform includes:

  • rotation;
  • uniform scale;
  • translation.

It preserves shape up to scale/rotation.

Matrix form:

[uv]=s[cosθ−sinθsinθcosθ][xy]+[txty] \begin{bmatrix} u\\v \end{bmatrix} = s \begin{bmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta\\ \sin\theta & \cos\theta \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} x\\y \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} t_x\\t_y \end{bmatrix}

This is often enough for face alignment.

25.2 Why not arbitrary perspective warp?

A stronger geometric transform can force landmarks to align more perfectly, but may distort identity-bearing facial geometry.

Alignment should remove nuisance pose without inventing a new face.

For moderate in-plane rotation, similarity transforms are a sensible choice.

Extreme 3D pose remains difficult because a 2D warp cannot reveal invisible facial surfaces.

25.3 Canonical crop

After alignment:

input face:
     /🙂

aligned:
      🙂

The recognition backbone receives a fixed-size tensor such as:

3 x 112 x 112

depending on implementation.

Consistent framing reduces intra-person variation.

25.4 Alignment failure is a hidden source of recognition failure

Suppose recognition confidence drops.

The problem may not be the recognizer.

It can be:

bad detection
   ->
bad landmark
   ->
bad affine transform
   ->
misaligned crop
   ->
bad embedding

This is why observability must track intermediate stages.

A production biometric pipeline should log non-sensitive quality metrics such as:

  • detector score;
  • landmark confidence;
  • pose estimate;
  • crop size;
  • blur score;
  • PAD score;
  • recognition similarity;

without casually storing raw biometric images.

Connection Bridge — Why the Recognition Stack Returns to Backbone Fundamentals

At first this can feel like a detour: after learning specialized face detectors, why return to ResNet? Because ArcFace is not a magical face-recognition network. It is a training objective layered on top of a feature extractor. If the backbone cannot learn deep, stable representations, the embedding loss has nothing useful to organize.

ResNet therefore connects the generic vision foundations to biometric recognition. Residual connections make deep identity backbones trainable; the final representation is normalized into an embedding space; metric-learning and margin-based losses then reshape that space so same-identity samples cluster and different identities separate. Backbone architecture determines what can be represented; the loss determines how that representation is geometrically organized.

26. ResNet from First Principles

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous25. Face Alignment: Landmarks, Similarity Transforms, and Why Alignment Matters
This chapter26. ResNet from First Principles
Next27. Face Embeddings: Turning an Identity into Geometry

ResNet was introduced in Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition by He et al.

Primary source: ResNet paper.

ResNet’s historical problem:

making networks deeper was not automatically making them better.

Even ignoring classic vanishing gradients, optimization degradation appeared: adding layers could make training error worse.

Residual learning reframed the transformation.

26.1 Ordinary block

Traditional block learns:

y=H(x) y=H(x)

All desired behavior must be represented by the learned layers.

26.2 Residual block

ResNet writes:

y=F(x)+x y=F(x)+x

Therefore:

F(x)=H(x)−x F(x)=H(x)-x

If identity is useful:

F(x) -> approximately 0

and the shortcut carries x.

That is much easier than requiring stacked nonlinear layers to reconstruct exact identity.

26.3 Gradient intuition

Let:

y=x+F(x,W) y=x+F(x,W)

Then:

∂y∂x=1+∂F∂x \frac{\partial y}{\partial x} = 1+ \frac{\partial F}{\partial x}

The derivative contains a direct identity term.

Over many residual blocks, this creates cleaner gradient highways than a purely sequential chain.

This is not a magical guarantee against every optimization problem, but it fundamentally changes deep-network trainability.

26.4 BasicBlock

ResNet-18/34 traditionally use something like:

x -------------------------+
|                           |
v                           |
3x3 Conv -> BN -> ReLU      |
|                           |
3x3 Conv -> BN              |
|                           |
+---------------------------+
            |
           add
            |
          ReLU

If spatial/channel dimensions change, the shortcut may use projection.

26.5 Bottleneck block

ResNet-50/101/152 use:

x --------------------------------+
|                                  |
v                                  |
1x1 reduce                         |
|                                  |
3x3 process                        |
|                                  |
1x1 expand                         |
|                                  |
+----------------------------------+
                |
               add

The 1×1 layers make deep networks computationally practical.

26.6 ResNet stage structure

A ResNet-50-style classifier is roughly:

stem
 |
stage 1: bottleneck x3
 |
stage 2: bottleneck x4
 |
stage 3: bottleneck x6
 |
stage 4: bottleneck x3
 |
global average pool
 |
classifier

For face recognition, the terminal classifier used during ordinary ImageNet classification is replaced/modified.

The backbone produces an embedding.

26.7 IResNet / face-recognition ResNet variants

Face-recognition libraries often use modified ResNet variants sometimes called IR or IResNet backbones.

These adjust details such as:

  • stem;
  • normalization placement;
  • activation ordering;
  • output embedding head;
  • pooling;
  • PReLU/BN choices.

Therefore saying:

“ArcFace uses ResNet-100”

usually means:

a face-recognition-specific residual backbone roughly in the ResNet family is trained under an ArcFace angular-margin objective.

Again, ArcFace is the loss, not the residual backbone.

26.8 Why ResNet is good for face recognition

Identity discrimination requires deep hierarchical features.

A deep residual model can gradually transform:

pixels
 ->
local edges/textures
 ->
facial parts
 ->
global geometry
 ->
identity-discriminative representation

while maintaining trainable gradient paths.

The final embedding is not a human-interpretable list like:

eye width
nose length
jaw angle

It is a learned distributed representation.

27. Face Embeddings: Turning an Identity into Geometry

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous26. ResNet from First Principles
This chapter27. Face Embeddings: Turning an Identity into Geometry
Next28. FaceNet and Metric Learning

The core idea behind modern face recognition is:

do not make the deployed model output a permanent list of known people.

Instead, map a face into an embedding space.

fθ(I)=z f_\theta(I) = z

where:

  • II = aligned face image;
  • fθf_\theta = neural network;
  • z∈ℝdz \in \mathbb{R}^{d} = embedding vector.

Typical embedding dimensions can be hundreds of values, often 512 in ArcFace/InsightFace-style systems.

27.1 Why embeddings scale better than classifiers

Imagine training a classifier over:

Alice
Bob
Carol
...
1 million users

A new user joins.

Do you retrain the classification head?

That is unacceptable for a payment system.

Embeddings separate representation learning from enrollment.

Enrollment:

new face
  |
embedding network
  |
vector
  |
store template

No retraining.

27.2 Desired geometry

For one person:

front photo        -> z1
slight left pose   -> z2
different lighting -> z3

We want:

d(z1,z2) small d(z_1,z_2) \text{ small}

and:

d(zRazi,zAlice) large d(z_{Razi},z_{Alice}) \text{ large}

The model learns identity-preserving invariance.

27.3 Intra-class versus inter-class

Intra-class variation

Same person varies due to:

  • pose;
  • expression;
  • age;
  • lighting;
  • camera;
  • hairstyle;
  • makeup;
  • occlusion.

Goal:

compress same-person embeddings

Inter-class variation

Different people can look similar.

Goal:

separate different identities

Modern face losses are largely about controlling this geometry.

27.4 L2 normalization

Embeddings are often normalized:

ẑ=z∥z∥2 \hat{z} = \frac{z}{\|z\|_2}

Then:

∥ẑ∥2=1 \|\hat{z}\|_2=1

So every embedding lies on the unit hypersphere.

Now identity information is encoded primarily in direction/angle, not vector magnitude.

That sets the stage for ArcFace.

27.5 Hypersphere intuition

You cannot visualize 512 dimensions, so use 3D.

Imagine all normalized embeddings on a sphere:

              Bob cluster
                 ••
             .-'    '-.
          .-'          '-.
 Alice •• |       O       | •• Carol
          '-.          .-'
             '-.____.-'

Each identity forms a directional cluster.

Training should:

  • tighten each cluster;
  • increase angular gaps between identities.

ArcFace directly manipulates angular decision margins.

28. FaceNet and Metric Learning

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous27. Face Embeddings: Turning an Identity into Geometry
This chapter28. FaceNet and Metric Learning
Next29. Softmax, SphereFace, CosFace, and ArcFace

FaceNet was introduced by Schroff, Kalenichenko, and Philbin in 2015.

Primary source: FaceNet paper.

Its key conceptual contribution:

directly learn an embedding space where distance corresponds to face similarity.

FaceNet famously uses triplet loss.

28.1 Triplet

A training triplet contains:

  • anchor AA;
  • positive PP: same identity;
  • negative NN: different identity.

Desired condition:

d(A,P)+α<d(A,N) d(A,P) + \alpha < d(A,N)

where α\alpha is a margin.

Loss:

L=max(0,d(A,P)−d(A,N)+α) L = \max \left( 0, d(A,P)-d(A,N)+\alpha \right)

28.2 Example

Suppose:

d(anchor, positive) = 0.40
d(anchor, negative) = 0.45
margin = 0.20

Then:

L=max(0,0.40−0.45+0.20)=0.15 L = \max(0,0.40-0.45+0.20)=0.15

The network is penalized because the negative is not sufficiently farther away.

If:

d(A,P)=0.30
d(A,N)=0.90

then:

L=max(0,0.30−0.90+0.20)=0 L=\max(0,0.30-0.90+0.20)=0

The triplet already satisfies the margin.

28.3 Triplet mining

Random triplets are often useless.

Imagine most negatives are already very far away.

They contribute zero loss.

Training needs informative negatives.

Easy negative

Already much farther than the positive.

No learning signal.

Hard negative

Negative is closer than the positive.

Potentially powerful but can destabilize training, especially with label noise.

Semi-hard negative

Farther than positive but still inside the margin.

FaceNet emphasizes mining useful triplets online.

This is a general metric-learning lesson:

the quality of sampled relationships can matter as much as the loss equation.

28.4 Why ArcFace-style classification losses became popular

Triplet loss has difficulties:

  • triplet mining complexity;
  • huge combinatorial space;
  • sensitivity to mining strategy;
  • potentially slower convergence.

Margin-based softmax losses instead use identity class weights during training to organize the hypersphere more globally.

At inference the classification weights disappear; the embedding remains.

29. Softmax, SphereFace, CosFace, and ArcFace

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous28. FaceNet and Metric Learning
This chapter29. Softmax, SphereFace, CosFace, and ArcFace
Next30. ArcFace Mathematics in Detail

To understand ArcFace, start with the ordinary classification logit.

For feature vector xx and class weight WjW_j:

zj=Wj⊤x+bj z_j=W_j^\top x+b_j

Softmax probability:

pj=ezj∑kezk p_j = \frac{e^{z_j}} {\sum_k e^{z_k}}

Cross-entropy encourages the correct class logit to be larger.

But generic softmax does not explicitly optimize a geometrically meaningful verification margin.

29.1 Normalize features and weights

If:

∥Wj∥=1,∥x∥=1 \|W_j\|=1,\quad \|x\|=1

then:

Wj⊤x=cosθj W_j^\top x = \cos\theta_j

where θj\theta_j is the angle between feature x and class prototype weight W_j.

Now classification becomes angular geometry.

Scale by ss:

zj=scosθj z_j=s\cos\theta_j

The scale is needed because normalized cosine values are bounded in [-1,1], which can make softmax optimization poorly conditioned without amplification.

29.2 SphereFace

SphereFace introduced an angular multiplicative margin idea:

cos(mθy) \cos(m\theta_y)

for target class yy.

It encourages stronger angular separation but can be difficult to optimize because the angular transformation is nonlinear and periodic.

29.3 CosFace

CosFace uses an additive cosine margin:

cosθy−m \cos\theta_y - m

before scaling.

It is simpler and effective.

29.4 ArcFace

ArcFace uses an additive angular margin:

cos(θy+m) \cos(\theta_y+m)

This has a particularly clear geometric interpretation directly in angular space.

Primary source: ArcFace: Additive Angular Margin Loss for Deep Face Recognition.

29.5 Why angular margin matters

Suppose two identities have prototype directions:

class A center: 20 degrees
class B center: 60 degrees

Ordinary softmax only needs a sample to fall on the correct side of the classification boundary.

ArcFace demands extra angular room.

It effectively says:

being barely closer to your own class is not enough; be closer by a margin.

That tightens intra-class clusters and increases inter-class separation.

30. ArcFace Mathematics in Detail

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous29. Softmax, SphereFace, CosFace, and ArcFace
This chapter30. ArcFace Mathematics in Detail
Next31. 1:1 Verification vs 1:N Identification

Let:

  • xix_i be the embedding for sample ii;
  • yiy_i its identity class;
  • WjW_j the classifier weight for class jj.

Normalize:

∥xi∥=1 \|x_i\|=1

∥Wj∥=1 \|W_j\|=1

Then:

Wj⊤xi=cosθj W_j^\top x_i = \cos\theta_j

where θj\theta_j is the angle between sample embedding and class weight.

30.1 Ordinary normalized softmax

Target logit:

scosθyi s\cos\theta_{y_i}

Other-class logits:

scosθj s\cos\theta_j

Loss:

Li=−logescosθyiescosθyi+∑j≠yiescosθj L_i = -\log \frac { e^{s\cos\theta_{y_i}} } { e^{s\cos\theta_{y_i}} + \sum_{j\neq y_i} e^{s\cos\theta_j} }

30.2 ArcFace modification

Replace target logit with:

scos(θyi+m) s\cos(\theta_{y_i}+m)

Loss:

Li=−logescos(θyi+m)escos(θyi+m)+∑j≠yiescosθj L_i = -\log \frac { e^{s\cos(\theta_{y_i}+m)} } { e^{s\cos(\theta_{y_i}+m)} + \sum_{j\neq y_i} e^{s\cos\theta_j} }

where:

  • mm is additive angular margin;
  • ss is feature scale.

This tiny-looking equation is the core ArcFace idea.

30.3 Geometric interpretation with two classes

Let class prototypes be W1W_1 and W2W_2.

Ordinary normalized softmax boundary is approximately where:

cosθ1=cosθ2 \cos\theta_1=\cos\theta_2

ArcFace for a class-1 training sample requires:

cos(θ1+m)>cosθ2 \cos(\theta_1+m) > \cos\theta_2

Because cosine decreases as angle increases over the relevant range, adding m makes the target condition harder.

The sample has to move closer in angle to its class center.

30.4 What happens during backpropagation

During training:

  1. aligned face enters residual backbone;
  2. network emits embedding xx;
  3. normalize xx;
  4. compare to normalized identity-class weights WjW_j;
  5. add angular margin only to correct-class angle;
  6. compute cross-entropy;
  7. backpropagate through the angle/logit transformation into the backbone.

Over millions of training examples, the backbone learns to arrange identities on a hypersphere.

30.5 What happens at inference

This is the part often explained incorrectly.

The ArcFace training classifier is not needed for ordinary deployed verification.

Inference:

aligned face
   |
trained backbone
   |
embedding
   |
L2 normalize
   |
compare to enrolled embedding

The learned identity class weights from the training dataset are not your production user database.

You do not add a new softmax class every time a customer enrolls.

30.6 ArcFace is not the database matcher

ArcFace shapes the embedding space during training.

At runtime, matching may use:

  • cosine similarity;
  • Euclidean distance on normalized vectors;
  • ANN retrieval for 1:N;
  • thresholding;
  • re-ranking.

So the architecture layers are:

ResNet/IResNet      = feature extractor
ArcFace             = training objective
embedding           = runtime representation
cosine/vector index = runtime matching mechanism

These four things are distinct.

30.7 Cosine and Euclidean distance become closely related after normalization

For unit vectors a,ba,b:

∥a−b∥22=∥a∥2+∥b∥2−2a⊤b \|a-b\|_2^2 = \|a\|^2+\|b\|^2-2a^\top b

Since both norms equal 1:

∥a−b∥22=2−2a⊤b \|a-b\|_2^2 = 2-2a^\top b

and:

a⊤b=cosθ a^\top b = \cos\theta

Therefore:

∥a−b∥22=2−2cosθ \|a-b\|_2^2 = 2-2\cos\theta

So ranking by cosine similarity and Euclidean distance is equivalent for perfectly normalized embeddings.

This is why normalization is so central.

30.8 Sub-center ArcFace

The ArcFace research line also introduced Sub-center ArcFace to improve robustness to noisy labels.

Instead of one center/prototype per identity:

identity A -> W_A

allow K sub-centers:

identity A -> W_A1, W_A2, ..., W_AK

A training sample only needs to be close to one positive sub-center.

Intuition:

dominant clean cluster
   +
small noisy/outlier subclusters

This helps isolate mislabeled or difficult samples rather than forcing all examples into one center.

For real biometric training data, label quality is enormously important. A powerful loss cannot fully compensate for corrupted identity labels.

30.9 Why training identity count matters

ArcFace-style classification training often involves a very large number of identities.

The classifier matrix can become huge:

W∈ℝd×Nclasses W \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times N_{classes}}

If:

d = 512
N = 10,000,000 identities

the full classifier is enormous.

Large-scale face training therefore uses techniques such as partial class sampling / Partial FC in some systems.

This is a training-system challenge, separate from deployed embedding lookup.

30.10 What ArcFace does not solve

ArcFace does not by itself solve:

  • face detection;
  • alignment;
  • bad image quality;
  • liveness;
  • presentation attacks;
  • deepfake injection;
  • demographic calibration;
  • template encryption;
  • payment fraud;
  • account takeover;
  • sensor tampering.

It solves a narrower but fundamental problem:

learn highly discriminative face embeddings with an explicit angular margin.

That clarity should govern architecture discussions.

Connection Bridge — From Learned Geometry to an Actual Identity Decision

ArcFace-style training gives you a good embedding space, not a finished authentication system. Once embeddings exist, the system must decide how to use them. In 1:1 verification, an account claim already narrows the comparison to one enrolled template. In 1:N identification, the system first has to search a population and then decide whether the best candidate is trustworthy enough.

That difference changes both the statistics and the systems architecture. Thresholds that look acceptable in isolated pair comparisons may behave differently at population scale; approximate nearest-neighbor retrieval adds another stage; false-match risk accumulates differently. The next chapters therefore move from neural-network training into decision theory, metrics, thresholds, and retrieval systems.

31. 1:1 Verification vs 1:N Identification

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous30. ArcFace Mathematics in Detail
This chapter31. 1:1 Verification vs 1:N Identification
Next32. Cosine Similarity, Euclidean Distance, Thresholds, FAR, FRR, EER, TAR

Biometric systems often use the words recognition, verification, and identification loosely. For a payment architecture, that is dangerous because they create different statistical and infrastructure problems.

31.1 Verification

Verification asks:

“Does this biometric match the claimed identity?”

The person/account has already been specified.

compare(Equery,Eclaimed) \text{compare}(E_{query}, E_{claimed})

This is one comparison, or a small template set if an account stores multiple enrollment embeddings.

Output:

match score = 0.71
threshold   = 0.63
decision    = match

The threshold is not universal. It must be calibrated for:

  • model;
  • camera;
  • preprocessing;
  • enrollment quality;
  • target FAR;
  • deployment population;
  • attack model.

31.2 Identification

Identification asks:

“Whose biometric is this?”

j*=argmaxjsim(Equery,Ej) j^* = \arg\max_j sim(E_{query}, E_j)

over a gallery.

A nearest neighbor is always returned unless the system includes an open-set rejection threshold.

That means:

nearest identity

does not imply:

correct identity

The system must ask:

Is nearest similarity high enough?
Is the margin over the second-best candidate high enough?
Did liveness pass?
Is gallery search calibrated for this population size?

31.3 Open-set identification

Real payment systems are open-set.

Someone may stand in front of the camera who is not enrolled.

The correct output must be:

UNKNOWN

not the nearest customer.

This creates two problems:

  1. candidate ranking;
  2. reject/accept calibration.

31.4 Gallery size changes the operational risk

Suppose an impostor has a tiny probability p of accidentally exceeding a threshold against any one template.

Searching against millions of templates creates many opportunities.

The comparisons are not perfectly independent, so simple multiplication is not an exact risk calculation, but the intuition is important:

A threshold calibrated for 1:1 verification cannot automatically be assumed safe for 1:N identification at massive scale.

Operational evaluation must match the actual gallery size and search process.

32. Cosine Similarity, Euclidean Distance, Thresholds, FAR, FRR, EER, TAR

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous31. 1:1 Verification vs 1:N Identification
This chapter32. Cosine Similarity, Euclidean Distance, Thresholds, FAR, FRR, EER, TAR
Next33. Vector Search at Scale: Exact Search, IVF, HNSW, and Candidate Re-Ranking

A biometric recognizer does not produce certainty.

It produces score distributions.

Imagine genuine comparisons:

same person scores:
0.58 0.62 0.70 0.81 0.74 ...

Impostor comparisons:

different people:
0.08 0.15 0.27 0.31 0.22 ...

Ideally the distributions are widely separated.

Reality:

                genuine
               /-------\
      impostor/         \
      /------\            \
_____/        \____ overlap \____

The overlap is where errors occur.

32.1 False Match Rate

FMR/FAR refers to impostor comparisons incorrectly accepted.

Terminology varies by standard/context; careful documents distinguish false match rate at the comparison layer from broader false acceptance at system level.

Conceptually:

FMR=impostor comparisons acceptedimpostor comparisons FMR = \frac{\text{impostor comparisons accepted}} {\text{impostor comparisons}}

In a payment context, this is security-sensitive.

32.2 False Non-Match Rate

FNMR=genuine comparisons rejectedgenuine comparisons FNMR = \frac{\text{genuine comparisons rejected}} {\text{genuine comparisons}}

This affects user friction.

A customer is legitimate but gets rejected.

32.3 Threshold tradeoff

Raise threshold:

harder to match

Typical effect:

  • fewer false matches;
  • more false non-matches.

Lower threshold:

  • fewer genuine users rejected;
  • more impostors potentially accepted.

There is no magical threshold that simultaneously minimizes both.

You choose based on risk.

32.4 Equal Error Rate

EER is the operating point where:

FMR=FNMR FMR = FNMR

It is convenient for benchmarking.

But it is often not the correct production operating point.

Financial authentication may require an extremely low false-match rate, accepting a higher false-non-match rate and falling back to another factor.

32.5 TAR at FAR

Research often reports:

TAR @ FAR = 1e-4
TAR @ FAR = 1e-6

True Accept Rate is:

TAR=1−FNMR TAR = 1-FNMR

A claim like:

TAR 95% at FAR 1e-6

means the system accepts about 95% of genuine attempts while operating at a measured impostor false-accept regime of one in a million comparisons under the test protocol.

Do not extrapolate a benchmark number directly to production.

Data distributions and attack conditions differ.

32.6 ROC and DET curves

A ROC-like biometric curve plots:

true accept rate vs false accept/match rate

A DET curve often makes error tradeoffs easier to inspect in low-error regimes.

For payment, you care about the tail of impostor distributions.

Average accuracy can look excellent while security-critical tails remain problematic.

32.7 Demographic performance

A single global metric can hide subgroup differences.

Evaluation should stratify relevant groups and acquisition conditions.

NIST SP 800-63B’s current biometric guidance explicitly requires stringent biometric accuracy and consideration of demographic groups where relevant, and requires PAD for facial recognition in its authentication context.

Reference: NIST SP 800-63B, Authenticator requirements.

For a commercial Indonesian payment system, NIST is not automatically the governing regulation, but it is a useful security-engineering reference.

32.8 Multiple enrollment templates

Instead of one embedding:

account -> E1

you may store:

account -> E1, E2, E3

capturing controlled variation.

Matching can use:

  • maximum similarity;
  • average template;
  • learned template aggregation;
  • quality-weighted aggregation.

But more templates increase storage/privacy exposure and change score distributions.

Every aggregation strategy requires calibration.

33. Vector Search at Scale: Exact Search, IVF, HNSW, and Candidate Re-Ranking

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous32. Cosine Similarity, Euclidean Distance, Thresholds, FAR, FRR, EER, TAR
This chapter33. Vector Search at Scale: Exact Search, IVF, HNSW, and Candidate Re-Ranking
Next34. Transformers for Vision: Q, K, V, Self-Attention, Cross-Attention

If the architecture uses 1:N identification, comparing one query against every enrolled embedding may be expensive.

Let:

N = 100 million
d = 512

A brute-force cosine scan requires billions of multiply-add operations per query.

GPUs can make brute force surprisingly practical at certain scales, but production systems often use Approximate Nearest Neighbor (ANN) indexes.

33.1 Exact search

Matrix view:

S=Eq S = E q

where:

  • E∈ℝN×dE \in \mathbb{R}^{N\times d} is gallery matrix;
  • q∈ℝdq \in \mathbb{R}^{d} is query.

For normalized vectors, dot product equals cosine similarity.

A highly optimized matrix/vector operation can be very fast.

Advantages:

  • exact ranking;
  • simple semantics.

Disadvantages:

  • cost grows linearly with gallery size;
  • memory bandwidth becomes important.

33.2 IVF: inverted file index

An IVF-like index first clusters embedding space into coarse regions.

Enrollment:

embedding
   |
nearest coarse centroid
   |
store in that list

Query:

q
 |
find nearest coarse centroids
 |
search only their lists

Instead of scanning all N, scan a subset.

Tradeoff:

search fewer lists -> faster, lower recall
search more lists  -> slower, higher recall

33.3 Product Quantization

High-dimensional vectors consume memory.

Product Quantization splits a vector into subvectors and quantizes each portion with a codebook.

Conceptually:

512-d vector
 |
split into groups
 |
each group -> nearest codeword
 |
store compact codes

Distance can be approximated from lookup tables.

Advantages:

  • much smaller memory footprint;
  • better cache behavior.

Cost:

  • approximation error.

For payment identity, candidate retrieval may use compressed representations but a final high-assurance comparison can re-score candidates using original/encrypted high-quality templates.

33.4 HNSW

Hierarchical Navigable Small World graphs organize vectors as a proximity graph.

At high levels:

few long-range links

At low levels:

dense local neighbor links

Search starts coarse and descends.

Conceptually:

query
 |
enter sparse top layer
 |
greedily move toward closer nodes
 |
descend
 |
refine search
 |
top-k nearest candidates

HNSW is popular because it often achieves excellent recall/latency tradeoffs.

33.5 ANN is retrieval, not final identity proof

Suppose HNSW returns:

candidate 1: user A
candidate 2: user B
candidate 3: user C

That is a retrieval stage.

A robust system can then perform exact similarities:

query vs A
query vs B
query vs C

and apply:

  • threshold;
  • score margin;
  • liveness;
  • transaction context.

Architecture:

embedding
 |
ANN candidate generation
 |
top K
 |
exact re-ranking
 |
open-set threshold
 |
identity candidate

Do not let ANN approximation directly authorize payment.

Connection Bridge — A Second Family of Visual Representation

So far, the dominant story has been convolutional: local filters build hierarchies, feature pyramids merge scales, and heads predict geometry. Transformers challenge the first assumption. Instead of hard-coding locality into convolution, they turn image regions into tokens and learn which tokens should interact through attention.

DETR matters because it applies that representational shift to detection itself. Rather than generating a dense cloud of candidates and pruning them afterward, DETR predicts a set of objects through learned queries and one-to-one matching. SAM then extends the transformer/foundation-model direction from fixed-category detection toward promptable segmentation, video memory, concept reasoning, and tracking. Read these chapters as a parallel evolutionary branch, not as “YOLO but with attention.”

34. Transformers for Vision: Q, K, V, Self-Attention, Cross-Attention

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous33. Vector Search at Scale: Exact Search, IVF, HNSW, and Candidate Re-Ranking
This chapter34. Transformers for Vision: Q, K, V, Self-Attention, Cross-Attention
Next35. Vision Transformer (ViT): Images as Token Sequences

To understand DETR and SAM 3, you need more than the phrase “uses transformers.”

The core transformer operation is attention.

Given token matrix XX, learn projections:

Q=XWQ Q=XW_Q

K=XWK K=XW_K

V=XWV V=XW_V

Then:

Attention(Q,K,V)=softmax(QK⊤dk)V Attention(Q,K,V) = softmax\left(\frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V

34.1 What Q, K, V mean operationally

A useful analogy:

  • Query: what information am I looking for?
  • Key: what information does this token advertise?
  • Value: what content should be retrieved if relevant?

For query token qiq_i, compute similarity with every key:

qi⊤kj q_i^\top k_j

Softmax converts those scores into weights.

Then sum values:

yi=∑jaijvj y_i = \sum_j a_{ij} v_j

So a token dynamically gathers information from other tokens.

34.2 Why divide by dk\sqrt{d_k}

Dot-product magnitude grows with dimension.

Large logits can make softmax extremely peaky and hurt optimization.

Scaling:

QK⊤dk \frac{QK^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}

keeps score variance more manageable.

34.3 Self-attention

Queries, keys, and values come from the same token set.

image tokens
 |
+--> Q
+--> K
+--> V
 |
self-attention

Every image token can reason about other image tokens.

34.4 Cross-attention

Queries come from one representation; keys/values from another.

Example in DETR:

object queries -> Q
image features -> K,V

Each object query searches the image.

Example in SAM 3 fusion:

image features
cross-attend with
text/exemplar prompt tokens

Now image representation becomes conditioned on the requested concept.

34.5 Multi-head attention

Instead of one attention function, split representation into multiple heads:

head 1
head 2
head 3
...

Each head has separate learned projections and can specialize in different relationships.

Outputs are concatenated/projected.

MultiHead(Q,K,V)=Concat(head1,…,headh)WO MultiHead(Q,K,V) = Concat(head_1,\ldots,head_h)W_O

No guarantee says head 1 will become “edges” and head 2 “faces.” Specialization emerges.

34.6 Positional information

Pure self-attention is largely permutation equivariant: it does not inherently know that token A came from upper-left and token B from lower-right.

Vision transformers therefore inject positional information.

Options include:

  • learned absolute positional embeddings;
  • sinusoidal embeddings;
  • relative position biases;
  • rotary-like position mechanisms;
  • box/region positional biases.

DETR and SAM-family systems use positional structure because localization is impossible without spatial awareness.

34.7 Transformer block

A canonical block:

x
|
LayerNorm
|
Multi-Head Attention
|
+ residual
|
LayerNorm
|
MLP / FFN
|
+ residual

The feed-forward network usually expands feature dimension then compresses it.

Transformer architecture alternates:

  • token interaction via attention;
  • per-token nonlinear processing via FFN.

34.8 Attention cost

For N tokens, ordinary full attention creates an N×NN\times N matrix.

Complexity roughly:

O(N2d) O(N^2 d)

For images, token count can be large.

That is why:

  • ViT uses patches;
  • hierarchical transformers reduce resolution;
  • windowed attention restricts interactions;
  • Deformable DETR samples sparse points;
  • CNN/attention hybrids place global attention at low resolution.

Efficiency architecture is largely about controlling token count.

35. Vision Transformer (ViT): Images as Token Sequences

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous34. Transformers for Vision: Q, K, V, Self-Attention, Cross-Attention
This chapter35. Vision Transformer (ViT): Images as Token Sequences
Next36. DETR: Object Detection as Set Prediction

Vision Transformer adapted the transformer idea by dividing an image into patches.

Suppose image:

224 x 224

Patch size:

16 x 16

Number of patches:

14×14=196 14\times14=196

Each patch is flattened/projected into a token.

image
 |
split into patches
 |
linear projection
 |
patch tokens
 |
+ positional embeddings
 |
Transformer encoder

35.1 Patch embedding is effectively learned downsampling

A patch projection can be implemented like a convolution:

kernel = patch size
stride = patch size

So the gap between CNN and ViT is smaller than diagrams sometimes imply.

Both transform local pixel neighborhoods into feature vectors.

The difference is what happens afterward.

35.2 CNN inductive bias vs transformer flexibility

CNN assumes:

  • locality;
  • weight sharing;
  • translation-friendly feature extraction.

Transformer assumes less local structure and learns relationships through attention.

Consequences:

  • CNNs can be data-efficient and hardware-friendly;
  • transformers can model global interactions flexibly;
  • hybrids often combine both.

35.3 Hierarchical vision transformers

A flat ViT keeps one token resolution.

Dense prediction often benefits from pyramids.

Hierarchical transformers progressively reduce spatial token count, analogous to CNN stages.

This makes them friendlier for:

  • detection;
  • segmentation;
  • multi-scale features.

SAM 2 uses a hierarchical backbone architecture in its released design family, while SAM 3 uses Meta’s Perception Encoder backbone.

36. DETR: Object Detection as Set Prediction

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous35. Vision Transformer (ViT): Images as Token Sequences
This chapter36. DETR: Object Detection as Set Prediction
Next37. Hungarian Matching: Why DETR Does Not Need Ordinary NMS

DETR — DEtection TRansformer was introduced by Carion et al.

Primary source: End-to-End Object Detection with Transformers.

DETR’s conceptual breakthrough is not merely:

“use a transformer for detection.”

It is:

formulate object detection as direct set prediction.

Traditional dense detector:

thousands of candidate locations/anchors
 |
scores + boxes
 |
NMS
 |
final set

DETR:

fixed set of learned object queries
 |
transformer decoder
 |
one prediction per query
 |
set-matching training
 |
final set

36.1 Image feature extraction

Original DETR uses a CNN backbone such as ResNet to create a feature map.

image
 |
ResNet
 |
C x H' x W'

Flatten spatial locations into tokens:

N = H' * W'

Add positional encoding.

These tokens enter transformer processing.

36.2 Transformer encoder

Self-attention among image tokens allows global context.

A token representing one region can directly interact with distant regions rather than waiting for many convolutional layers to enlarge receptive field.

This helps global object reasoning.

36.3 Object queries

DETR creates a fixed set of learned query vectors:

q1
q2
q3
...
q100

They do not have fixed semantic labels like:

q1 = person
q2 = car

They are latent detection slots.

During decoding, they compete/cooperate to explain objects in the image.

36.4 Decoder cross-attention

Each query attends to image features.

query q_i
    |
    +------ cross-attention ------> image token set
                                   |
                                   v
                            gathered object info

After several decoder layers, each query predicts:

  • class;
  • box;
  • or “no object.”

36.5 Why queries can avoid duplicate objects

The model is trained with one-to-one bipartite matching.

Ground truth:

GT1: person
GT2: car
GT3: dog

Predictions:

Q1
Q2
Q3
...
Q100

Hungarian matching assigns each GT to one prediction.

Other queries learn “no object.”

The loss therefore teaches:

one target should be explained by one query.

This is the root of DETR’s NMS-free behavior.

36.6 DETR versus YOLO intuition

YOLO:

"At many spatial positions, do you see an object here?"

DETR:

"I have a set of latent object slots; each slot should claim at most one object."

This is not exact mathematical wording, but it captures the representational difference.

36.7 Original DETR limitations

The original model had challenges:

  • long training schedule;
  • weaker small-object performance;
  • expensive global attention at higher resolutions;
  • slow convergence of object queries.

This spawned many successors:

  • Deformable DETR;
  • Conditional DETR;
  • DAB-DETR;
  • DINO;
  • RT-DETR;
  • and others.

SAM 3’s image detector is explicitly described as DETR-based, but it includes important modern changes and prompt conditioning.

37. Hungarian Matching: Why DETR Does Not Need Ordinary NMS

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous36. DETR: Object Detection as Set Prediction
This chapter37. Hungarian Matching: Why DETR Does Not Need Ordinary NMS
Next38. Deformable DETR and Efficient Attention

Suppose there are three ground-truth objects and five predictions.

We need a one-to-one assignment.

Construct a cost matrix:

Cij=cost of assigning prediction i to GT j C_{ij} = \text{cost of assigning prediction } i \text{ to GT } j

Cost can combine:

  • class mismatch;
  • L1 box distance;
  • IoU/GIoU box cost.

Example:

          GT person   GT car   GT dog
Q1           0.2       3.1      2.8
Q2           2.9       0.3      2.4
Q3           2.6       2.2      0.4
Q4           0.7       2.9      2.1
Q5           3.0       1.8      2.6

Greedy assignment might make a locally attractive choice that harms total matching.

The Hungarian algorithm finds the minimum-cost bipartite matching.

Result:

Q1 -> person
Q2 -> car
Q3 -> dog
Q4 -> no-object
Q5 -> no-object

37.1 Set loss

Once assignments are chosen, train:

  • matched query classification;
  • matched query box regression;
  • unmatched queries as “no object.”

The loss is permutation-invariant to prediction order.

It does not matter whether person is emitted by Q1 or Q78.

It matters that exactly one suitable query explains it.

37.2 Why this changes detector behavior

In dense detectors, many locations can legitimately learn the same object.

Then NMS removes duplicates.

In DETR:

duplicate predictions

compete during matching.

Only one receives the positive assignment.

The other duplicate is penalized as no-object.

So uniqueness is trained into the prediction process.

37.3 One-to-one assignment tradeoff

Sparse one-to-one supervision can make optimization harder.

This is why modern YOLO end-to-end systems often retain one-to-many auxiliary supervision during training.

The broad research convergence is interesting:

DETR world:
start one-to-one, make training denser/easier

YOLO world:
start one-to-many, add one-to-one for NMS-free inference

The families approach similar end goals from opposite directions.

38. Deformable DETR and Efficient Attention

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous37. Hungarian Matching: Why DETR Does Not Need Ordinary NMS
This chapter38. Deformable DETR and Efficient Attention
Next39. Segmentation: Semantic, Instance, Panoptic, and Promptable

Full cross-attention over every image token can be expensive and diffuse.

Deformable DETR introduces sparse, learned sampling.

Instead of one query attending densely over all pixels/tokens:

q -> [everywhere]

it attends to a small number of sampling points around reference locations across multi-scale features.

Conceptually:

P3 map: sample o o o
P4 map: sample   o o
P5 map: sample     o

The locations and weights are learned.

38.1 Why “deformable”

In deformable convolution, sampling locations are learned rather than fixed on a regular kernel grid.

Deformable attention borrows the philosophy:

attend where useful, not everywhere.

For each query/head/feature level, the model predicts sampling offsets relative to reference points.

38.2 Multi-scale deformable attention

A small object benefits from fine-resolution P3.

A large object may benefit from P5 context.

Deformable attention can sample several feature pyramid levels.

This addresses two original DETR weaknesses:

  • small objects;
  • convergence speed.

38.3 SAM 3 and vanilla attention nuance

The SAM 3 paper says its detector follows the DETR paradigm and predicts iterative box deltas following Deformable-DETR-style box refinement, but it explicitly notes that it uses vanilla attention in the described detector while adding box-region positional bias.

This is a good example of why model names are not enough.

A paper may borrow:

  • iterative reference-box refinement from one family;
  • vanilla attention from another;
  • MaskFormer mask heads;
  • a new presence token;
  • a separate tracker.

Real models are compositions of research ideas.

Connection Bridge — Boxes Are Sometimes Too Coarse

Detection deliberately compresses an object into a rectangle. That is computationally convenient, but a box contains background and says nothing about exact shape. Segmentation changes the output representation from four box coordinates to pixel membership. This is not automatically better for every task; it is simply richer and more expensive information.

SAM’s importance comes from making that richer representation promptable. Instead of training a separate segmentation model for every closed label set, the system can condition mask prediction on points, boxes, text/concepts, exemplars, or temporal memory depending on the generation. That is why SAM belongs after DETR and attention: its flexibility is built on the idea that visual representations can be conditioned by prompts and queried dynamically.

39. Segmentation: Semantic, Instance, Panoptic, and Promptable

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous38. Deformable DETR and Efficient Attention
This chapter39. Segmentation: Semantic, Instance, Panoptic, and Promptable
Next40. SAM 1: Image Encoder, Prompt Encoder, Mask Decoder

Detection predicts rectangles.

Segmentation predicts pixel ownership.

That sounds like a small difference, but the output representation changes the task fundamentally.

39.1 Semantic segmentation

Semantic segmentation assigns a class to every pixel:

pixel -> person
pixel -> floor
pixel -> wall
pixel -> terminal

If two people stand together, both are simply person.

There is no separate identity for person A versus person B.

Output:

Y∈{1,…,C}H×W Y \in \{1,\ldots,C\}^{H\times W}

39.2 Instance segmentation

Instance segmentation separates individual objects:

person instance 1
person instance 2
person instance 3

Each object has its own mask.

Output is a set:

{(mi,ci,si)}i=1N \{(m_i,c_i,s_i)\}_{i=1}^N

where mim_i is a binary/soft mask.

This is more relevant when the system needs:

“the customer directly in front of the terminal,”

rather than merely:

“person pixels exist.”

39.3 Panoptic segmentation

Panoptic segmentation combines:

  • things: countable instances such as people/cars;
  • stuff: amorphous regions such as road/sky/wall.

The goal is a complete scene partition with instance identity for “things.”

39.4 Promptable segmentation

SAM changed the interface.

Instead of defining a fixed class vocabulary:

person
car
dog
chair

the system accepts a prompt describing what should be segmented.

SAM 1 visual prompts:

positive click
negative click
bounding box
mask

SAM 3 extends the concept to:

noun phrase
image exemplar
visual refinement prompts

That turns segmentation into a conditioned task.

Formally:

M=f(I,P) M = f(I,P)

where:

  • II is media;
  • PP is prompt;
  • MM is the requested mask/set of masks.

40. SAM 1: Image Encoder, Prompt Encoder, Mask Decoder

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous39. Segmentation: Semantic, Instance, Panoptic, and Promptable
This chapter40. SAM 1: Image Encoder, Prompt Encoder, Mask Decoder
Next41. SAM 2: Streaming Memory for Video

The original Segment Anything Model was introduced in 2023.

Primary research source: Segment Anything, ICCV 2023.

The architecture is deliberately asymmetric:

EXPENSIVE:
image encoder
     |
     v
reusable image embedding

CHEAP:
prompt encoder
     |
mask decoder
     |
mask

The point is interactive latency.

If a user makes ten clicks on the same image, you do not want to re-run a huge image backbone ten times.

40.1 Image encoder

SAM 1 uses a large Vision Transformer-style image encoder pretrained using masked-autoencoder techniques.

Conceptually:

1024-ish image
  |
patch embedding
  |
vision transformer
  |
dense image feature grid

The expensive image representation is computed once.

40.2 Why a dense image embedding

Classification needs one global vector:

image -> "cat"

Segmentation needs spatial output.

So the encoder preserves a spatial token grid.

Each output token corresponds to a region of the image and carries semantic/contextual information.

40.3 Prompt encoder: sparse prompts

A positive point contains:

(x, y)
label = foreground

A negative point:

(x, y)
label = background

SAM converts coordinates into positional encodings and combines them with learned embeddings that indicate prompt type.

Conceptually:

epoint=PE(x,y)+eforeground/background e_{point} = PE(x,y)+e_{foreground/background}

A box is represented through its corners plus learned corner-type embeddings.

This turns geometric user intent into tokens.

40.4 Dense mask prompts

A mask prompt already has spatial structure.

Instead of representing it as one token, SAM passes it through convolutions to produce a dense prompt embedding compatible with image features.

Thus:

points/boxes -> sparse tokens
mask         -> dense spatial embedding

40.5 Learned output tokens

The mask decoder also includes learned output tokens.

These behave somewhat like query slots:

mask token 1
mask token 2
mask token 3
IoU/quality token

They interact with prompt and image features.

40.6 Two-Way Transformer

The SAM mask decoder uses a Two-Way Transformer.

Ordinary cross-attention:

prompt/query -> image

SAM also lets image features attend back toward tokens.

Conceptually one decoder block contains operations like:

token self-attention
        |
token -> image cross-attention
        |
token MLP
        |
image -> token cross-attention

This is why “two-way.”

Information flows in both directions.

40.7 Dynamic mask prediction

After transformer interaction:

  1. image features are upsampled;
  2. a mask token is passed through a small MLP;
  3. that output acts like a dynamic linear classifier over spatial image features.

Very simplified:

m(x,y)=wmask⊤F(x,y) m(x,y) = w_{mask}^{\top}F(x,y)

where wmaskw_{mask} is generated from the mask token.

So the prompt-conditioned token effectively produces a mask-specific classifier.

40.8 Ambiguity-aware multiple masks

Suppose you click on the center of a person’s shirt.

Possible intended objects:

shirt
person torso
whole person

A single answer can be ambiguous.

SAM predicts multiple candidate masks plus quality scores.

This is important conceptually:

uncertainty can sometimes be represented as multiple plausible structured outputs instead of forcing one averaged answer.

40.9 SAM 1 is not a semantic detector by default

If you click a face, SAM can segment the object.

But it does not inherently say:

this is Razi

or necessarily:

this is class "face"

It solves promptable segmentation, not biometric identity recognition.

41. SAM 2: Streaming Memory for Video

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous40. SAM 1: Image Encoder, Prompt Encoder, Mask Decoder
This chapter41. SAM 2: Streaming Memory for Video
Next42. SAM 3: Segment Anything with Concepts

SAM 2 extends promptable segmentation from static images to images and video.

Primary sources:

  • Meta SAM 2 research page
  • SAM 2 paper

Meta describes SAM 2 as a transformer architecture with streaming memory.

The key new challenge is time.

41.1 Why video is not just independent images

Suppose frames:

t=1: person visible
t=2: person turns
t=3: partial occlusion
t=4: hidden behind another person
t=5: visible again

If each frame is segmented independently, the model discards valuable temporal evidence.

A video model should remember:

  • previous mask;
  • previous appearance;
  • object location history;
  • prompt context.

41.2 SAM 2 architecture mental model

current frame
    |
image encoder
    |
current-frame features
    |
memory attention <----------- memory bank
    |
prompt/mask decoder
    |
current mask
    |
memory encoder
    |
update memory bank

The memory loop is the central architectural extension.

41.3 Image encoder

SAM 2’s released architecture family uses a hierarchical visual backbone rather than simply reusing SAM 1’s original flat large ViT design.

A hierarchical encoder naturally gives multi-resolution features and efficient high-resolution perception.

The exact backbone family/model size depends on checkpoint.

41.4 Memory bank

The memory bank stores representations derived from earlier frames and conditioning frames.

It is not equivalent to:

save every raw video frame forever

It stores learned memory features useful for propagating object identity/masks.

This is conceptually similar to an external state for a recurrent system.

41.5 Memory encoder

After predicting a mask, SAM 2 encodes information about:

frame feature + mask/object state

into memory representation.

The memory needs to preserve appearance and spatial clues relevant to that tracked object.

41.6 Memory attention

For current frame features:

Q = current frame representation
K,V = memory representations

Cross-attention asks:

“Which past object information helps interpret the current frame?”

This gives the model a learned temporal retrieval mechanism.

41.7 Streaming design

Instead of processing the whole video as one gigantic tensor:

all frames at once

SAM 2 can operate sequentially:

frame t
  ->
use memory from previous frames
  ->
predict
  ->
update memory
  ->
frame t+1

This is suitable for interactive/online video.

41.8 Occlusion

Memory is valuable when the object temporarily disappears.

Without temporal state:

object missing -> tracking identity lost

With memory:

object missing briefly
     |
past appearance retained
     |
object returns
     |
re-associate

No tracker is perfect, but memory improves temporal consistency.

41.9 Why tracking identity is not biometric identity

SAM can preserve:

object track ID = 7

meaning:

the same visual object across these frames.

That does not imply:

customer account ID = 382994

Track identity is session-local object continuity.

Biometric identity is a person-to-enrollment relationship.

Never collapse these concepts.

42. SAM 3: Segment Anything with Concepts

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous41. SAM 2: Streaming Memory for Video
This chapter42. SAM 3: Segment Anything with Concepts
Next43. SAM 3 Detector: Perception Encoder, Prompt Tokens, Fusion Encoder, DETR Decoder

SAM 3 was introduced by Meta in November 2025.

Primary sources:

  • Meta SAM 3 research page
  • SAM 3 paper

SAM 3 expands from:

“segment the object indicated by this point/box”

toward:

“find, segment, and track all instances of this visual concept.”

The paper calls this Promptable Concept Segmentation (PCS).

42.1 Prompt types

SAM 3 supports concept prompts such as:

Noun phrase

"red backpack"
"person wearing glasses"
"yellow school bus"

The research formulation intentionally focuses on relatively simple visually groundable noun phrases rather than unrestricted reasoning-heavy language.

Image exemplars

A user/system provides an example box:

this object is a positive example

or:

this object is a negative example

The system finds other instances that match the concept.

Visual refinement prompts

Like SAM 2-style clicks/boxes/masks for correcting individual masks.

42.2 Output

PCS aims to return:

  • all matching instances;
  • instance masks;
  • semantic mask information;
  • object identities across video frames.

This combines ideas from:

open-vocabulary detection
instance segmentation
tracking
promptable segmentation

in one model family.

42.3 Top-level SAM 3 structure

The SAM 3 paper describes:

                 Perception Encoder
                 /                \
                /                  \
        DETECTOR                  TRACKER
        image-level                video
            |                       |
concept recognition +          memory-based
localization                   mask propagation

Detector and tracker share the vision backbone but are architecturally decoupled because their objectives conflict.

42.4 Why detector and tracker have conflicting goals

Detector:

identify all objects matching a category/concept.

It should be identity-agnostic within that concept.

Tracker:

preserve distinction between individual instances over time.

If there are three people:

detector:
person, person, person

tracker:
person track A
person track B
person track C

Detection wants category equivalence.

Tracking wants instance separation.

SAM 3 explicitly separates these jobs.

43. SAM 3 Detector: Perception Encoder, Prompt Tokens, Fusion Encoder, DETR Decoder

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous42. SAM 3: Segment Anything with Concepts
This chapter43. SAM 3 Detector: Perception Encoder, Prompt Tokens, Fusion Encoder, DETR Decoder
Next44. SAM 3 Presence Token: Decoupling Recognition from Localization

The SAM 3 detector is one of the most interesting architectures in modern vision because it combines aligned vision-language representation with DETR-style object queries and segmentation.

The paper describes a dual encoder-decoder transformer for image-level capability.

Let’s expand it.

43.1 Perception Encoder (PE)

SAM 3 uses Meta’s Perception Encoder backbone.

Primary source: Meta Perception Encoder research.

PE is a large-scale vision encoder trained using vision-language learning and alignment strategies for general perception.

One interesting result from the Perception Encoder research is that the best transferable visual representations for certain tasks may appear in intermediate layers, not simply at the final network output.

Meta introduces alignment mechanisms so the encoder can support:

  • semantic/language alignment;
  • spatial/dense prediction tasks.

This makes it suitable as a shared backbone for SAM 3.

43.2 Image encoding

Image:

I I

passes through PE:

FI=PEvision(I) F_I = PE_{vision}(I)

producing spatial visual embeddings.

These initially exist independently of the user’s requested concept.

Call them unconditioned image embeddings.

43.3 Text prompt encoding

A noun phrase:

"person wearing red glasses"

is converted into text/prompt tokens through the aligned language side.

Conceptually:

T=PEtext(prompt) T = PE_{text}(prompt)

These tokens encode the requested concept.

43.4 Exemplar encoder

If the prompt includes a positive/negative visual example, SAM 3 uses an exemplar encoder.

The paper describes exemplar encoding using:

  • bounding-box positional information;
  • binary positive/negative label embedding;
  • ROI-pooled visual features;
  • a small transformer.

Conceptually:

example bbox
   |
ROI pool image feature
   |
+ position embedding
+ positive/negative embedding
   |
small transformer
   |
exemplar tokens

43.5 Prompt tokens

Text and exemplar representations are combined as prompt tokens.

text tokens ----------+
                      |
exemplar tokens ------+--> prompt token set

This is important.

SAM 3 does not merely concatenate a text string onto the final classifier.

The prompt conditions visual representation before object queries make final predictions.

43.6 Fusion encoder

The SAM 3 paper says the fusion encoder takes unconditioned image embeddings and conditions them by cross-attending to prompt tokens.

Think:

image features
     |
     |  cross-attention
     v
prompt-conditioned image features
     ^
     |
prompt tokens

Now the visual tensor is not just:

“what is in this image?”

It becomes more like:

“which visual evidence matters given the concept I am asking about?”

43.7 DETR-like decoder

After prompt-conditioned fusion:

learned object queries
        |
        v
cross-attend to conditioned image embeddings
        |
        v
query representations
        |
        +--> binary class/match score
        +--> bounding box
        +--> mask

The SAM 3 paper says each decoder layer predicts:

  • a binary classification logit indicating whether the object query matches the prompt;
  • a box delta refining the previous layer’s box prediction.

This is classic iterative DETR-style refinement.

43.8 Binary match classification

Closed-set COCO detector:

query ->
person score
car score
dog score
...

SAM 3 concept detector:

prompt = "red backpack"

query ->
does this object match "red backpack"?
yes/no score

The concept semantics enter through conditioning.

This is one reason open-vocabulary systems have a different output contract from fixed-class detectors.

43.9 Box-region positional bias

The paper says SAM 3 uses box-region positional bias to help focus attention on each object’s spatial region.

Why?

An object query that has already estimated:

rough box around left side

should preferentially inspect relevant features near that region during refinement.
