This provides a spatial prior to attention.

43.10 Mask head

SAM 3 adapts its mask head from MaskFormer-like architectures.

At high level:

object query
   |
mask embedding
   |
dot/interact with pixel-level image features
   |
per-pixel mask score

This lets a query produce both:

bbox
mask

for the same instance.

43.11 Semantic segmentation head

The paper also describes a semantic head that predicts a binary label for every pixel:

does this pixel correspond to the requested prompt?

So SAM 3 can produce:

  • instance-level masks;
  • semantic prompt mask.

These solve complementary representations.

44. SAM 3 Presence Token: Decoupling Recognition from Localization

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous43. SAM 3 Detector: Perception Encoder, Prompt Tokens, Fusion Encoder, DETR Decoder
This chapter44. SAM 3 Presence Token: Decoupling Recognition from Localization
Next45. SAM 3 Tracker and Memory Architecture

This is arguably the most conceptually interesting SAM 3 addition.

Open-vocabulary detection creates a difficult conflict.

An object query needs to answer:

  1. Is the concept anywhere in this image?
  2. Where exactly is one instance?

Those require different context.

44.1 Recognition needs global context

Prompt:

"baseball player"

Understanding whether the concept exists may benefit from:

  • field;
  • uniform;
  • bat;
  • surrounding game context.

Global evidence helps recognition.

44.2 Localization needs local precision

To draw a box, a query must focus on:

  • object boundaries;
  • precise extent;
  • local geometry.

Too much global mixing can hurt exact localization.

44.3 Presence token formulation

SAM 3 introduces a learned global presence token.

It predicts:

P(concept present in image) P(\text{concept present in image})

Call this:

ppresence p_{presence}

Each object proposal/query predicts roughly:

P(qi is a match∣concept is present) P(q_i \text{ is a match} \mid \text{concept is present})

Call that:

pi p_i

Final query score is formed using the presence signal and proposal score.

The paper describes multiplying them.

Conceptually:

scorei=ppresence⋅pi score_i = p_{presence} \cdot p_i

44.4 Example: negative prompt

Prompt:

"giraffe"

Image:

a payment counter with two humans

Without a presence mechanism, some local query might see:

brown vertical structure

and weakly hallucinate a giraffe-like candidate.

Presence head:

global context:
P(giraffe exists) = 0.001

Even if a local query has moderately noisy score:

proposal score = 0.36

combined score becomes tiny.

This helps hard negative concepts.

44.5 Why this is more than another objectness score

Traditional objectness asks:

is there some object here?

Presence token asks:

is this requested concept present anywhere globally?

It is prompt-conditioned recognition.

That distinction is fundamental.

44.6 Architectural lesson for biometric systems

The broader lesson is extremely transferable:

When one prediction head is asked to solve two objectives requiring incompatible context, explicitly decouple the objectives.

Examples:

recognition vs localization
identity vs liveness
retrieval vs final verification
biometric confidence vs payment authorization

Architectural clarity often improves both safety and model performance.

45. SAM 3 Tracker and Memory Architecture

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous44. SAM 3 Presence Token: Decoupling Recognition from Localization
This chapter45. SAM 3 Tracker and Memory Architecture
Next46. YOLO vs DETR vs SAM: What Each Representation Is Really Doing

For video, SAM 3 combines:

  • detector;
  • SAM 2-style tracker;
  • matching;
  • memory;
  • temporal disambiguation.

The paper describes the tracker as inheriting the SAM 2 transformer encoder-decoder architecture.

45.1 First frame

Given prompt:

"customer"

detector finds:

object A
object B

Each detected instance initializes a masklet.

A masklet is a mask trajectory across time:

M_A(t1), M_A(t2), M_A(t3), ...

45.2 Propagation

At each new frame:

previous masklets
      |
tracker + memory
      |
predicted current masks

Call propagated masks:

ℳ̂t \hat{\mathcal{M}}_t

Meanwhile detector independently finds concept objects:

𝒪t \mathcal{O}_t

The system then associates them.

45.3 Matching tracker and detector

SAM 3 uses an IoU-based matching mechanism between:

  • propagated tracker masks;
  • current detector masks.

Why run both?

Tracker has temporal continuity.

Detector can discover new objects or correct drift.

This is a classic tracking architecture principle:

prediction from history
      +
fresh observation
      =
more robust state

45.4 New object spawning

If detector finds an object not matched to an existing track:

new masklet

This allows an object entering the scene later to become tracked.

45.5 Memory encoder

The paper says SAM 3’s tracker includes:

  • prompt encoder;
  • mask decoder;
  • memory encoder;
  • memory bank.

The memory encoder records object appearance/state from past reliable frames.

45.6 Memory attention

Current visual features attend to spatial memory features.

Conceptual directions:

current visual feature -> query
past memory feature    -> key/value

The model asks:

What past information helps identify and segment this object now?

45.7 Two-way mask decoder

The mask decoder performs a two-way transformer interaction between:

  • encoder hidden states;
  • output/prompt tokens.

For ambiguity, multiple candidate masks and confidences can be predicted; the most confident is chosen for tracking.

45.8 Avoid memory poisoning

A tracker can drift.

Suppose:

frame 20:
tracker accidentally jumps from customer A to customer B

If that wrong prediction is stored as memory, future frames reinforce the error.

This is self-contamination.

SAM 3 addresses reliability partly by retaining confidently present frames and periodically using high-confidence detector masks to refresh/re-prompt the tracker.

General lesson:

stateful AI systems need defenses against corrupt state propagation.

This is directly relevant to agent systems, trackers, caches, and recurrent perception.

45.9 Temporal disambiguation

Crowded scenes create collisions.

Two people overlap.

IoU matching for one frame may be ambiguous.

SAM 3 uses temporal consistency signals—such as how consistently a masklet is matched across a temporal window—to suppress unreliable tracks and resolve failures.

Time itself becomes evidence.

45.10 Why SAM 3 still should not identify a payment user

SAM 3 can say:

this is the same "person" track for 80 frames

It still does not securely prove:

this person owns account X

A useful biometric architecture can combine:

SAM/YOLO tracking ID
        +
ArcFace biometric ID

but they must remain separate namespaces.

For example:

session_track_07
   |
face crop across several good frames
   |
aggregate embeddings
   |
verify account 3928

That is a sensible composition.

Connection Bridge — Unifying the Perception Models Before Security Enters

By here you have three distinct ways of asking questions about an image. YOLO-style detectors efficiently map dense multi-scale features to boxes and classes. DETR-style systems use queries and set prediction to reason about object instances. SAM-style systems prioritize promptable masks and, in later versions, concept-conditioned detection and temporal tracking.

The mistake would be to ask which is “best” in the abstract. A payment product should choose the cheapest representation that preserves the information required by the next stage. If a tight aligned face crop is enough, a heavy segmentation foundation model may add latency without reducing risk. If exact foreground separation or robust temporal object masks solve a real failure mode, segmentation may earn its cost. The chapters that follow introduce a new criterion that dominates model elegance: can the system distinguish genuine authorized presence from a plausible visual imitation?

46. YOLO vs DETR vs SAM: What Each Representation Is Really Doing

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous45. SAM 3 Tracker and Memory Architecture
This chapter46. YOLO vs DETR vs SAM: What Each Representation Is Really Doing
Next47. Liveness and Presentation Attack Detection

Let’s strip away brand names.

46.1 YOLO

Representation:

multi-scale spatial feature maps

Prediction mechanism:

dense spatial points
  ->
box regression + class prediction

Typical strength:

  • real-time efficiency;
  • hardware-friendly convolution;
  • mature deployment;
  • strong closed-set detection.

Mental question:

“At these spatial locations/scales, what object is present and what are its boundaries?”

46.2 DETR

Representation:

image tokens/features
+
learned object query slots

Prediction mechanism:

queries attend to image
+
one-to-one set matching

Typical strength:

  • clean end-to-end set formulation;
  • global context;
  • natural removal of traditional anchor/NMS machinery;
  • flexible extensions to segmentation/open vocabulary.

Mental question:

“Each query slot should claim one object from the image set.”

46.3 SAM 1

Representation:

dense reusable image embedding
+
prompt tokens

Prediction:

prompt-conditioned mask

Mental question:

“Given the user’s visual prompt, which pixels belong to the intended object?”

46.4 SAM 2

Adds:

temporal memory

Mental question:

“Given prompt + past object memory, which pixels belong to this same object now?”

46.5 SAM 3

Adds:

concept prompts
DETR-based detector
presence token
exemplar conditioning
memory tracker

Mental question:

“Given this semantic/visual concept, find every matching object, segment them, and preserve instance identities through time.”

46.6 ArcFace system

Representation:

normalized identity embedding on hypersphere

Prediction:

similarity / distance

Mental question:

“How close is this face to the enrolled identity representation?”

46.7 Why SAM 3 is overkill for some biometric pipelines

If the only needed operation is:

find one frontal face in a kiosk frame

then:

SCRFD

may be dramatically simpler.

SAM 3 is valuable when you need:

  • open-vocabulary scene reasoning;
  • exact masks;
  • interactive concept refinement;
  • multi-object video tracking;
  • broad generalization.

Architecture quality means choosing the right abstraction level, not the most impressive model.

46.8 A useful comparison matrix

Model/family Primary representation Output Global context Multi-scale Temporal memory Identity embedding
YOLO26 CNN/attention feature pyramid boxes/classes (+ task heads) selective yes no native biometric memory no
RetinaFace face-specific pyramid face boxes + landmarks limited/implicit yes no no
SCRFD efficient face pyramid face boxes/landmarks limited/implicit yes no no
ResNet + ArcFace deep face feature normalized embedding image-level implicit no yes
DETR image features + object queries set of boxes/classes strong attention variants yes no no
SAM 1 ViT image embedding + prompt masks strong encoded no no
SAM 2 image embedding + memory video masklets strong yes/hierarchical yes no
SAM 3 PE + prompt fusion + queries + memory concept boxes/masks/tracks strong yes yes no

The last column is why you still need a face-recognition model.

Connection Bridge — Recognition Is Not Proof of Presence

This is the security pivot of the book. A recognition model is trained to be tolerant to many visual changes so that two images of the same person map nearby. That very invariance can work against you when an attacker presents a high-quality photo, replay, mask, deepfake feed, or injected frame. The recognition network may correctly recognize the identity represented in the pixels while being completely wrong about whether the real person is present.

Presentation-attack detection therefore adds signals whose job is not identity but capture authenticity: texture, depth, infrared response, temporal physiology, motion consistency, challenge-response, trusted sensor paths, and anti-injection controls. Biometric security is strongest when multiple independent assumptions must fail simultaneously.

47. Liveness and Presentation Attack Detection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous46. YOLO vs DETR vs SAM: What Each Representation Is Really Doing
This chapter47. Liveness and Presentation Attack Detection
Next48. RGB, Depth, IR, Texture, Temporal, and Challenge-Based Liveness

A face recognizer answers a narrow question:

“Does the captured facial appearance resemble the enrolled identity?”

That is not enough for authentication.

A printed photograph of the correct person can contain excellent identity features.

A replayed video can contain blinking, mouth motion, and natural facial expressions.

A 3D mask can reproduce depth.

A digitally injected deepfake can bypass the camera entirely.

This is why biometric systems need Presentation Attack Detection (PAD) and, beyond PAD, broader capture-integrity controls.

Current standards context:

  • ISO/IEC 30107-3:2023 defines principles/methods for performance assessment and reporting of biometric PAD.
  • Current NIST SP 800-63B requires PAD for facial recognition in the authentication context it specifies and emphasizes that biometric characteristics are not secrets.

47.1 Biometric characteristics are not passwords

If your password leaks, you can change it.

If your face leaks:

you cannot rotate your face

A high-resolution image may be obtainable from:

  • social media;
  • CCTV;
  • ID photographs;
  • public events;
  • video calls.

Therefore security must not rely on:

“the attacker cannot obtain what the user looks like.”

The secret/security properties must come from:

  • trusted capture;
  • liveness/PAD;
  • possession/context factors;
  • secure templates;
  • transaction intent;
  • risk controls.

47.2 Presentation attack taxonomy

Examples:

Printed photo

camera <- printed face

Potential cues:

  • paper texture;
  • planar geometry;
  • printing artifacts;
  • no true depth;
  • specular behavior.

Screen replay

camera <- phone/tablet displaying victim video

Potential cues:

  • display pixel grid;
  • refresh/rolling-shutter artifacts;
  • moiré;
  • glass reflections;
  • planar depth;
  • color gamut/frequency clues.

Cutout attack

Printed photo with holes around:

eyes
mouth

allows attacker to introduce real blinking/motion behind the print.

3D mask

Silicone/resin/latex mask.

Potentially defeats simple depth-only checks.

Makeup/disguise

May seek:

  • impersonation;
  • evasion.

Deepfake/re-enactment

Synthetic facial content generated from victim identity.

Camera injection

Attacker bypasses physical presentation and feeds synthetic frames into software pipeline.

The last attack is not solved by visual liveness alone.

47.3 PAD versus liveness

The terms are often used interchangeably.

Liveness detection asks whether biometric evidence appears to originate from a living subject present at capture.

PAD is broader: automated determination of presentation attacks at the biometric capture subsystem.

NIST explicitly defines liveness as a subset of PAD-related determination concepts.

A system can be “live” yet malicious.

Example:

attacker wearing sophisticated impersonation mask

The attacker is alive.

The presentation is still an attack.

47.4 Binary classification is the simplest formulation

Naive anti-spoof network:

face crop
   |
CNN
   |
2 logits
   |
live / spoof

Loss:

L=−ylogp−(1−y)log(1−p) L=-y\log p-(1-y)\log(1-p)

Problem:

The network may learn dataset shortcuts.

For example:

Dataset A live images -> camera model X
Dataset A spoof images -> camera model Y

The model learns camera fingerprint instead of spoof physics.

Then production camera Z breaks it.

This is one of the largest anti-spoofing problems:

domain generalization.

47.5 Spoof cues are often fine-grained

Identity recognition wants invariance to many texture details.

PAD may depend on exactly those details:

  • printing dots;
  • display subpixels;
  • reflection;
  • skin microtexture;
  • shading;
  • local gradients.

This means recognition and liveness objectives can conflict.

Another reason not to simply ask one network to do both without careful multi-task design.

48. RGB, Depth, IR, Texture, Temporal, and Challenge-Based Liveness

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous47. Liveness and Presentation Attack Detection
This chapter48. RGB, Depth, IR, Texture, Temporal, and Challenge-Based Liveness
Next49. Deepfakes, Replay Attacks, Injection Attacks, and Sensor Trust

There is no single perfect PAD signal.

Robust systems combine physically different evidence.

48.1 RGB texture analysis

Single RGB camera is cheapest.

Network learns cues such as:

skin texture
paper texture
screen artifacts
color distortion
moire
reflection patterns

Architectures:

  • ordinary CNN;
  • ResNet;
  • MobileNet;
  • transformer;
  • specialized central-difference networks.

Advantage:

works with commodity camera

Weakness:

domain shift
high-quality displays/prints
unknown attacks
lighting sensitivity

48.2 Central Difference Convolution

A notable anti-spoofing architecture is CDCN — Central Difference Convolutional Network.

Primary source: Searching Central Difference Convolutional Networks for Face Anti-Spoofing.

Ordinary convolution:

y(p0)=∑pn∈Rw(pn)x(p0+pn) y(p_0)= \sum_{p_n\in R} w(p_n)x(p_0+p_n)

Central-difference term considers local differences relative to center:

yCD(p0)=∑pn∈Rw(pn)[x(p0+pn)−x(p0)] y_{CD}(p_0) = \sum_{p_n\in R} w(p_n) [ x(p_0+p_n)-x(p_0) ]

A mixed formulation can blend vanilla intensity and gradient-oriented behavior.

Why?

Gradient-like signals emphasize fine local texture changes.

Printed/display media and real 3D skin interact with light differently.

CDC explicitly gives the architecture an inductive bias toward local difference information.

48.3 Why gradients may generalize better than raw color

Raw RGB:

highly sensitive to illumination
camera white balance
skin tone
exposure

Local differences can capture patterns less dependent on absolute intensity.

Not perfectly invariant—but potentially more robust.

This resembles classical Local Binary Pattern intuition, modernized into learnable convolution.

48.4 Depth-supervised anti-spoofing

A real face is 3D.

A printed photograph is approximately planar.

One training strategy predicts a pseudo-depth map.

For live sample:

nose protrudes
eye sockets recede
cheeks curve

For planar spoof:

depth approximately flat / zero target

Architecture:

RGB face
  |
CNN
  |
pixel-wise depth map
  |
depth supervision

Why use auxiliary depth instead of only binary label?

Because the model is forced to learn geometric clues, not merely dataset correlations.

Research such as Learning Deep Models for Face Anti-Spoofing: Binary or Auxiliary Supervision uses depth and temporal physiological signals as auxiliary supervision.

Primary source: Liu, Jourabloo, Liu.

48.5 Actual depth sensor

Instead of predicting depth from RGB, use hardware:

  • structured light;
  • time-of-flight;
  • stereo;
  • active depth camera.

Now the input may include:

RGB
depth

A printed photo:

flat plane

Real face:

3D geometry

But do not conclude:

depth solves spoofing.

A high-quality 3D mask has depth.

Sensor attacks may manipulate depth data.

Depth becomes one independent signal.

48.6 Near-infrared

NIR imaging captures reflectance outside visible spectrum.

Different materials respond differently.

Possible benefit:

skin vs paper vs display vs mask

can become easier to distinguish.

Multimodal anti-spoof systems often combine:

RGB
Depth
IR

The multi-modal CDCN research explicitly studied these three modalities.

Primary source: Multi-Modal Face Anti-Spoofing Based on Central Difference Networks.

48.7 Fusion architectures

Early fusion

Concatenate modalities near input:

RGB
Depth -> stack -> one network
IR

Problem:

modalities have different physical meaning/scales.

Mid-level fusion

Separate encoders:

RGB -> encoder ---+
Depth -> encoder -+--> feature fusion -> PAD head
IR -> encoder ----+

This lets each modality learn specialized low-level features.

Late fusion

Independent scores:

RGB score
Depth score
IR score
   |
weighted/rule fusion

Advantages:

  • modular;
  • easier diagnostics;
  • can fail gracefully if modality missing.

For security systems, modular observability is valuable.

48.8 Temporal liveness

Video gives motion.

Signals:

Optical/motion cues

3D face motion under viewpoint changes differs from a rigid 2D plane.

Blink

Historically popular but weak alone.

A replay video can blink.

Facial motion consistency

Track landmarks/deformation over time.

rPPG

Remote photoplethysmography seeks subtle skin color variations correlated with blood pulse.

Very simplified:

blood volume changes
 ->
tiny RGB variation
 ->
temporal signal

A printed photo lacks physiological pulse.

A replay video may contain original pulse information, however, and compression/lighting complicate measurement.

No single physiological cue should be treated as unbeatable.

48.9 Active challenge-response

Ask user to:

turn head left
look at dot
blink
smile
move closer

Randomized challenge makes prerecorded replay harder.

System verifies both:

liveness
AND
did action match fresh challenge?

Problem:

  • user friction;
  • accessibility;
  • latency;
  • sophisticated generative attacks may respond in real time.

For low-friction payments, passive PAD is attractive, but high-risk transactions may use step-up challenges.

48.10 Challenge verification architecture

Example:

random challenge generator
        |
"turn head right"
        |
camera video
        |
face tracking
        |
landmark/head-pose estimator
        |
temporal state machine
        |
challenge satisfied?

The challenge must be generated after the authentication session begins.

Otherwise attacker can precompute response.

49. Deepfakes, Replay Attacks, Injection Attacks, and Sensor Trust

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous48. RGB, Depth, IR, Texture, Temporal, and Challenge-Based Liveness
This chapter49. Deepfakes, Replay Attacks, Injection Attacks, and Sensor Trust
Next50. Biometric Enrollment Architecture

Modern biometric security must distinguish two attack planes:

  1. physical presentation plane
  2. digital injection plane

PAD mostly targets the first.

49.1 Physical path

human/artifact
   |
optics
   |
camera sensor
   |
ISP
   |
OS driver
   |
application

A print/screen/mask interacts with optics and sensor.

Visual PAD can inspect those physical traces.

49.2 Injection path

Attacker may compromise:

virtual camera
camera API
application memory
IPC
driver
network stream

and feed:

perfect synthetic video

after the sensor.

Now the PAD model sees pixels with no trustworthy provenance.

Even a brilliant anti-spoof model can fail if its input can be replaced.

49.3 Trusted capture

Security architecture needs assurance that:

frames analyzed

actually came from:

expected sensor
during this transaction

Possible system mechanisms:

  • device attestation;
  • signed capture metadata;
  • secure camera pipeline;
  • hardware-backed keys;
  • Trusted Execution Environment;
  • anti-tamper controls;
  • secure channel between capture endpoint and verifier;
  • nonce/challenge binding.

The exact implementation depends on hardware/platform.

49.4 Bind capture to transaction nonce

Suppose backend creates:

nonce = random 256-bit value
transaction_id = T

The trusted terminal can cryptographically attest:

capture session C
was performed for nonce N
on trusted device D
at time t

and sign evidence.

This helps prevent replay of a previous valid biometric session.

The biometric image itself should not be thought of as the anti-replay token.

49.5 Deepfake detector is not sufficient

A deepfake detector asks:

does this media look synthetic?

But generators change.

Distribution shifts.

Attackers optimize against detectors.

A secure architecture layers:

trusted capture
+
PAD
+
fresh challenge/context
+
identity matching
+
risk engine

not:

one deepfake classifier

49.6 Presentation Attack Instruments

Standards use terminology such as Presentation Attack Instrument (PAI) for artifacts used to perform presentation attacks.

Examples:

printed image
display
mask
prosthetic

Testing must include diverse attack instruments.

If evaluation uses:

one printer
one phone
one lighting setup

the reported PAD result says little about broad attack resilience.

49.7 PAD metrics

Common metrics include concepts such as:

  • APCER — Attack Presentation Classification Error Rate;
  • BPCER — Bona fide Presentation Classification Error Rate;
  • ACER — average of attack/bona-fide error in some research protocols;
  • IAPAR in certain standards/guidance contexts.

The exact definitions/protocols matter.

Do not compare PAD papers only by one headline number if:

  • datasets differ;
  • attack species differ;
  • train/test domains differ;
  • sensors differ.

49.8 Cross-dataset testing is especially important

Train:

Dataset A

Test:

Dataset B

This exposes shortcut learning.

Intra-dataset:

same cameras
same collection process
same spoof media

can make results look unrealistically good.

A payment system must test:

  • unseen phones/screens;
  • unseen printers;
  • masks;
  • lighting;
  • camera replacements;
  • demographic diversity;
  • adversarially selected attacks.

49.9 ISO/IEC 30107-3 is about testing/reporting, not one algorithm

A common misconception:

“ISO 30107 means use algorithm X.”

No.

ISO/IEC 30107-3:2023 establishes principles/methods for PAD performance assessment and reporting and attack classification.

It does not prescribe one CNN or sensor.

That distinction matters when building regulatory evidence.

49.10 NIST’s security framing

Current NIST SP 800-63B emphasizes:

  • biometric characteristics are not secrets;
  • facial recognition should use PAD in its authentication framework;
  • biometric data requires sensitive-data protection;
  • explicit authentication intent matters;
  • biometrics are used with another factor in its specified assurance framework.

Even when not legally binding for a deployment, this is a valuable design warning:

Face similarity alone is a weak basis for authorizing money movement.

Connection Bridge — From Model Outputs to an Identity Lifecycle

Before verification can work, the system needs something trustworthy to compare against. Enrollment is not “save one selfie.” It is the ceremony that binds biometric evidence to an account, establishes template quality, records policy and consent, and creates the long-lived reference against which later transactions are evaluated.

This is also where biometric systems become fundamentally different from ordinary classifiers: the stored artifact is security-sensitive and difficult to revoke because a face is not a password. The enrollment, verification, risk, cryptography, and privacy chapters should therefore be read as one lifecycle rather than separate backend topics.

50. Biometric Enrollment Architecture

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous49. Deepfakes, Replay Attacks, Injection Attacks, and Sensor Trust
This chapter50. Biometric Enrollment Architecture
Next51. Biometric Payment Verification Architecture

A face-payment system does not begin when somebody looks at a checkout camera. It begins much earlier, during enrollment.

Enrollment is the ceremony that establishes a binding between:

real-world person
      │
      ▼
verified account identity
      │
      ▼
biometric observations
      │
      ▼
reference biometric template

This is one of the most security-critical moments in the whole lifecycle.

If enrollment binds the wrong face to an account, perfect recognition during payment merely authenticates the wrong binding very reliably.

That is why the following two questions must be separated:

  1. Identity proofing: who is this person in the account/legal sense?
  2. Biometric enrollment: what biometric representation should represent that verified person later?

A high-quality ArcFace model cannot solve weak identity proofing.

50.1 Enrollment is not simply camera -> embedding -> database

A naive implementation might be:

camera
  │
  ▼
face image
  │
  ▼
ArcFace network
  │
  ▼
512-D vector
  │
  ▼
database

A production enrollment pipeline is closer to:

IDENTITY-PROOFED ACCOUNT
          │
          ▼
    enrollment session
          │
          ├────────────── session nonce / anti-replay state
          │
          ▼
     trusted capture
          │
          ▼
    face detection
          │
          ▼
     quality gates
          │
          ├── sufficient resolution?
          ├── blur acceptable?
          ├── pose acceptable?
          ├── illumination acceptable?
          ├── face unobstructed?
          └── exactly expected subject count?
          │
          ▼
    presentation-attack detection
          │
          ▼
     landmark alignment
          │
          ▼
     several embeddings
          │
          ▼
 quality-weighted aggregation
          │
          ▼
 reference biometric template
          │
          ▼
 encryption + version metadata
          │
          ▼
  biometric template store

The template should therefore be understood as the result of a controlled measurement process, not as a vector casually extracted from one selfie.

50.2 Why one enrollment photo is fragile

Imagine one photo happens to contain:

  • slight motion blur;
  • 25° yaw;
  • glasses glare;
  • dim light;
  • a partially closed eye.

The network might still generate an embedding, but that does not mean the embedding is the best stable representation of the person’s face.

A better enrollment captures a short sequence:

frame 01 -> quality 0.71
frame 02 -> quality 0.92
frame 03 -> quality 0.89
frame 04 -> quality 0.45  reject
frame 05 -> quality 0.94
frame 06 -> quality 0.90
...

Then the system can keep several high-quality, sufficiently diverse observations.

This reduces the chance that the reference template encodes an accidental nuisance condition rather than identity.

50.3 Multi-frame template aggregation

Suppose the recognition model emits L2-normalized embeddings:

e1,e2,…,ek,∥ei∥2=1 e_1,e_2,\ldots,e_k, \qquad \|e_i\|_2=1

A simple template can be produced by averaging and renormalizing:

e‾=1k∑i=1kei \bar e = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_i

t=e‾∥e‾∥2 t = \frac{\bar e}{\|\bar e\|_2}

A quality-weighted version is:

e‾=∑iqiei∑iqi \bar e = \frac{\sum_i q_i e_i}{\sum_i q_i}

where qiq_i is a quality score.

This has an intuitive interpretation:

several noisy measurements
          │
          ▼
average away incidental variation
          │
          ▼
more stable identity direction

But do not blindly average consecutive frames that are almost identical. Ten copies of one bad pose are not ten independent identity measurements.

A sensible capture policy tries to obtain some diversity while staying inside acceptable operating conditions.

50.4 Face-image-quality assessment is its own model/problem

Quality is not the same as recognition confidence.

A quality estimator asks something closer to:

Is this sample likely to be useful for reliable recognition?

Potential quality dimensions include:

resolution
blur
exposure
illumination uniformity
pose
occlusion
compression artifacts
face size
landmark confidence
gaze / eye state depending on policy

A system can have:

recognition embedding generated successfully

while still deciding:

sample quality too poor -> ask for recapture

This is important because neural networks nearly always return something. The existence of an output should not be confused with the validity of the measurement.

50.5 Enrollment PAD is at least as important as transaction PAD

Suppose an attacker can enroll a photograph or synthetic face under an account.

Later, transaction-time liveness may be irrelevant because the attacker has corrupted the reference itself.

Hence:

ENROLLMENT
  identity proofing
  +
  trustworthy capture
  +
  PAD

TRANSACTION
  biometric verification
  +
  PAD

are both important.

This is another example of failure containment: security cannot rest on one liveness classifier at checkout.

50.6 Duplicate enrollment detection

A payment provider may want to detect whether one biometric identity has been enrolled under several accounts.

That changes the problem from 1:1 verification to a controlled form of 1:N search:

new enrollment embedding
         │
         ▼
search existing enrollment gallery
         │
         ▼
possible duplicate candidates

This should generally be treated as a separate risk/review process rather than silently equating nearest-neighbor similarity with proven duplicate identity.

Why?

Because large-gallery searches create different statistical behavior from 1:1 verification.

A candidate retrieval stage might be:

ANN index
   │
   ▼
top-K candidates
   │
   ▼
exact similarity / stronger comparison
   │
   ▼
policy / review

The ANN index retrieves candidates; it should not itself be treated as the final identity verdict.

50.7 Template versioning

Your biometric representation depends on a model version.

Suppose you enroll using:

FaceModel v1
embedding dimension = 512
preprocessing = alignment recipe A

Two years later you deploy:

FaceModel v2
embedding dimension = 512
preprocessing = alignment recipe B

Even when dimensions happen to match, vectors from different models are generally not guaranteed to inhabit the same embedding space.

Therefore a template record should carry metadata such as:

template_id
subject_id_reference
model_family
model_version
preprocessing_version
embedding_dimension
enrollment_timestamp
quality metadata
sensor class
key/encryption version
template status

Do not assume:

512 numbers from model A
=
512 numbers from model B

They are coordinates relative to learned spaces, not universal biometric coordinates.

50.8 Re-enrollment and migration

Model upgrades create an uncomfortable question:

How do we migrate millions of enrolled templates?

If raw enrollment images were deliberately not retained for privacy/security reasons, you cannot simply rerun the new network over old images.

Possible strategies include:

  • dual-model transition during successful future authentications;
  • explicit re-enrollment;
  • maintaining old and new verification services during a migration window;
  • carefully researched cross-model compatibility techniques, if validated for the use case.

There is no universal magic transform from an arbitrary old embedding space into a new one.

This lifecycle issue should be designed before deployment.

50.9 A biometric embedding is still sensitive biometric data

A frequent architectural mistake is:

“We don’t store photos, only vectors, therefore it is no longer biometric data.”

That is not a safe assumption.

The vector exists specifically to represent identity-related biological characteristics and to enable matching. It therefore deserves biometric-grade protection.

In Indonesia, Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection explicitly categorizes biometric data as specific personal data. Its explanation includes physical, physiological, or behavioral characteristics enabling unique identification, such as facial imagery, fingerprints, and retinal characteristics.

For an Indonesian payment-system design, this should affect:

  • purpose limitation;
  • access control;
  • retention;
  • auditability;
  • breach impact analysis;
  • system separation;
  • consent/legal-basis analysis with counsel;
  • incident response.

This document is an engineering reference, not legal advice, but the engineering architecture should be created with these constraints from the beginning rather than retrofitted after the model works.

51. Biometric Payment Verification Architecture

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous50. Biometric Enrollment Architecture
This chapter51. Biometric Payment Verification Architecture
Next52. Risk Engine and Payment Authorization: AI Confidence Is Not Authorization

Now assume a person is properly enrolled.

The payment transaction should still not be modeled as:

camera -> face model -> pay

Instead we should explicitly represent transaction intent, identity claim, biometric evidence, risk, and payment authorization.

51.1 A strong default: 1:1 verification, not blind 1:N identification

Consider two user experiences.

Experience A — blind identification

customer stands in front of terminal
            │
            ▼
        capture face
            │
            ▼
search 20,000,000 enrolled users
            │
            ▼
nearest candidate = Razi
            │
            ▼
         payment

Experience B — claimed account + verification

customer presents/scans account token
            │
            ▼
      account = Razi
            │
            ▼
        capture face
            │
            ▼
compare against Razi reference
            │
            ▼
     verification decision

The second is normally the cleaner security primitive because it asks:

Does this face match this claimed identity?

rather than:

Among everyone we know, who is this probably closest to?

The first can be built, but it requires open-set identification engineering, much more careful large-gallery calibration, candidate ambiguity handling, and stronger protections against false identification.

51.2 The payment request should have cryptographic freshness

The biometric observation should be associated with a specific current transaction, not merely with an account.

Conceptually:

transaction_id
amount
merchant_id
terminal_id
nonce
creation_time
expiry

become part of a transaction context.

The biometric service then answers within that context:

"biometric verification for transaction T succeeded"

rather than producing a reusable generic message:

"Razi was verified sometime"

The latter invites replay and context substitution.

51.3 Full transaction sequence

A simplified flow:

Customer       Terminal       Biometric Edge      Biometric Backend      Risk Engine      Payment Core
   │              │                 │                    │                    │                │
   │ initiate     │                 │                    │                    │                │
   ├─────────────>│                 │                    │                    │                │
   │              │ create tx       │                    │                    │                │
   │              ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────>│
   │              │<──────────────────── transaction ID / challenge / nonce ──────────────────┤
   │              │                 │                    │                    │                │
   │ look camera  │                 │                    │                    │                │
   │─────────────>│ capture         │                    │                    │                │
   │              ├────────────────>│                    │                    │                │
   │              │                 │ detect/quality/PAD │                    │                │
   │              │                 │ align/embed        │                    │                │
   │              │                 ├───────────────────>│                    │                │
   │              │                 │                    │ match template     │                │
   │              │                 │                    ├───────────────────>│                │
   │              │                 │                    │ bio evidence       │                │
   │              │                 │                    │ + device/tx state  │                │
   │              │                 │                    │                    ├───────────────>│
   │              │                 │                    │                    │ auth decision   │
   │              │<───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │ result       │                 │                    │                    │                │
   │<─────────────│                 │                    │                    │                │

The exact service boundaries can differ, but the conceptual separation matters.

51.4 Capture should be a short temporal process

Instead of betting the transaction on one frame:

frame 1 -> detect -> align -> quality
frame 2 -> detect -> align -> quality
frame 3 -> detect -> align -> quality
...

Track the same face across a short window.

Then require conditions such as:

stable subject tracking
sufficient high-quality frames
consistent liveness evidence
consistent identity embeddings
no unexpected face switching

This converts the problem from:

classify one picture

into:

accumulate mutually consistent evidence over a short physical interaction.

That is generally much stronger.

51.5 Why tracking is useful even when SAM 3 is not used

For a kiosk, you probably do not need a huge foundation segmentation model just to maintain face identity across frames.

A lightweight tracker can associate detections using:

  • bounding-box IoU;
  • motion prediction;
  • facial embeddings;
  • landmarks;
  • temporal continuity.

The role of tracking is not legal identity.

It provides an ephemeral session identity:

track_id = 42

meaning:

this sequence of detections appears to correspond to the same physical subject during this session.

That track can then own the accumulated:

quality history
PAD evidence
recognition embeddings
pose history

51.6 Multi-frame identity consistency

For high-quality accepted frames, compute embeddings:

e1, e2, e3, e4, e5

Then check not only similarity to the enrolled template but also mutual consistency.

If:

e1 ~ enrollment = high
e2 ~ enrollment = high
e3 ~ enrollment = high
e4 ~ enrollment = very low
e5 ~ enrollment = high

there may be:

  • a tracking switch;
  • severe motion/occlusion;
  • injection artifact;
  • transient quality failure.

A robust system does not blindly average every vector that arrived.

51.7 Verification scoring

For normalized query qq and reference rr:

s=qTr s=q^Tr

because with unit vectors cosine similarity reduces to a dot product.

Decision:

s≥τ⇒candidate biometric match s \ge \tau \Rightarrow \text{candidate biometric match}

But this should be thought of as:

biometric evidence = {
    similarity: s,
    threshold_profile: version,
    sample_quality: q,
    PAD_result: ...,
    capture_integrity: ...,
    model_version: ...
}

not just:

is_match = true

The richer evidence allows policy and audit layers to reason about why a transaction was accepted.

51.8 The threshold must be calibrated on the deployment population

A threshold copied from a GitHub README is not a payment-security threshold.

Model scores depend on:

  • recognition architecture;
  • training data;
  • sensor;
  • preprocessing;
  • lighting;
  • demographic distribution;
  • pose;
  • gallery/reference quality;
  • compression;
  • model quantization;
  • operational attack environment.

Therefore calibration should use representative validation data and target an explicit operating point.

For example, if the security requirement chooses a maximum false-match rate, select τ\tau from measured score distributions to satisfy that target while observing the resulting false-non-match cost.

There is no threshold that simultaneously maximizes security and convenience without trade-offs.

51.9 Similarity is not probability

Suppose cosine similarity is:

0.74

It is incorrect to report:

"74% chance this is Razi"

Cosine similarity is a geometric score in the model’s embedding space.

If a system wants a probability-like calibrated score, it needs an explicitly validated calibration model under a defined population and operating condition.

Even then, communicating it as a universal probability can be misleading when data distribution shifts.

51.10 Open-set identification is different mathematics operationally

In 1:N search, the system compares a probe against many templates:

si=qTri,i=1,…,N s_i=q^Tr_i, \qquad i=1,\ldots,N

and looks at:

maxisi \max_i s_i

As NN grows, the behavior of the maximum impostor score matters.

Even if each individual impostor comparison has a tiny chance of exceeding a threshold, a large gallery supplies many opportunities for a spurious high score.

That is why large-scale identification should be evaluated using open-set identification metrics rather than reusing only 1:1 verification metrics.

Connection Bridge — The Most Important Boundary in the Entire Architecture

Everything before this point produces evidence. Nothing before this point should directly move money. Detection confidence, liveness score, embedding similarity, device reputation, transaction amount, velocity, and account context are measurements with uncertainty. The risk engine combines them under policy; authorization then applies the financial and cryptographic rules that decide whether the transaction is valid.

This separation is how you contain model failure. If recognition quality degrades under unusual lighting, the correct system response is not necessarily a false approval or hard denial. It may lower trust, request a second factor, fall back to another payment path, or refuse biometric authorization while leaving the account usable. AI should influence the decision; it should not silently become the decision.

52. Risk Engine and Payment Authorization: AI Confidence Is Not Authorization

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous51. Biometric Payment Verification Architecture
This chapter52. Risk Engine and Payment Authorization: AI Confidence Is Not Authorization
Next53. Edge vs Cloud Inference

This is perhaps the most important systems-design principle in this document.

A recognition model is an uncertain sensor.

It should not itself own the policy:

ArcFace match -> transfer money

Instead:

biometric evidence
      │
      ├── recognition score
      ├── PAD score/result
      ├── sample quality
      ├── capture integrity
      └── temporal consistency
               │
               ▼
          RISK / AUTH POLICY
               │
        ┌──────┼───────┐
        ▼      ▼       ▼
      allow  step-up  deny

52.1 Why separate risk from recognition?

Because recognition answers a narrow question:

How similar are these biometric representations under this model?

Payment authorization asks a much broader question:

Is there sufficient trustworthy evidence to authorize this particular financial action under current policy?

Those are not the same function.

52.2 Inputs to a transaction risk decision

Depending on product and regulation, risk evidence might include:

biometric similarity
PAD outcome
capture/sensor integrity
transaction amount
merchant identity
terminal/device trust
account status
transaction velocity
recent suspicious activity
location consistency where lawful/relevant
challenge freshness
prior failed attempts
model/service health

The point is not to create a mysterious mega-model.

The point is to make authorization depend on multiple independently useful signals, with explicit policy.

52.3 Step-up authentication

A useful policy pattern is:

high confidence + normal risk
          │
          ▼
        approve

uncertain biometric OR elevated transaction risk
          │
          ▼
  request additional factor

strong spoof/integrity evidence
          │
          ▼
          deny

Additional factors might include an authenticated device interaction, PIN, secure app confirmation, or another approved authentication mechanism depending on the deployment design.

This is safer than trying to make one face threshold solve every risk level.

52.4 Thresholds can be policy-dependent—but must remain measurable

It is possible to use different decision policies for different risk classes, but this easily becomes dangerous if done informally.

Every threshold profile should be:

versioned
measured
reviewed
reproducible
auditable

Example metadata:

policy_id: biometric-payment-v7
recognition_threshold_profile: arcface-r100-terminalA-v3
pad_policy: rgbir-pad-v4
step_up_policy: tx-risk-v5

A future incident investigation should be able to reconstruct the logic that was active for a particular transaction.

52.5 Model confidence is not system confidence

You might receive:

face detector confidence = 0.99
recognition cosine = 0.81
PAD classifier = 0.97 live

That still does not imply:

system confidence = 99%

Those outputs are produced by different models with different meanings and calibrations.

Do not multiply them, average them, or call the result “confidence” without a carefully validated probabilistic model.

Often the safer engineering approach is explicit gating and policy:

capture integrity MUST pass
quality MUST pass
PAD MUST pass
recognition MUST exceed calibrated threshold
transaction risk MUST satisfy policy

with well-defined fallback paths.

53. Edge vs Cloud Inference

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous52. Risk Engine and Payment Authorization: AI Confidence Is Not Authorization
This chapter53. Edge vs Cloud Inference
Next54. Template Security, Cryptography, HSMs, TEEs, and Privacy

A biometric-payment architecture immediately creates an infrastructure question:

Which computations run on the terminal/device, and which run in a server environment?

There is no single universal answer.

53.1 All-cloud architecture

camera
  │
  ▼
raw/video frames
  │ network
  ▼
cloud detection + PAD + recognition
  │
  ▼
matching

Advantages

  • centralized model deployment;
  • easier GPU provisioning;
  • simpler fleet model-version control;
  • more compute for large models.

Problems

  • raw biometric imagery traverses the network;
  • bandwidth and latency;
  • degraded/offline behavior;
  • larger privacy exposure;
  • server becomes a rich attack target;
  • camera-to-cloud injection path becomes critical.

53.2 All-edge architecture

camera
  │
  ▼
terminal inference
  │
  ├── detection
  ├── PAD
  ├── recognition
  └── local matching

Advantages

  • low latency;
  • potentially less raw biometric data leaves device;
  • reduced network dependence;
  • sensor/inference can be tightly coupled.

Problems

  • constrained compute;
  • key/template distribution becomes harder;
  • terminal compromise has severe implications;
  • fleet patching/model governance;
  • model extraction/reverse engineering;
  • device heterogeneity.

53.3 Hybrid architecture

A common design direction is:

TRUSTED TERMINAL
camera
  │
  ▼
quality + PAD + alignment + embedding
  │
  ▼
protected embedding + attested session evidence
  │
  │ encrypted channel
  ▼
SERVER
reference retrieval + matching + policy + audit

This can reduce raw-image exposure while retaining centralized reference control.

But note the assumption hidden inside it:

You must trust that the embedding really came from the expected camera and approved inference pipeline.

That leads directly to capture integrity and device attestation.

53.4 Secure capture path

For payment, consider the entire chain:

physical photons
     │
     ▼
camera sensor
     │
     ▼
driver / ISP
     │
     ▼
OS media stack
     │
     ▼
inference process
     │
     ▼
biometric result

If malware can inject arbitrary frames between the camera and inference service, a fantastic liveness model may simply be evaluating attacker-controlled video.

Thus the question becomes:

How do we establish provenance of captured frames?

Potential controls depend on hardware/platform but can include:

  • trusted execution environments;
  • hardware-backed keys;
  • secure boot;
  • signed application/runtime;
  • device attestation;
  • restricted camera pipeline;
  • anti-tamper measures;
  • nonce-bound session evidence.

This is why biometric-payment security is not only computer vision.

53.5 Why a TEE is interesting

A Trusted Execution Environment attempts to isolate security-sensitive computation from the ordinary operating environment.

Conceptually:

Normal OS
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│ UI / networking / applications   │
│                                  │
│   ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
│   │ isolated trusted region  │   │
│   │                          │   │
│   │ keys                     │   │
│   │ biometric operations     │   │
│   │ protected state          │   │
│   └──────────────────────────┘   │
└──────────────────────────────────┘

A TEE does not magically make software bug-free, but it can reduce the portion of the system that must be trusted.

For high-value systems, minimizing the trusted computing base is valuable.

53.6 Quantization and edge deployment

A large FP32 recognition model may be too expensive for a kiosk/mobile accelerator.

Quantization can represent weights/activations using lower precision, such as INT8.

Rough intuition:

FP32: 32 bits/value
FP16: 16 bits/value
INT8:  8 bits/value

Benefits can include:

  • smaller model;
  • lower memory bandwidth;
  • faster inference on supported accelerators;
  • lower power.

But biometric accuracy must be re-evaluated after conversion.

You cannot assume:

same architecture + INT8 = same biometric score distribution

Quantization may slightly shift embeddings or PAD scores, which can affect a tight operating threshold.

Therefore calibration belongs to the deployed artifact, not merely the original PyTorch checkpoint.

53.7 Runtime options

Depending on target hardware, deployment might involve:

PyTorch -> ONNX -> TensorRT
PyTorch -> Core ML
PyTorch -> TFLite / LiteRT
PyTorch -> OpenVINO
PyTorch -> vendor NPU compiler

The exact framework is secondary to validating:

  • numerical parity;
  • preprocessing parity;
  • throughput;
  • latency distribution;
  • thermal throttling;
  • memory pressure;
  • failure behavior.

A model that benchmarks at 12 ms in a warm lab test may behave differently after ten minutes of sustained kiosk operation.

54. Template Security, Cryptography, HSMs, TEEs, and Privacy

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous53. Edge vs Cloud Inference
This chapter54. Template Security, Cryptography, HSMs, TEEs, and Privacy
Next55. Failure Containment and Threat Modeling

Passwords can be reset.

Faces cannot be conveniently replaced.

That makes biometric-template compromise qualitatively different from password compromise.

54.1 Do not treat an embedding as a password hash

A password system can approximately do:

password
  │
  ▼
slow salted one-way KDF
  │
  ▼
verifier

Face matching instead requires preserving enough similarity structure to compare future measurements.

face capture A -> embedding A
face capture B -> embedding B

similarity(A,B) must remain meaningful

That means the template cannot simply be replaced by an ordinary cryptographic hash:

SHA256(embedding)

because even tiny differences in an embedding produce unrelated cryptographic hashes.

Biometric template protection is therefore its own research/security domain.

54.2 Basic storage protection

At minimum, think in layers:

application authorization
        │
        ▼
biometric service API
        │
        ▼
access-controlled template service
        │
        ▼
encrypted database
        │
        ▼
keys protected separately / HSM where appropriate

Principles:

  • encrypt at rest;
  • encrypt in transit;
  • separate encryption keys from encrypted records;
  • rotate keys with explicit versions;
  • minimize services able to decrypt templates;
  • use strong service authentication;
  • audit every access;
  • partition identity metadata and biometric data where practical;
  • enforce retention/deletion policies.

54.3 HSMs

A Hardware Security Module is specialized hardware for protecting and using cryptographic keys.

Rather than distributing master keys to ordinary application processes:

application
   │ request cryptographic operation
   ▼
HSM
   │
   └── master key remains protected inside boundary

An HSM is not a biometric matcher by definition. Its relevance is key custody, signing, encryption, and trust infrastructure around the biometric/payment system.

54.4 Separate subject identity from template storage

Instead of a database row like:

name: Razi
email: ...
account: ...
face_embedding: [...]

a more defensive design can use indirection:

ACCOUNT SERVICE
account_id -> biometric_subject_token

BIOMETRIC STORE
biometric_subject_token -> encrypted template

The goal is to reduce the value of any single compromised datastore and make cross-domain access explicit.

It does not remove all privacy risk, but it improves containment.

54.5 Template access should be narrow

Ask:

Which services actually need raw decrypted embeddings?

Usually far fewer than initially assumed.

A useful architecture has one narrowly scoped matching service and does not allow every backend engineer/service to query arbitrary templates.

For example:

payment orchestrator
      │
      │ verify(subject_token, probe)
      ▼
biometric matcher
      │
      ├── fetch/decrypt reference internally
      ├── compare
      └── return controlled result/evidence

rather than:

payment orchestrator -> dump template
analytics service     -> dump template
support tool          -> dump template
fraud notebook        -> dump template
...

54.6 Cancellable biometrics and protected-domain representations

Research literature explores methods that transform biometric features into protected representations that can potentially be revoked/reissued or matched without exposing an ordinary template directly.

Important categories include ideas such as:

  • cancellable biometric transforms;
  • biometric cryptosystems;
  • secure multiparty computation;
  • homomorphic-encryption-assisted matching;
  • trusted-hardware matching.

These methods have different assumptions and trade-offs.

Do not adopt a scheme merely because it says “encrypted embeddings.” Ask:

What leakage remains?
Can matching accuracy be maintained?
Can records be cross-linked?
What happens if transformation keys leak?
Is revocability genuine?
What is the computational cost?
What attacks have been studied?
Has the scheme been independently reviewed?

For a payment system, cryptographic design deserves specialist review.

54.7 Do embeddings reveal anything about the face?

It is unsafe to assume embeddings are semantically opaque just because humans cannot look at 512 floating-point values and see a face.

Research has repeatedly explored inference and reconstruction attacks against learned representations.

Therefore the practical security stance should be:

Treat face embeddings as valuable biometric secrets/sensitive data even if exact reconstruction is believed difficult for a particular model.

Security should not depend on “attackers probably can’t understand the vector.”

54.8 Retention and raw frames

A privacy-minimizing architecture should ask whether it needs to retain transaction-time frames at all.

Possible categories:

ephemeral frames used only in memory
short-retention evidence for disputes/security
long-term raw biometric archive

Each step dramatically changes breach impact and governance.

Debugging convenience is not automatically a sufficient reason to create a permanent face-video warehouse.

If examples are needed for model improvement, a separate governed data pipeline should define purpose, access, consent/legal basis, retention, de-identification limitations, and deletion.

55. Failure Containment and Threat Modeling

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous54. Template Security, Cryptography, HSMs, TEEs, and Privacy
This chapter55. Failure Containment and Threat Modeling
Next56. Metrics and Evaluation for a Real Biometric Payment System

A threat model asks:

What are we protecting, from whom, through which attack surfaces, and what happens when one control fails?

This should happen before model selection is declared finished.

55.1 Assets

Potential high-value assets include:

biometric templates
raw enrollment media
raw transaction media
account-to-template bindings
cryptographic keys
model weights
threshold/policy configuration
payment authorization credentials
terminal identities
logs/audit records

An attacker does not necessarily need to “break ArcFace.”

They may attack any of these instead.

55.2 Attack surface map

                       ATTACK SURFACE
                             │
     ┌───────────────┬───────┼────────┬────────────────┐
     ▼               ▼       ▼        ▼                ▼
 physical         capture   model   backend          operations
 presentation     injection inference APIs          /insiders
     │               │       │        │                │
 photo/replay      virtual   evasion template       threshold
 mask/deepfake     camera    attacks theft          changes
 sensor tamper     stream    model    auth bypass   data export
                  replace   extract  API abuse      log tamper

A mature design puts controls around all branches.

55.3 Presentation attack

Attacker presents something to the legitimate sensor:

printed face
phone replay
high-resolution display
3D mask
partial mask
makeup/prosthetic manipulation

Primary defenses include PAD, sensor modality, capture protocol, transaction context, and risk controls.

55.4 Injection attack

Attacker bypasses physical presentation and inserts digital content into the pipeline:

camera
  X
  │
attacker injects frames here
  ▼
inference

This can defeat PAD systems that assume all input came from a genuine sensor.

Defenses therefore require capture-path trust, not just visual classification.

55.5 Replay at the protocol layer

Even with valid biometric evidence, an attacker might try to replay a previously successful authentication result.

Therefore successful biometric evidence should be bound to:

specific transaction
specific challenge/nonce
short validity window
specific terminal/session

and preferably authenticated cryptographically.

55.6 Template theft

If attacker obtains templates, consequences may include:

  • privacy harm;
  • offline similarity experimentation;
  • cross-system linkage depending on model/commonality;
  • attempts to generate presentation/injection inputs;
  • inability to simply “change your face password.”

Hence template protection deserves a security tier comparable to other high-value authentication data.

55.7 Account-template binding attack

Perhaps the template is secure, but an attacker changes:

account A -> template A

to:

account A -> attacker template

No face-model attack is required.

Therefore the binding itself requires integrity, authorization, audit, and protected administrative processes.

55.8 Threshold/configuration attack

Suppose production threshold is:

0.63

and an attacker/accident changes it to:

0.10

The model weights remain untouched but security collapses.

Treat model/policy configuration as security-sensitive code:

  • version control;
  • code review;
  • signed deployment artifacts where appropriate;
  • separation of duties;
  • audit trail;
  • rollback capability;
  • automated sanity tests.

55.9 Model substitution

A terminal might claim it is running:

approved-pad-v4

while actually running:

test-model-with-pad-disabled

Model/runtime attestation or trusted deployment controls can help constrain this.

Again: model identity is part of the trust chain.

55.10 Adversarial examples

Neural networks can be sensitive to carefully optimized perturbations.

For a practical payment system, the useful stance is neither:

adversarial ML makes computer vision impossible

nor:

adversarial examples only happen in papers.

Instead:

  • include adversarial/red-team testing;
  • maintain sensor/PAD diversity where justified;
  • constrain inputs;
  • monitor anomalous score patterns;
  • avoid single-model authorization;
  • keep step-up paths.

The entire system should degrade safely when the perception stack is uncertain.

55.11 Denial of service

Security is not only unauthorized payment.

An attacker could intentionally cause:

false PAD failures
camera saturation
face crowding
occlusion
compute exhaustion
request floods

which prevents legitimate transactions.

Availability engineering therefore belongs in the threat model.

55.12 Insider risk

Privileged staff may have access to:

templates
raw media
thresholds
models
administrative account binding
logs

Controls can include:

  • least privilege;
  • strong administrative authentication;
  • dual control for sensitive changes;
  • immutable/tamper-evident logs;
  • alerting on unusual biometric-data access;
  • time-bounded privileges;
  • production data isolation from research environments.

55.13 Failure containment architecture

The system should assume components can fail.

For example:

face detector fails
      │
      ▼
no transaction; recapture/fallback

PAD unavailable
      │
      ▼
no silent bypass; step-up/fallback

recognition service unavailable
      │
      ▼
alternative approved payment/auth route

risk engine timeout
      │
      ▼
fail according to explicit policy, not arbitrary allow

A dangerous anti-pattern is:

try:
    run_security_check()
except:
    return APPROVED

Financial security systems need deliberately specified degraded modes.

56. Metrics and Evaluation for a Real Biometric Payment System

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous55. Failure Containment and Threat Modeling
This chapter56. Metrics and Evaluation for a Real Biometric Payment System
Next57. A Reference Production Architecture

One of the easiest ways to fool yourself in biometric R&D is to report one impressive accuracy number.

A production system is a chain:

camera
  -> detector
  -> landmarks/alignment
  -> quality gate
  -> PAD
  -> recognition
  -> matching
  -> risk policy
  -> transaction outcome

Every stage needs its own metrics, and the combined system needs end-to-end evaluation.

56.1 Detection metrics

For a face detector, typical concepts include:

  • precision: of predicted faces, how many are real faces?
  • recall: of real faces, how many were detected?
  • IoU: how well does the predicted box overlap ground truth?
  • AP / mAP: area under precision-recall behavior under defined IoU criteria.

For biometric capture, recall under difficult operational conditions can matter more than leaderboard mAP alone.

A detector that performs wonderfully on normal web images but misses faces under kiosk lighting is not the detector you need.

56.2 Landmark/alignment metrics

Landmark accuracy is often evaluated using normalized landmark error.

Very roughly:

NME=1K∑i=1K∥pi−p̂i∥2d NME = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^K \frac{\|p_i-\hat p_i\|_2}{d}

where:

  • pip_i is true landmark;
  • p̂i\hat p_i is predicted landmark;
  • dd is a normalization distance such as inter-ocular distance or face-box scale, depending on benchmark.

Why care?

Because recognition receives the aligned crop, not the original camera frame.

Alignment errors become input errors for the recognizer.

56.3 Verification metrics

For 1:1 biometric verification there are two fundamental error types.

False Match

Two different people are accepted as a match.

A != B
but
system says MATCH

False Non-Match

The same person is rejected.

A == A
but
system says NO MATCH

Common terminology includes:

  • FMR: False Match Rate;
  • FNMR: False Non-Match Rate;
  • TAR: True Accept Rate;
  • FAR is also frequently used informally/within various evaluation traditions for false acceptance; always define exactly what your report means.

A useful reporting form is:

TAR @ FMR = 1e-4
TAR @ FMR = 1e-5
TAR @ FMR = 1e-6

rather than simply:

accuracy = 99.7%

Payment security cares deeply about the low-false-match region of the score distribution.

56.4 ROC curves

A receiver operating characteristic curve varies threshold τ\tau and plots trade-offs.

At a loose threshold:

more genuine users accepted
but
more impostors accepted

At a strict threshold:

fewer impostors accepted
but
more genuine users rejected

There is no threshold-free deployment.

Even if an ML paper reports a threshold-independent metric, the production system eventually has to decide something.

56.5 DET curves

Detection Error Tradeoff curves are common in biometric evaluation.

They directly emphasize the trade-off between false-match/accept and false-non-match/reject behavior.

They are useful because the operational question is often:

At the security error rate we can tolerate, what customer rejection rate do we incur?

56.6 Identification metrics are different

For 1:N/open-set identification, useful concepts include:

  • candidate rank;
  • Rank-1 identification rate;
  • false-positive identification rate (FPIR);
  • false-negative identification rate (FNIR);
  • detection-and-identification rate under an FPIR operating point.

Do not evaluate a million-person gallery by reporting only pairwise verification accuracy.

The gallery search changes the operational problem.

56.7 PAD metrics: APCER and BPCER

ISO/IEC 30107 terminology commonly distinguishes attacks from bona fide presentations.

Two central concepts are:

APCER — Attack Presentation Classification Error Rate

The proportion of attack presentations incorrectly classified as bona fide.

Conceptually:

spoof presented
     │
     ▼
PAD says REAL

BPCER — Bona Fide Presentation Classification Error Rate

The proportion of genuine presentations incorrectly classified as attacks.

real customer
     │
     ▼
PAD says SPOOF

These embody the same security/usability tension:

  • decrease APCER aggressively -> may increase BPCER;
  • make PAD permissive -> customer experience improves until attacks start passing.

Evaluation should include multiple attack instrument types, not just one generic “spoof” class.

56.8 Cross-attack generalization

A PAD model trained on:

phone A replay
printer B print

may learn shortcuts that fail on:

phone C OLED
printer D glossy stock
3D silicone mask
new deepfake renderer

Therefore testing should include unseen attack instruments and domains.

The central scientific question is not merely:

Can the network memorize known spoof textures?

It is:

Has it learned sufficiently general physical evidence of bona-fide presentation?

That is a much harder problem.

56.9 Demographic performance

Biometric evaluation should be sliced across relevant demographic groups and environmental conditions.

A global average can conceal substantial subgroup differences.

For each group, examine at least:

score distributions
FMR at selected threshold
FNMR at selected threshold
PAD errors
detection failure
quality-rejection rates

The objective is not merely fairness reporting after launch. It is to catch hidden failure modes before they become financial/customer harm.

56.10 Sensor stratification

Evaluate separately by:

camera model
lens
resolution
firmware
ISP pipeline
RGB vs IR
terminal generation

A change in image signal processing can alter:

  • sharpening;
  • noise;
  • tone mapping;
  • color;
  • compression;

which can shift model behavior even though “the camera still looks fine” to a human.

56.11 Environmental stratification

Test:

bright daylight
backlight
dim indoor
mixed lighting
warm/cool illumination
motion
standing distance
height variation
head pose
glasses
masks/partial occlusion
facial hair variation over time

Operational data distribution is part of the system.

56.12 End-to-end transaction metrics

Component metrics are necessary but not sufficient.

The product ultimately needs metrics such as:

legitimate first-attempt success rate
legitimate success within N retries
median/p95/p99 auth latency
PAD rejection rate
recognition rejection rate
capture-quality rejection rate
step-up rate
false authorization incidence
terminal failure rate
fallback-payment success rate

This is where computer-vision engineering meets payment UX and reliability engineering.

56.13 Latency budget

Suppose your customer-experience goal is approximately sub-second biometric decisioning.

You need a latency budget rather than one benchmark:

capture stabilization          120 ms
detection/tracking              15 ms
quality/alignment                5 ms
PAD inference                   25 ms
recognition                      8 ms
network                         80 ms
server match                    10 ms
risk decision                   30 ms
payment authorization          150 ms
------------------------------------------------
illustrative total             443 ms

Those numbers are examples, not guarantees.

The important architectural idea is budget ownership.

If everyone optimizes their component in isolation, total p99 latency can still be terrible.

56.14 Tail latency matters

Customers feel slow transactions at p95/p99, not just averages.

A service with:

mean = 100 ms
p99  = 2.8 s

can create an awful kiosk experience even though the mean looks excellent.

Measure latency distributions by stage.

56.15 Reliability under dependency failure

Chaos/failure testing should deliberately break:

camera service
PAD model server
recognition server
template database
HSM/KMS
risk engine
network
payment core

Then verify that the system transitions to the intended safe/fallback state.

A model benchmark cannot tell you whether dependency failure accidentally disables authentication.

Connection Bridge — Reassembling the Pieces into a System

The preceding chapters intentionally decomposed the problem. This chapter starts putting it back together. Notice that a production architecture is not simply the earlier neural networks connected in a straight line. It also contains quality gates, trusted capture, storage boundaries, cryptographic services, fallbacks, observability, and policy enforcement.

A useful way to evaluate any proposed design is to trace one failure through it: What if detection is wrong? What if liveness is uncertain? What if vector search returns the wrong candidate? What if the network is unavailable? What if the sensor pipeline is compromised? Good system design makes each failure local and recoverable instead of letting confidence propagate unchecked toward money movement.

57. A Reference Production Architecture

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous56. Metrics and Evaluation for a Real Biometric Payment System
This chapter57. A Reference Production Architecture
Next58. What I Would Prototype First

Now we can assemble everything into one coherent system.

This is not the only valid architecture, but it shows where the ideas in this document belong.

57.1 High-level system

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
