That makes it possible to ask harder questions that would have been premature at the beginning: Why does a residual path improve optimization mathematically? Why do one-to-many and one-to-one assignments coexist during detector training? Why can quantization move biometric operating points? Why is ANN retrieval not the same thing as identification?

Think of Part II as changing lenses from architecture literacy to research and production literacy. The goal is no longer to recognize a diagram; it is to predict where a design will break, what metric will expose the break, and what alternative mechanism could fix it.

Part II — Advanced Deep Dives

The first seventy-two chapters establish the main architecture map. The chapters below deliberately go one layer deeper into the mechanisms that are easiest to hand-wave: optimization, assignment, transformer internals, sensor physics, temporal estimation, low-precision inference, biometric security, and the systems engineering that binds all of them together.

The goal of this second part is not to introduce more names. It is to make the names mechanically understandable.

73. Backpropagation, Optimization, and Why Deep Vision Networks Learn at All

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous72. Equation and Tensor-Shape Cheat Sheet
This chapter73. Backpropagation, Optimization, and Why Deep Vision Networks Learn at All
Next74. The Full Object-Detection Lineage: Sliding Windows, R-CNN, Fast/Faster R-CNN, SSD, FCOS, and YOLO

It is easy to say that a CNN “learns features.” That sentence is correct but hides almost everything interesting. A model learns because a scalar loss creates a chain of derivatives that tells every parameter how a tiny change in that parameter would change the final error.

That is backpropagation.

73.1 Forward pass first

Consider a tiny network:

image x
  |
  v
Conv W1
  |
  v
activation h1
  |
  v
Conv W2
  |
  v
prediction y_hat
  |
  v
loss L(y_hat, y)

The forward pass computes values from left to right.

For one layer:

z=Wx+b z = Wx + b

h=ϕ(z) h = \phi(z)

where φ is an activation such as ReLU, SiLU, or GELU.

The final prediction may be a class probability, a bounding box, an embedding, or a set of object-query outputs. Training wraps that output in an objective function such as cross-entropy, CIoU, focal loss, ArcFace, or a weighted sum of several losses.

The important idea is that the entire computation is differentiable enough that we can ask:

If I changed this weight by an infinitesimal amount, how would the loss change?

That number is a derivative.

73.2 The chain rule is the engine

Suppose:

L=L(y) L = L(y)

y=f(z) y = f(z)

z=g(w) z = g(w)

Then:

∂L∂w=∂L∂y∂y∂z∂z∂w \frac{\partial L}{\partial w} = \frac{\partial L}{\partial y} \frac{\partial y}{\partial z} \frac{\partial z}{\partial w}

This is the chain rule.

Deep learning is essentially the industrial-scale application of this rule through enormous computation graphs.

A real detector may contain millions of parameters, yet automatic differentiation can propagate gradients from the final detection loss back through:

box loss
  -> detection head
  -> neck fusion
  -> backbone block
  -> early convolutions
  -> individual kernel weights

The detector does not know that a certain kernel should become an edge detector. It only knows that nudging that kernel in a certain direction reduces the downstream training objective.

73.3 A convolution gradient is still just structured linear algebra

For a convolution, the same kernel weight participates at many image positions because of weight sharing.

That means its gradient is the sum of contributions from all locations where that weight was used.

Conceptually:

kernel weight w_11
  used at patch A
  used at patch B
  used at patch C
  ...

∂L/∂w_11 = contribution_A + contribution_B + contribution_C + ...

This is one reason convolution learns reusable spatial patterns rather than position-specific templates.

73.4 Why gradients vanish

Imagine repeatedly multiplying by derivatives smaller than 1:

0.850≈1.4×10−5 0.8^{50} \approx 1.4\times10^{-5}

The gradient reaching early layers becomes tiny.

Historically this made very deep networks hard to train.

The opposite can happen if repeated Jacobians amplify gradients, causing exploding values.

This is why architectural details such as residual connections, normalization, initialization, and activation choice matter so much.

73.5 Residual connections create short derivative paths

For a residual block:

y=F(x,W)+x y = F(x,W)+x

Differentiate with respect to x:

∂y∂x=∂F∂x+I \frac{\partial y}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial F}{\partial x}+I

That identity term is profound.

Even if the learned branch has a poor derivative at some point, there is a direct path through the shortcut.

This does not mean gradients can never vanish in a ResNet. It means optimization has a much friendlier route through depth.

This is the mathematical reason the phrase “skip connection” is more than visual-diagram trivia.

73.6 Gradient descent

Once gradients are known, the simplest update is:

Wt+1=Wt−η∇WL W_{t+1}=W_t-\eta\nabla_WL

where η is the learning rate.

If the gradient points uphill, subtracting it moves downhill.

That sounds straightforward, but a neural network loss landscape is extremely high-dimensional, noisy, anisotropic, and often badly conditioned. The practical problem is not merely “follow the gradient.” It is “follow noisy mini-batch gradients in a way that converges quickly without destabilizing the representation.”

73.7 Mini-batch stochastic gradient descent

Instead of computing gradients over the entire dataset, training normally uses a mini-batch:

Dataset: 5,000,000 images

step 1 -> sample batch of 128
step 2 -> another 128
step 3 -> another 128
...

The gradient is therefore noisy.

This noise can actually help optimization because it prevents training from behaving like a deterministic local search over one fixed surface.

73.8 Momentum

Momentum maintains a moving accumulation of gradients:

vt=βvt−1+gt v_t = \beta v_{t-1} + g_t

Wt+1=Wt−ηvt W_{t+1}=W_t-\eta v_t

Intuitively:

one noisy gradient      -> small influence
many gradients pointing
in same direction       -> momentum builds

It damps oscillation in steep directions and accelerates movement in persistent directions.

Classic SGD + momentum remains extremely strong for computer vision.

73.9 Adam and AdamW

Adam maintains estimates of first and second gradient moments:

mt=β1mt−1+(1−β1)gt m_t=\beta_1m_{t-1}+(1-\beta_1)g_t

vt=β2vt−1+(1−β2)gt2 v_t=\beta_2v_{t-1}+(1-\beta_2)g_t^2

The update scales parameters according to these moment estimates.

AdamW decouples weight decay from the gradient update, which gives cleaner regularization behavior than treating L2 regularization as an ordinary loss term inside Adam.

Transformers are frequently trained with AdamW. CNN detectors have historically used both SGD-family and AdamW-family recipes depending on architecture and dataset.

73.10 Weight decay

Weight decay discourages arbitrarily large parameters.

One way to think about it:

fit the data
    +
prefer simpler/smaller parameter magnitudes

This is not the same as saying small weights are always better. It is a regularizing pressure that often improves generalization.

73.11 Learning-rate schedules

The learning rate is rarely constant.

Common strategies include:

  • step decay;
  • cosine decay;
  • one-cycle schedules;
  • linear warmup;
  • cosine warm restarts;
  • polynomial decay.

Warmup

A model at initialization can produce unstable activations and gradients.

Warmup begins with a small learning rate and increases it gradually:

learning rate
   ^
   |        /-----------------
   |       /
   |      /
   |_____/
   +--------------------------> steps
       warmup

This is common in large vision training recipes.

73.12 Gradient clipping

If a gradient norm becomes huge, clip it:

g←g⋅min(1,c∥g∥) g \leftarrow g\cdot \min\left(1,\frac{c}{\|g\|}\right)

This is especially common in transformer training and recurrent systems, but can be useful elsewhere.

73.13 Mixed precision training

Training in FP32 everywhere wastes compute on modern accelerators.

A mixed-precision pipeline may use FP16 or BF16 for many matrix operations while retaining numerically sensitive accumulations in FP32.

Benefits:

  • higher throughput;
  • lower memory usage;
  • larger possible batch sizes.

The complication is dynamic range. FP16 gradients can underflow, so frameworks may use loss scaling.

73.14 Backpropagation through a detector is multi-objective

A detector rarely has one loss.

A generic structure is:

L=λboxLbox+λclsLcls+λobjLobj+λauxLaux L = \lambda_{box}L_{box} +\lambda_{cls}L_{cls} +\lambda_{obj}L_{obj} +\lambda_{aux}L_{aux}

Modern anchor-free detectors may omit a separate objectness head, while transformer detectors use classification, box, generalized-IoU, and auxiliary decoder-layer losses.

The weights λ matter because gradients from different objectives can compete.

For example:

classification wants:
"separate person from background"

box loss wants:
"move boundaries precisely"

small-object assignment wants:
"provide enough positive examples"

Training is a negotiation between these signals.

73.15 Backpropagation through ArcFace

ArcFace modifies the target-class logit geometrically.

If normalized feature x and normalized class weight W_y form angle θ_y, ArcFace uses approximately:

scos(θy+m) s\cos(\theta_y+m)

for the target class.

The cross-entropy gradient then pushes the feature so that the correct class angle becomes smaller by a stronger margin constraint.

Backpropagation therefore does not merely say:

classify this image as identity 7421.

It says:

move the representation on the unit hypersphere so identity 7421 becomes angularly compact and separated from competing identities by the imposed margin.

This is why the loss shapes the geometry of the embedding space.

73.16 The optimizer disappears at inference

After training:

optimizer        gone
backprop graph   gone
labels           gone
loss function    usually gone

Inference is only the forward computation required to produce outputs.

This distinction matters when reading architectures. A training recipe may contain sophisticated assignment, auxiliary heads, teacher models, or losses that do not exist in the final runtime graph.

YOLO26 is a good current example: its training process uses both one-to-many and one-to-one supervision, while deployment can select the one-to-one NMS-free path.

74. The Full Object-Detection Lineage: Sliding Windows, R-CNN, Fast/Faster R-CNN, SSD, FCOS, and YOLO

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous73. Backpropagation, Optimization, and Why Deep Vision Networks Learn at All
This chapter74. The Full Object-Detection Lineage: Sliding Windows, R-CNN, Fast/Faster R-CNN, SSD, FCOS, and YOLO
Next75. Residual Networks Revisited: Gradient Highways, Bottlenecks, Pre-Activation, and IResNet

YOLO makes more sense when you understand what it was escaping from.

Object detection has always had to solve two problems simultaneously:

  1. localization — where is the object?
  2. classification — what is it?

The history of detectors is largely a history of how those two questions are coupled.

74.1 Sliding-window detection

A primitive approach is:

for every image location:
    crop a window
    classify the crop

Then repeat at multiple scales because objects may be large or small.

scale 1: scan 64x64 windows
scale 2: scan 128x128 windows
scale 3: scan 256x256 windows

This is brutally redundant. Neighboring windows overlap heavily, so almost the same pixels are processed again and again.

Classical HOG + SVM pedestrian detectors worked in this spirit, but deep CNNs made the repeated-computation cost painful.

74.2 R-CNN: region proposals plus CNN classification

R-CNN proposed a smarter pipeline:

image
  |
  v
Selective Search
  |
~2000 region proposals
  |
  v
crop each region
  |
  v
CNN feature extraction
  |
  +--> class classifier
  +--> box regressor

The major improvement was to avoid blindly scanning every possible window.

But R-CNN still ran the CNN separately for many proposed regions.

That is computationally expensive and storage-heavy.

74.3 Fast R-CNN: compute convolution once

Fast R-CNN inverted the order:

image
  |
  v
CNN once
  |
  v
shared feature map
  |
  +--> ROI 1
  +--> ROI 2
  +--> ROI 3

Each region proposal is mapped onto the shared feature map, then ROI pooling produces a fixed-size representation.

This removes enormous duplicated computation.

The remaining bottleneck is proposal generation itself, which still depends on an external algorithm such as Selective Search.

74.4 Faster R-CNN: learn the proposals

Faster R-CNN introduced a Region Proposal Network (RPN).

image
  |
  v
backbone feature map
  |
  +--> RPN -> region proposals
  |
  +--> ROI head -> classify + refine boxes

Now the proposal mechanism shares convolutional features with the detector.

The RPN predicts, at many locations, whether candidate anchor boxes likely contain an object and how those anchors should shift.

This architecture established the modern two-stage detector pattern:

Stage 1: propose candidate object regions
Stage 2: classify/refine those regions

Two-stage detectors can be extremely accurate because the second stage spends dedicated computation on a smaller set of candidate regions.

74.5 Why anchors appeared

Suppose a feature-map cell needs to predict objects of different shapes.

Instead of predicting arbitrary rectangles from nothing, anchor-based systems define reference boxes:

small square
wide rectangle
tall rectangle
large square
...

The network predicts offsets relative to those anchors.

If anchor center is (x_a,y_a) and dimensions (w_a,h_a), a model may predict transformed offsets that decode to a new box.

Anchors provide useful priors, but introduce design choices:

  • how many anchors?
  • what scales?
  • what aspect ratios?
  • which ground-truth box matches which anchor?
  • what IoU threshold defines positive versus negative?

Modern anchor-free designs remove much of this machinery.

74.6 SSD: one-stage multi-scale detection

Single Shot MultiBox Detector (SSD) made one-stage dense prediction practical.

Instead of proposals followed by a second classifier:

image
  |
  v
CNN
  |
  +--> feature map A -> detections
  +--> feature map B -> detections
  +--> feature map C -> detections

Different feature scales specialize in different object sizes.

This idea strongly overlaps with the multi-scale philosophy later seen throughout YOLO.

74.7 YOLOv1: global single-pass reasoning

YOLOv1 reframed detection as a single regression problem over the entire image.

That was philosophically important.

Instead of a proposal pipeline:

proposal -> classify -> refine

YOLO says:

image -> network -> boxes + classes

This makes detection easy to optimize for real-time inference.

Its early limitations included localization error and weaker handling of small/grouped objects, but the architectural direction was enormously influential.

74.8 RetinaNet and focal loss

One-stage detectors historically faced a severe class imbalance:

millions of candidate background locations
vs
few real objects

If ordinary cross-entropy treats all easy background examples equally, training can be dominated by trivial negatives.

Focal loss modifies cross-entropy so easy examples contribute less:

FL(pt)=−αt(1−pt)γlog(pt) FL(p_t)=-\alpha_t(1-p_t)^\gamma\log(p_t)

If p_t is already near 1 for an easy sample, (1-p_t)^γ becomes tiny.

This lets training focus on difficult examples.

The conceptual importance goes beyond RetinaNet: modern detector losses are often designed around quality-aware weighting rather than treating every positive/negative equally.

74.9 FCOS: anchor-free dense detection

FCOS showed that dense detection can work without anchor boxes.

At a positive feature location, predict distances to four box sides:

        top
         |
     +-------+
left |   •   | right
     +-------+
         |
       bottom

So the model predicts approximately:

(l,t,r,b) (l,t,r,b)

This removes anchor-shape design and simplifies assignment.

A centerness branch suppresses predictions that originate far from object centers.

Modern YOLO anchor-free heads follow the same broad movement away from handcrafted anchor templates, although implementation details differ.

74.10 CenterNet and keypoint-style detection

Another anchor-free family treats objects as center points or keypoints.

The network predicts a heatmap of object centers and regresses object size/offset.

This demonstrates a general theme:

bounding boxes are not the only possible representation of object detection.

One can predict corners, centers, side distances, distributions, or object queries.

74.11 Dense prediction versus set prediction

The most useful conceptual split today is:

Dense detectors

Examples:

  • YOLO;
  • RetinaNet;
  • FCOS;
  • many anchor-free one-stage systems.

They evaluate many spatial candidates.

P3: thousands of locations
P4: thousands fewer
P5: fewer again

Training needs assignment rules deciding which locations supervise which objects.

Inference often historically needed NMS to remove duplicates.

Set-prediction detectors

DETR says:

I want a fixed set of object slots.
Each ground-truth object matches one slot.
Other slots predict no-object.

This changes detection from dense candidate scoring into a combinatorial matching problem.

NMS becomes unnecessary in the original formulation because training explicitly pushes predictions toward one-to-one set structure.

74.12 Why YOLO and DETR are converging conceptually

Modern detectors borrow ideas across lineages.

YOLO26, for example, trains a conventional one-to-many path and a one-to-one end-to-end path. The one-to-one path supports NMS-free inference.

That means the old binary distinction:

YOLO = dense/NMS
DETR = set/NMS-free

is no longer reliable.

The field is converging toward hybrid designs that preserve efficient convolutional multi-scale backbones while adopting end-to-end assignment principles.

75. Residual Networks Revisited: Gradient Highways, Bottlenecks, Pre-Activation, and IResNet

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous74. The Full Object-Detection Lineage: Sliding Windows, R-CNN, Fast/Faster R-CNN, SSD, FCOS, and YOLO
This chapter75. Residual Networks Revisited: Gradient Highways, Bottlenecks, Pre-Activation, and IResNet
Next76. Detection Training Mechanics: Positive Assignment, Focal Loss, Quality-Aware Scores, and Modern Heads

ResNet is central to both object detection and face recognition, so it deserves a second, deeper treatment.

75.1 What degradation problem ResNet addressed

Before residual learning, simply adding layers to a network could make training accuracy worse, even when overfitting was not the explanation.

This is the degradation problem.

In theory, a deeper network should at least be able to emulate a shallower one by making extra layers identity mappings.

In practice, learning those identities through many nonlinear layers was difficult.

Residual learning makes identity explicit:

y=x+F(x) y=x+F(x)

If no transformation is useful, learn:

F(x)≈0 F(x)\approx0

instead of reconstructing x through multiple nonlinear layers.

75.2 Basic block versus bottleneck block

Basic block

Common in ResNet-18/34:

x ----------------------+
|                       |
v                       |
3x3 Conv                 |
Norm                     |
Act                      |
3x3 Conv                 |
Norm                     |
|                       |
+-----------------------+
        add
         |
        Act

Bottleneck block

Common in ResNet-50/101/152:

1x1 reduce
3x3 process
1x1 expand
+
shortcut

The 1×1 layers manage channel dimensionality so the expensive 3×3 convolution operates in a narrower space.

75.3 Projection shortcuts

If input and output shapes differ, identity addition is impossible.

Example:

input:  256 x 56 x 56
output: 512 x 28 x 28

A shortcut may use a stride-2 1×1 convolution:

shortcut: 256x56x56 -> 512x28x28

Then addition is valid.

75.4 Pre-activation ResNet

A later ResNet formulation moved normalization and activation before convolution:

BN -> ReLU -> Conv -> BN -> ReLU -> Conv -> add

rather than putting activation after the residual addition.

The motivation is an even cleaner identity path through the network.

This illustrates an important lesson: residual learning is not one exact block diagram; it is a design principle with many concrete variants.

75.5 Why ResNet works well for face embeddings

Face recognition needs a backbone that:

  • preserves subtle identity information;
  • tolerates nuisance variation;
  • trains deeply and stably;
  • produces a compact global representation.

Residual networks satisfy those needs well and have become a default backbone family for deep face recognition.

75.6 IResNet in face recognition

The ArcFace ecosystem commonly uses IR / IResNet-style backbones rather than an untouched ImageNet ResNet.

Face-recognition ResNets often change details such as:

  • normalization placement;
  • stem design;
  • downsampling behavior;
  • final embedding head;
  • removal/replacement of some ImageNet classification assumptions.

The exact implementation matters. Saying “ResNet-100” in face recognition does not necessarily mean the canonical torchvision ResNet-101 minus one layer.

Always inspect the source/config used by the face-recognition framework.

75.7 The embedding head

A face backbone may end roughly like:

spatial feature map
   |
flatten / global representation
   |
linear projection
   |
BatchNorm / normalization
   |
512-D embedding

The classifier used during training may contain millions of identity-class weights, but those classifier weights are not required for normal verification inference.

Inference retains the feature extractor.

75.8 Residual networks as ensembles of paths

One useful interpretation of residual networks is that skip connections create many effective path lengths.

Information can travel through:

short path
medium path
long path

rather than one monolithic chain of equal depth.

This partially explains their optimization robustness and why residual ideas appear almost everywhere now—including transformers, YOLO attention blocks, and segmentation decoders.

76. Detection Training Mechanics: Positive Assignment, Focal Loss, Quality-Aware Scores, and Modern Heads

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous75. Residual Networks Revisited: Gradient Highways, Bottlenecks, Pre-Activation, and IResNet
This chapter76. Detection Training Mechanics: Positive Assignment, Focal Loss, Quality-Aware Scores, and Modern Heads
Next77. YOLO26 Under the Microscope: Dual Heads, DFL-Free Regression, STAL, MuSGD, and Deployment

A detector does not learn simply because boxes are annotated.

The model may produce thousands of candidate predictions per image. Training must decide:

Which candidates are responsible for each ground-truth object?

That is label assignment.

It is one of the most underappreciated parts of object detection.

76.1 The assignment problem

Suppose a 640×640 detector predicts across:

P3: 80x80 = 6400 locations
P4: 40x40 = 1600 locations
P5: 20x20 = 400 locations
---------------------------
Total = 8400 locations

If each location can predict multiple classes and a box, which of those 8400 locations should learn from one face bounding box?

If too few are positive, supervision is sparse.

If too many are positive, many duplicate predictions learn the same object.

If poor-quality locations are assigned, optimization becomes noisy.

76.2 Old IoU-threshold assignment

Anchor-based systems often did something like:

IoU(anchor, GT) >= 0.7 -> positive
IoU(anchor, GT) <= 0.3 -> negative
otherwise              -> ignore

This is simple but brittle.

The thresholds are hand-designed and do not necessarily reflect which predictions are easiest to learn.

76.3 Center-based assignment

Anchor-free systems may prefer candidate points near the center of the object.

Why?

A point near the center has a balanced geometric relationship to the four sides and usually corresponds to stronger object evidence.

This avoids assigning edge/background-like locations as strong positives.

76.4 ATSS

Adaptive Training Sample Selection chooses positives using statistics of candidate IoUs rather than one global fixed threshold.

The broader lesson is that assignment can be adaptive to each ground-truth object.

76.5 SimOTA

SimOTA, popularized through YOLOX, approximates optimal-transport-style dynamic matching.

Instead of a fixed number of positives per object, the number can depend on candidate quality.

Conceptually:

candidate quality = classification quality + localization quality

choose a dynamic set of low-cost candidates

This helped move detection training away from static anchor heuristics.

76.6 Task-aligned assignment

Classification confidence and localization quality can disagree.

Example:

Prediction A:
class person = 0.99
IoU          = 0.42

Prediction B:
class person = 0.80
IoU          = 0.91

Which one should be considered the “best” positive?

Task-aligned assignment attempts to align classification and localization quality so high classification scores correspond more closely to accurately localized boxes.

This is a recurring modern detector theme.

76.7 Focal loss

For binary classification, ordinary cross-entropy is:

CE(pt)=−log(pt) CE(p_t)=-\log(p_t)

Focal loss:

FL(pt)=−αt(1−pt)γlog(pt) FL(p_t)=-\alpha_t(1-p_t)^\gamma\log(p_t)

If the model already predicts an easy negative correctly with p_t=0.999, the focusing term is extremely small.

That prevents oceans of easy background examples from dominating optimization.

76.8 Quality focal loss and varifocal ideas

A binary label says:

object = 1
background = 0

But object predictions differ in quality.

A box with IoU 0.95 should ideally receive a higher ranking score than one with IoU 0.52.

Quality-aware classification objectives encode localization quality into the classification target or weighting so confidence better reflects overall detection quality.

This matters because inference ranks predictions by confidence.

76.9 DFL: distributional box regression

Instead of predicting one scalar distance directly, Distribution Focal Loss represents a box boundary as a distribution over discrete bins.

For one side:

bins: 0 1 2 3 4 5 ... 15
prob: .0 .0 .1 .2 .5 .2 ...

The final continuous coordinate can be the expectation over bins.

This gives the model a richer representation of localization uncertainty than direct scalar regression.

Ultralytics YOLOv8 and YOLO11 use DFL-style regression with reg_max=16 in their current architecture descriptions.

YOLO26 deliberately removes DFL, simplifying the head and export path. The removal is not evidence that DFL was “wrong”; it reflects a different efficiency/accuracy design point.

76.10 IoU losses revisited

IoU

IoU=|A∩B||A∪B| IoU=\frac{|A\cap B|}{|A\cup B|}

Good as a geometric overlap measure, but if boxes do not overlap:

IoU=0 IoU=0

and the loss may provide poor directional information.

GIoU

Adds a penalty using the smallest enclosing box.

DIoU

Adds center-distance information.

CIoU

Adds center distance plus aspect-ratio consistency.

The purpose of these variants is to create a smoother optimization signal that cares about more than raw overlap.

76.11 Decoupled heads

A shared terminal head asks one feature representation to solve both:

  • what is it?
  • where exactly is it?

Those tasks are related but not identical.

A decoupled head splits them:

fused feature
   |
   +--> classification branch
   |
   +--> box branch

This lets each branch specialize.

Modern YOLO detectors use this pattern.

76.12 One-to-many versus one-to-one supervision

One-to-many

One ground-truth object can supervise multiple positive predictions.

Benefits:

  • dense learning signal;
  • strong optimization;
  • often very good accuracy.

Cost:

  • duplicate predictions at inference;
  • historically requires NMS.

One-to-one

Each ground-truth object maps to one prediction.

Benefits:

  • natural end-to-end set output;
  • can remove NMS.

Challenge:

  • weaker/sparser supervision can make training harder.

This is why dual-head or dual-assignment strategies are attractive: use richer supervision while training, but retain a clean one-to-one inference path.

77. YOLO26 Under the Microscope: Dual Heads, DFL-Free Regression, STAL, MuSGD, and Deployment

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous76. Detection Training Mechanics: Positive Assignment, Focal Loss, Quality-Aware Scores, and Modern Heads
This chapter77. YOLO26 Under the Microscope: Dual Heads, DFL-Free Regression, STAL, MuSGD, and Deployment
Next78. Transformers as Linear Algebra: QKV Shapes, Multi-Head Attention, Positional Information, and Decoder Queries

YOLO26 is useful to study because it illustrates how a modern “YOLO” is no longer defined by one historical YOLO paper. It is an engineered system composed of a YOLO-style convolutional backbone/neck, modern attention blocks, revised assignment, multiple training heads, and deployment-oriented simplification.

According to current Ultralytics documentation, the core YOLO26 design changes include:

  • a C3k2 + C2PSA backbone lineage inherited from YOLO11;
  • an SPPF residual shortcut;
  • DFL-free box regression;
  • dual one-to-many and one-to-one detection heads;
  • optional native NMS-free inference;
  • Progressive Loss;
  • Small-Target-Aware Label Assignment (STAL);
  • MuSGD optimization;
  • task-specific head/loss refinements across detection, segmentation, pose, OBB, and other tasks.

Current official architecture guide: https://docs.ultralytics.com/guides/yolo-architecture/

Current YOLO26 model guide: https://docs.ultralytics.com/models/yolo26/

77.1 Backbone recap

The high-level path remains:

input image
   |
stride-2 Conv
   |
C3k2 stages
   |
SPPF (+ residual shortcut)
   |
C2PSA
   |
P3/P4/P5 features

The backbone extracts increasingly semantic features while reducing spatial resolution.

77.2 C3k2

C3k2 is a C2f-style cross-stage partial block that can use either bottleneck or C3k internals depending on configuration/scale.

The conceptual point is not the name. It is the routing pattern:

input
  |
channel transform/split
  |\
  | \---- bypass/intermediate reuse
  v
repeated internal blocks
  |
concatenate multiple features
  |
fusion convolution

The block preserves multiple gradient/information paths without paying the cost of applying the deepest transformation to the entire feature tensor.

77.3 C2PSA

C2PSA inserts position-sensitive attention into a CSP-style split/fuse structure.

Conceptually:

input
  |
 split
 /   \
A     B
|     |
|   PSA blocks
|     |
 \   /
 concat
   |
 fuse

Only part of the representation goes through the more expensive attention path, which controls compute.

The PSA block combines attention with an FFN-like convolutional projection and residual connections.

This is a good example of hybridization:

modern YOLO is not “CNN versus transformer.” It is convolutional feature extraction with selective attention inserted where global/contextual reasoning is valuable.

77.4 SPPF with residual shortcut

SPPF applies repeated 5×5 max pooling to cheaply produce multiple effective receptive fields.

YOLO26 adds a shortcut when dimensions allow:

x ----------------------+
|                       |
v                       |
SPPF processing --------+
          |
          v
        x + y

This preserves a direct deep-feature path while injecting pooled context.

77.5 Dual-head training

Current Ultralytics documentation describes two heads:

One-to-many head

Default inference path.

It produces dense predictions and uses NMS.

For a standard 640-scale detector, the exported tensor pattern is described approximately as:

(N, nc + 4, 8400)

where nc is number of classes.

One-to-one head

Selected with NMS-free inference.

The documentation describes an output approximately:

(N, 300, 6)

for up to 300 final detections.

The key idea:

training:
    learn from both dense and one-to-one objectives

inference:
    choose conventional NMS path
    OR
    choose end-to-end one-to-one path

This lets deployment choose the accuracy/latency/export tradeoff.

77.6 Why remove DFL?

DFL gives rich distributional coordinate prediction but increases head complexity.

Removing it can simplify:

  • output decoding;
  • graph export;
  • low-level runtime implementation;
  • head arithmetic.

YOLO26 uses direct regression with an unconstrained range rather than a finite distributional-bin representation.

The deeper engineering lesson is:

model architecture is partly dictated by deployment graphs, not only benchmark accuracy.

A slightly more complex loss/head can be worthwhile on GPU but undesirable on CPU/NPU export paths.

77.7 STAL: Small-Target-Aware Label Assignment

Small objects have fewer candidate spatial locations.

For a tiny face occupying only a small region of P3, a strict assignment strategy may produce too few positives.

STAL is designed to maintain positive label coverage for small targets.

Conceptually:

large object:
many plausible candidate points

small object:
few plausible candidate points

assignment must avoid starving the small object of supervision

This is directly relevant to biometric kiosks when faces can enter the scene at a distance before approaching the terminal.

77.8 Progressive Loss

If a model trains two heads but inference ultimately uses one, the optimization should gradually emphasize the inference-time behavior.

Progressive Loss shifts training emphasis toward the inference-time head over training.

The conceptual analogy is curriculum/annealing:

early training:
strong auxiliary/dense supervision helps representation learning

later training:
optimize harder for the exact behavior needed at inference

77.9 MuSGD

Ultralytics describes MuSGD as a hybrid optimizer combining SGD with Muon-style orthogonalized updates for eligible matrix-like weights.

For convolution kernels, weights can be reshaped into 2D matrices for the Muon-style step.

You do not need to memorize the optimizer to understand YOLO26. The important lesson is that architecture comparison should separate:

runtime architecture
from
training recipe

MuSGD changes how parameters are learned, not the shape of the exported inference graph.

77.10 Objects365 pretraining

The official training recipe states that released YOLO26 base models are pretrained on Objects365 and then fine-tuned on COCO rather than trained from random initialization on COCO.

This matters when comparing models.

If two models use different data/pretraining regimes, raw AP differences are not pure architecture comparisons.

77.11 Why YOLO26 is relevant to a biometric system

Potential uses:

  • person detection in the transaction zone;
  • coarse face/person localization;
  • detecting phones/screens that may participate in replay attacks;
  • monitoring multiple people around the terminal;
  • scene-state detection.

But a YOLO detector is still not a biometric verifier.

It should feed downstream components rather than decide identity.

78. Transformers as Linear Algebra: QKV Shapes, Multi-Head Attention, Positional Information, and Decoder Queries

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous77. YOLO26 Under the Microscope: Dual Heads, DFL-Free Regression, STAL, MuSGD, and Deployment
This chapter78. Transformers as Linear Algebra: QKV Shapes, Multi-Head Attention, Positional Information, and Decoder Queries
Next79. DETR After DETR: Conditional, DAB, DN-DETR, DINO, and RT-DETR

The phrase “attention lets every token look at every token” is useful intuition, but eventually you need the matrix mechanics.

78.1 Start with tokens

Suppose a ViT receives a 224×224 image and uses 16×16 patches.

Number of patches:

14×14=196 14\times14=196

Each RGB patch contains:

16×16×3=768 16\times16\times3=768

raw values before projection.

Flatten each patch and linearly project it to model dimension d:

196 patches
   |
   v
196 tokens of dimension d

So the token matrix is:

X∈ℝN×d X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}

with N=196.

78.2 Q, K, and V projections

Learn three matrices:

WQ,WK,WV∈ℝd×d W_Q,W_K,W_V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

Then:

Q=XWQ Q=XW_Q

K=XWK K=XW_K

V=XWV V=XW_V

All have shape:

N×d N\times d

Interpretation:

  • Query: what information is this token seeking?
  • Key: what kind of information does this token advertise?
  • Value: what content should be transferred if selected?

78.3 Attention score matrix

Compute:

S=QKTdk S=\frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}

If Q and K have N tokens, then:

S∈ℝN×N S\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}

Every row contains how strongly one query token relates to every key token.

Then:

A=softmax(S) A=softmax(S)

Each row becomes a normalized attention distribution.

Finally:

Y=AV Y=AV

Each output token is a weighted combination of value vectors.

78.4 Why divide by square root of d_k?

Dot products grow in magnitude as dimensionality increases.

Large logits push softmax into saturated regions where gradients become tiny.

Scaling by:

1dk \frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}

keeps score magnitudes more stable.

78.5 Multi-head attention

Instead of one huge attention operation, split representation into h heads.

If:

d_model = 768
heads   = 12

then each head may use:

d_head = 64

Each head learns different projection matrices:

head 1 -> maybe shape relationships
head 2 -> maybe texture/context interactions
head 3 -> maybe long-range correspondence
...

Do not interpret heads too literally; learned roles are distributed and not guaranteed to map to human concepts.

Outputs from all heads are concatenated and projected back to model dimension.

78.6 Self-attention versus cross-attention

Self-attention

Q, K, V come from the same token set.

image tokens -> Q
image tokens -> K
image tokens -> V

Used for interactions within an image representation.

Cross-attention

Queries come from one source; keys/values from another.

DETR decoder:

object queries -> Q
image features -> K,V

So object queries ask the encoded image:

where is the evidence for the object slot I should represent?

SAM-style decoders similarly use prompt/object tokens to query image representations.

78.7 Positional information

Vanilla attention is permutation-equivariant. Without position, it does not inherently know that token 15 is above token 29.

Vision transformers therefore inject positional information through methods such as:

  • learned absolute position embeddings;
  • sine/cosine embeddings;
  • relative positional bias;
  • rotary-style encodings in some modern systems;
  • convolutional positional structure.

Spatial reasoning requires some encoding of geometry.

78.8 Feed-forward network

A transformer block also contains a per-token MLP/FFN:

FFN(x)=W2ϕ(W1x+b1)+b2 FFN(x)=W_2\phi(W_1x+b_1)+b_2

This expands and contracts channel dimension.

For example:

768 -> 3072 -> 768

Attention mixes information between tokens.

The FFN performs nonlinear transformation within each token representation.

78.9 Residual structure

A typical pre-norm transformer block:

x
|\
| LayerNorm -> Attention --+
|                          |
+--------------------------+
             |
             v
             y
             |\
             | LayerNorm -> FFN --+
             |                    |
             +--------------------+

Transformers rely on residual connections just as heavily as ResNets.

78.10 Computational complexity

Full attention requires an N×N score matrix.

Complexity scales roughly quadratically with token count:

O(N2d) O(N^2d)

For images, high spatial resolution means many tokens.

Example:

14x14 -> 196 tokens
56x56 -> 3136 tokens

The second case has dramatically more pairwise interactions.

This motivates:

  • window attention;
  • sparse attention;
  • deformable attention;
  • hierarchical transformers;
  • token pooling;
  • hybrid CNN-transformer designs.

78.11 DETR object queries are not image patches

This is a common misunderstanding.

Object queries are learned decoder inputs/object slots.

They are not each permanently tied to “top-left,” “car,” or “person.”

During training, Hungarian matching determines which query is assigned to which ground-truth object in each image.

Over time, queries learn how to interact with encoded image features so the set collectively covers objects.

78.12 Why query-based detection is elegant

Traditional dense detection produces many overlapping hypotheses and cleans them later.

Query-based detection says:

I have K slots.
Each slot should either own one object or predict no-object.

That reframes postprocessing as a learning problem.

This design philosophy is now influencing real-time detectors beyond pure DETR systems.

79. DETR After DETR: Conditional, DAB, DN-DETR, DINO, and RT-DETR

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous78. Transformers as Linear Algebra: QKV Shapes, Multi-Head Attention, Positional Information, and Decoder Queries
This chapter79. DETR After DETR: Conditional, DAB, DN-DETR, DINO, and RT-DETR
Next80. Face Recognition Beyond ArcFace: CurricularFace, MagFace, AdaFace, and Quality-Aware Embeddings

Original DETR was conceptually beautiful but practically imperfect. Its two famous weaknesses were:

  1. slow convergence;
  2. weak performance on small objects compared with mature multi-scale dense detectors.

Much of the DETR family can be understood as a sequence of attempts to make object queries more spatially informed, make attention more efficient, and make matching easier to optimize.

79.1 Original DETR recap

A simplified DETR pipeline:

image
  |
  v
CNN backbone
  |
  v
spatial feature map
  |
flatten + positional encoding
  |
Transformer encoder
  |
encoded image memory
  |
  +--------------------------+
                             |
learned object queries ------+
                             |
                             v
                    Transformer decoder
                             |
                     repeated query states
                             |
                     +-------+-------+
                     |               |
                     v               v
                  classes          boxes

Training performs bipartite matching between predicted slots and ground-truth objects.

The simplicity is deceptive: the decoder initially receives learned queries with weak spatial priors, so it must discover both where and what through attention.

79.2 Conditional DETR

Conditional DETR explicitly separates content and spatial components in decoder attention.

The broad idea is:

make each query’s spatial search more focused rather than asking a generic content vector to learn location from scratch.

This accelerates convergence because cross-attention becomes easier to optimize.

79.3 DAB-DETR: boxes as dynamic anchors

DAB-DETR makes a particularly intuitive change: treat decoder queries as dynamic anchor boxes.

Instead of a query being merely an abstract learned vector, it carries a reference box representation that gets refined across decoder layers.

Conceptually:

query content + reference box
          |
          v
       decoder layer 1
          |
      refined box
          |
       decoder layer 2
          |
      refined box
          |
         ...

The detector now has an explicit spatial hypothesis that can evolve.

This is one reason the boundary between “anchor detector” and “DETR” became blurry. The word anchor can refer to a learned/dynamic reference used inside a query-based set predictor, not only the fixed hand-designed templates of Faster R-CNN-era detectors.

79.4 DN-DETR: denoising training

Matching is difficult early in training because predictions are poor.

DN-DETR adds noisy versions of ground-truth labels and boxes as extra training queries.

Example:

Ground truth:
class = person
box   = [0.30, 0.22, 0.55, 0.81]

Denoising query:
class = person (possibly label-noised)
box   = [0.33, 0.18, 0.58, 0.78]

The model learns to reconstruct the clean target from a perturbed version.

Why useful?

Because this provides a stable, direct learning signal even while ordinary matching queries are still chaotic.

The denoising branch is a training aid. It need not be part of normal inference.

79.5 DINO

DINO—DETR with Improved deNoising anchOr boxes—combines several DETR improvements.

The original paper emphasizes:

  • contrastive denoising training;
  • mixed query selection for better anchor initialization;
  • a “look forward twice” box-prediction scheme;
  • strong multi-scale performance.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.03605

The main mental model is:

Original DETR:
queries start relatively abstract

DINO:
queries are better initialized,
training gets denoising supervision,
and box refinement is better structured

This is why modern DETR descendants can converge much faster than the original architecture.

79.6 Deformable DETR revisited

Full cross-attention over a high-resolution feature map is expensive and diffuse.

Deformable DETR lets each query attend to a small set of learned sampling points around reference locations and across multiple feature scales.

Instead of:

query -> compare with every spatial token

approximately:

query -> reference point
      -> sample a few offsets on P3
      -> sample a few offsets on P4
      -> sample a few offsets on P5

The paper reports much faster convergence than original DETR and stronger small-object performance.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.04159

79.7 RT-DETR

RT-DETR explicitly targets the real-time regime.

Its design is useful to compare with YOLO because it asks:

Can a transformer-based, NMS-free detector achieve real-time speed without abandoning end-to-end set prediction?

The architecture uses an efficient hybrid encoder for multi-scale features and query selection designed to provide high-quality decoder initialization.

A valuable implementation idea is decoder-layer speed tuning: inference can trade accuracy for latency by reducing decoder depth without retraining the whole model.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.08069

79.8 The convergence of real-time detection families

By the mid-2020s, the field no longer fits the simple historical categories:

YOLO = CNN
DETR = Transformer

A more accurate map is:

YOLO-family systems
  -> convolutional multi-scale extraction
  -> selective attention
  -> anchor-free heads
  -> one-to-one/end-to-end paths

DETR-family systems
  -> convolutional or transformer backbones
  -> multi-scale pyramids
  -> sparse/deformable attention
  -> learned/dynamic spatial references
  -> optimized real-time encoders

The best mental distinction is increasingly the prediction and assignment formulation, not whether the network contains convolutions or attention.

Connection Bridge — ArcFace Solved Margin Geometry, Not Every Face-Recognition Problem

ArcFace is an elegant objective because it creates a clear angular margin between identities, but real biometric data varies dramatically in quality. A blurred, tiny, occluded face should not necessarily contribute to training or decision-making in the same way as a clean frontal sample. Later objectives therefore explore curriculum, magnitude, and adaptive margins as ways to make the embedding space quality-aware.

This progression mirrors the detector story: once the core representation works, the research frontier moves toward which examples deserve how much influence, and how should uncertainty be encoded rather than ignored?

80. Face Recognition Beyond ArcFace: CurricularFace, MagFace, AdaFace, and Quality-Aware Embeddings

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous79. DETR After DETR: Conditional, DAB, DN-DETR, DINO, and RT-DETR
This chapter80. Face Recognition Beyond ArcFace: CurricularFace, MagFace, AdaFace, and Quality-Aware Embeddings
Next81. Face Detection Before RetinaFace: MTCNN and the Evolution Toward Dense Face Detectors

ArcFace is an excellent anchor point, but face-recognition research did not stop at a fixed additive angular margin.

Later work asks deeper questions:

  • Should hard examples always receive more emphasis?
  • What if an example is hard because it is low quality rather than identity-informative?
  • Can embedding magnitude encode face quality?
  • Should the training margin adapt to estimated image quality?

These questions matter directly in payment systems because camera quality varies in the wild.

80.1 The fixed-margin assumption

ArcFace applies an angular margin m to the target class:

cos(θy+m) \cos(\theta_y+m)

This treats training samples under one fixed geometric rule.

But samples differ:

Sample A:
frontal, sharp, well-lit

Sample B:
25° yaw, mild blur

Sample C:
strong motion blur + occlusion

Should C receive exactly the same optimization pressure as A?

Maybe not.

If C is nearly information-destroyed, forcing an extreme margin can cause the network to overfit noise.

80.2 CurricularFace

CurricularFace introduces curriculum learning into margin-based face recognition.

The intuition:

early training:
learn easy/clean structure first

later training:
put more emphasis on hard samples

This mirrors how many human learning systems work: solve stable examples before aggressively focusing on confusing boundary cases.

The method adaptively changes the relative weighting of easy and hard negatives based on training state.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.00288

Why this matters

A face network in early training does not yet have a meaningful embedding geometry.

If it aggressively emphasizes extremely hard negatives immediately, some may be:

  • mislabeled;
  • low-quality;
  • severe outliers;
  • unstable because the representation is immature.

Curriculum provides a schedule for difficulty.

80.3 MagFace

MagFace connects embedding magnitude with recognition quality.

Under its training objective, higher-quality/easier-to-recognize faces are encouraged to have larger feature norms.

That produces one representation serving two purposes:

feature direction -> identity
feature magnitude -> quality signal

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.06627

This is conceptually powerful because quality assessment is not bolted on as a totally separate model.

However, do not assume feature norm from every face-recognition model is a calibrated quality score. That property depends on the training formulation.

80.4 AdaFace

AdaFace explicitly adapts the angular margin according to estimated image quality.

The paper uses feature norm as a proxy for quality and changes the training emphasis accordingly.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2204.00964

The high-level reasoning:

high-quality hard example:
probably contains useful identity information
-> worth pushing strongly

low-quality hard example:
may be hard because information is missing
-> do not over-emphasize blindly

This is extremely relevant to kiosk scenarios because blur, glare, pose, distance, and exposure can all degrade identity evidence.

80.5 Quality-aware verification versus quality-aware training

These are different concepts.

Quality-aware training

The learning objective changes according to sample quality.

Quality-aware inference

The runtime system uses a quality estimate to decide:

  • whether to attempt matching;
  • how many frames to aggregate;
  • whether to request recapture;
  • whether to require step-up authentication.

A production system may use both.

80.6 Why confidence is not quality

Suppose a model outputs a high similarity:

cosine similarity = 0.82

That does not automatically mean the capture was high quality.

A poor-quality image could accidentally land close to a template.

Quality is an independent dimension:

match confidence
capture quality
PAD confidence
sensor integrity

These should remain separate features in the risk engine.

80.7 Embedding aggregation

If an enrollment has several good captures:

e1,e2,…,en e_1,e_2,\ldots,e_n

one can build a template by normalized averaging:

e‾=∑iwiei∥∑iwiei∥ \bar e = \frac{\sum_i w_ie_i}{\left\|\sum_iw_ie_i\right\|}

where w_i can reflect quality.

This can improve robustness compared with storing exactly one arbitrary frame.

But template aggregation must be validated carefully because averaging across inconsistent captures can also blur identity structure.

81. Face Detection Before RetinaFace: MTCNN and the Evolution Toward Dense Face Detectors

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous80. Face Recognition Beyond ArcFace: CurricularFace, MagFace, AdaFace, and Quality-Aware Embeddings
This chapter81. Face Detection Before RetinaFace: MTCNN and the Evolution Toward Dense Face Detectors
Next82. Camera and Sensor Physics for Biometrics

A biometric pipeline often begins with a specialized face detector rather than a generic object detector.

Understanding MTCNN helps explain how the field moved toward modern single-stage dense detectors such as RetinaFace and SCRFD.

81.1 MTCNN

MTCNN stands for Multi-task Cascaded Convolutional Networks.

It jointly performs face detection and facial landmark localization through a cascade of three networks.

The three stages are commonly described as:

  • P-Net — Proposal Network;
  • R-Net — Refine Network;
  • O-Net — Output Network.

Primary reference: Zhang et al., Joint Face Detection and Alignment Using Multitask Cascaded Convolutional Networks, IEEE SPL 2016.

81.2 Why a cascade?

Most image windows are obviously not faces.

So do not spend expensive computation on all of them.

many cheap candidates
      |
      v
P-Net rejects easy negatives
      |
fewer candidates
      v
R-Net refines/rejects
      |
very few candidates
      v
O-Net gives final boxes + landmarks

This coarse-to-fine design resembles classical cascaded detectors but uses learned CNN stages.

81.3 P-Net

P-Net operates fully convolutionally over an image pyramid.

The image pyramid handles face scale:

large image scale -> detect small faces
smaller scale     -> detect larger faces

P-Net outputs candidate face scores and bounding-box corrections.

NMS removes many overlaps before passing candidates onward.

81.4 R-Net

Candidate regions are cropped/resized and sent to R-Net.

R-Net is more expensive but sees far fewer candidates.

It further rejects false positives and refines boxes.

81.5 O-Net

O-Net performs final classification/regression and predicts facial landmarks.

These landmarks enable alignment.

81.6 Multi-task learning

The model jointly learns:

  • face/non-face classification;
  • bounding-box regression;
  • landmark regression.

This shared supervision is important because face location and facial geometry reinforce each other.

81.7 Why RetinaFace represented a different direction

MTCNN uses a sequential cascade and image pyramid.

RetinaFace instead is a modern single-stage dense detector with multi-scale feature pyramids and joint landmark prediction.

That is much closer to the one-stage detector lineage discussed earlier.

81.8 RetinaFace’s extra supervision

RetinaFace does more than generic face boxes.

The research formulation combines:

  • face classification;
  • bounding-box regression;
  • five facial landmarks;
  • additional dense 3D face supervision during training.

This encourages representations sensitive to facial geometry.

81.9 SCRFD’s research question

SCRFD asks a systems-level question:

Given a fixed compute budget, where should computation be allocated across backbone, neck, and head for face detection?

This is particularly relevant on terminals and edge devices.

The best face detector is not necessarily the one with the largest generic object-detection backbone. Specialized compute allocation can produce better accuracy/latency tradeoffs.

81.10 Detector selection in a payment kiosk

A useful selection matrix:

Requirement Implication
One close frontal customer tiny specialized detector may be enough
Multiple people in scene stronger multi-face detector/tracker
Faces far from terminal small-face recall becomes important
CPU-only kiosk SCRFD/MobileNet-style efficiency matters
Need landmarks RetinaFace/SCRFD-style face detector helps
Need rich scene objects too generic YOLO alongside face detector may make sense

Do not use a foundation model merely because it is more general.

Connection Bridge — The Neural Network Does Not See the World; It Sees a Sensor’s Measurement

A frequent ML blind spot is to treat the input image as ground truth. It is not. Exposure time, gain, optics, rolling shutter, spectral response, compression, motion blur, depth technology, and illumination determine what information even reaches the model. A recognition failure may therefore be impossible to fix with a larger backbone if the sensor never captured a discriminative signal in the first place.

This is why sensor physics precedes the deeper PAD chapter. Liveness methods based on NIR, depth, rPPG, reflectance, or temporal cues depend on physical measurement assumptions. Security claims are only as strong as the sensing assumptions underneath them.

82. Camera and Sensor Physics for Biometrics

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous81. Face Detection Before RetinaFace: MTCNN and the Evolution Toward Dense Face Detectors
This chapter82. Camera and Sensor Physics for Biometrics
Next83. Presentation Attack Detection in Depth: Physics, rPPG, Depth, NIR, Replay, Masks, and Injection

No neural network can recover information that the sensor never captured.

For biometric systems, camera physics is part of the ML architecture.

82.1 Exposure

A camera sensor integrates photons over an exposure interval.

Longer exposure:

  • gathers more light;
  • improves signal in dark scenes;
  • increases motion blur.

Shorter exposure:

  • freezes motion better;
  • captures fewer photons;
  • may increase noise if gain must rise.

This is a direct tradeoff between blur and noise.

82.2 Gain / ISO

Electronic or analog gain amplifies the sensor signal.

It also amplifies noise.

A dark kiosk camera may produce a visible face after high gain, but fine skin/eye texture can become noisy—exactly the information recognition and PAD may need.

82.3 Motion blur

If the face moves during exposure, one facial point contributes light along a path rather than a point.

Consequences:

motion
  -> blurred eye edges
  -> landmark uncertainty
  -> poor alignment
  -> weaker embedding

This is why a quality gate should detect blur before trusting recognition.

82.4 Rolling shutter

Many CMOS sensors expose rows at slightly different times.

A moving head can therefore become geometrically distorted:

top of face captured at t0
middle at t0 + Δ
bottom at t0 + 2Δ

Fast motion may bend vertical structures or skew geometry.

A global-shutter sensor captures the frame more simultaneously and reduces this class of artifact.

82.5 Dynamic range

A face may be partly shadowed while a bright window is behind it.

If the sensor’s dynamic range is insufficient:

  • highlights clip to white;
  • shadows collapse to black.

Both destroy identity information.

HDR methods can combine exposures or use sensors with higher dynamic range, but multi-exposure HDR can introduce motion artifacts if the person moves.

82.6 Focal length and field of view

A wide field of view covers more scene but produces fewer pixels per face at the same sensor resolution.

A narrow FOV gives better face detail but requires the subject to be in a more constrained zone.

For payment terminals, physical UX and optics should be co-designed:

standing distance
+ camera resolution
+ focal length
+ face-size requirement
= pixels available for biometrics

82.7 Perspective distortion

A very close wide-angle camera exaggerates depth:

  • nose appears larger;
  • ears/facial sides recede;
  • geometry differs from enrollment captures.

Alignment cannot fully undo 3D perspective effects using a 2D affine transform.

This is another argument for designing a stable capture distance.

82.8 Lens distortion

Radial distortion can bend straight lines near image edges.

Common models include radial terms such as:

xd=x(1+k1r2+k2r4+⋯) x_d=x(1+k_1r^2+k_2r^4+\cdots)

Camera calibration can estimate distortion parameters and rectify frames.

For a fixed kiosk camera, calibration is comparatively tractable.

82.9 RGB sensors

RGB cameras are inexpensive and information-rich, but visible appearance changes with:

  • lighting spectrum;
  • shadows;
  • color temperature;
  • makeup;
  • display replay quality.

RGB-only PAD is possible but faces a harder physical inference problem than multimodal capture.

82.10 Near-infrared (NIR)

NIR illumination/cameras capture reflectance outside normal visible wavelengths.

Benefits can include:

  • more controlled illumination;
  • robustness in dark environments;
  • extra material/skin cues;
  • reduced dependence on visible color.

But NIR requires dedicated emitter/sensor support and its own eye-safety and hardware considerations.

82.11 Depth sensing

Depth helps distinguish a 3D face from a flat print or display.

Common mechanisms include:

Structured light

Project a known pattern and infer deformation.

Stereo vision

Use disparity between two cameras.

Approximate depth relation:

Z=fBd Z=\frac{fB}{d}

where:

  • f = focal length;
  • B = baseline between cameras;
  • d = disparity.

Time-of-Flight

Emit modulated light and estimate distance from propagation time/phase.

Each technology has failure modes involving sunlight, reflective materials, range, cost, and calibration.

82.12 Sensor synchronization

If RGB and depth frames are captured at different times, a moving subject creates misalignment.

Multimodal PAD therefore needs:

  • time synchronization;
  • geometric calibration;
  • registration between sensors.

The ML model cannot assume pixel (x,y) in RGB corresponds to the same physical point in depth unless calibration guarantees it.

82.13 A sensor is part of the trust boundary

A perfect PAD model is useless if an attacker can replace the camera stream with a generated video.

So the system must distinguish:

what the pixels look like

from:

whether those pixels came from an authenticated physical sensor

That distinction becomes central in the security chapters.

83. Presentation Attack Detection in Depth: Physics, rPPG, Depth, NIR, Replay, Masks, and Injection

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous82. Camera and Sensor Physics for Biometrics
This chapter83. Presentation Attack Detection in Depth: Physics, rPPG, Depth, NIR, Replay, Masks, and Injection
Next84. Face Quality, Multi-Frame Aggregation, and Decision Stability

Presentation Attack Detection (PAD) is the formal security problem often casually called “liveness.”

ISO/IEC 30107-3:2023 standardizes principles for PAD performance assessment and reporting; it does not prescribe one particular anti-spoofing algorithm.

NIST SP 800-63B’s current biometric guidance requires PAD for facial recognition in the digital-authentication context it governs and emphasizes that biometric characteristics are not secrets. This is an excellent security mental model even when designing outside the specific U.S. federal scope.

83.1 Presentation versus injection attack

These are fundamentally different.

Presentation attack

The attacker presents an artifact to a genuine sensor:

printed face -> camera
phone replay -> camera
3D mask      -> camera

Injection attack

The attacker bypasses or tampers with the capture chain:

generated video
      |
virtual camera / compromised driver
      |
biometric software

A presentation detector cannot solve a compromised sensor pipeline by itself.

83.2 Attack taxonomy

Possible face attacks include:

  • high-resolution print;
  • curved print;
  • cut-eye print;
  • phone/tablet replay;
  • pre-recorded video;
  • real-time deepfake;
  • 2D digital overlay;
  • paper mask;
  • rigid 3D mask;
  • silicone mask;
  • partial prosthetic;
  • makeup/adversarial patterns;
  • camera-stream injection;
  • embedding/template injection.

Each attack exploits different weaknesses.

Therefore “our liveness accuracy is 99%” is meaningless without specifying the attack species and test protocol.

83.3 RGB texture methods

Printed paper and displays have micro-textures different from skin.

A CNN can learn cues such as:

  • printing dot patterns;
  • moiré;
  • display subpixel structure;
  • reflectance;
  • edge artifacts;
  • frequency-domain differences.

Problem:

These cues can overfit to specific printers/screens used during training.

A model that detects “this exact iPhone display” is not a general PAD system.

83.4 Frequency-domain methods

Replayed/printed content may introduce periodic patterns not obvious in raw RGB space.

Fourier or frequency-oriented features can expose:

  • halftone printing;
  • screen refresh artifacts;
  • resampling patterns;
  • moiré.

Modern networks can learn such representations directly or combine spatial/frequency branches.

83.5 Depth-based PAD

A live face has 3D facial relief:

nose protrudes
cheeks curve
eye sockets recede

A flat photograph is approximately planar.

Depth is therefore a strong physical cue against 2D attacks.

But depth is not sufficient against high-quality 3D masks.

83.6 NIR / active illumination

Material reflectance differs across wavelengths.

Skin, paper, displays, and mask materials can respond differently under NIR illumination.

A multimodal network can fuse RGB + NIR + depth.

Fusion patterns include:

Early fusion

Concatenate channels near the input.

Mid-level fusion

Separate encoders, then merge learned features.

Late fusion

Independent PAD scores combined by a decision layer.

Mid/late fusion can be more robust when modalities have different resolutions/noise characteristics.

83.7 Temporal cues

Video provides evidence unavailable in one frame:

  • blink dynamics;
  • mouth/head motion;
  • optical flow;
  • 3D parallax;
  • subtle skin-color variation;
  • temporal consistency.

A replay can imitate some of these, so no single cue should be treated as proof.

83.8 rPPG

Remote photoplethysmography estimates tiny temporal color changes caused by blood-volume variation.

A simplified pipeline:

video
  |
face/skin ROI
  |
spatial color aggregation
  |
temporal signal
  |
band-pass / learned temporal representation
  |
pulse-related periodicity

A live face may exhibit physiological variation that a static print lacks.

But sophisticated replay videos can contain the original pulse signal, and lighting/compression can corrupt it.

Therefore rPPG is useful evidence, not an absolute liveness oracle.

83.9 Challenge-response liveness

The system asks for a random action:

turn left
blink twice
follow moving dot
read random digits

Randomness prevents a fixed replay from satisfying the challenge easily.

But challenges hurt UX and accessibility, and deepfake systems can increasingly synthesize responses in real time.

Use challenge-response as one layer, not the entire security model.

83.10 Passive versus active PAD

Passive

No explicit user action.

Pros:

  • seamless UX;
  • fast.

Cons:

  • must infer authenticity from available sensory cues.

Active

Requires challenge or controlled illumination.

Pros:

  • can generate stronger evidence.

Cons:

  • slower;
  • more friction;
  • accessibility concerns.

A payment terminal might use passive PAD by default and step up to active PAD only when risk is elevated.

83.11 APCER and BPCER

Two important ISO PAD metrics:

APCER

Attack Presentation Classification Error Rate.

Roughly: fraction of attack presentations incorrectly classified as bona fide under the defined protocol.

BPCER

Bona Fide Presentation Classification Error Rate.

Roughly: fraction of genuine presentations incorrectly classified as attacks.

Security/UX tradeoff:

very strict PAD
-> APCER may improve
-> BPCER may worsen
-> real customers get rejected

Threshold selection must therefore be risk-based and empirically validated.

83.12 Generalization is the real PAD problem

The most dangerous benchmark failure mode is training and testing on the same attack equipment.

Example:

train attacks:
Printer A
Phone A
Mask material A

test attacks:
Printer A
Phone A
Mask material A

A model can memorize device artifacts.

A serious evaluation should test unseen:

  • displays;
  • printers;
  • materials;
  • lighting;
  • cameras;
  • subjects;
  • locations.

Cross-dataset and cross-device performance is often far more informative than in-domain accuracy.

83.13 PAD for payment should be fused with transaction risk

A useful architecture:

PAD score
face match score
capture quality
device attestation
merchant/terminal state
transaction amount
velocity/history
        |
        v
    risk engine

A marginal PAD result on a low-value familiar-device transaction may trigger recapture, while the same result on a high-value anomalous transaction may require another authenticator.

The key is that PAD contributes evidence; it does not autonomously move money.

84. Face Quality, Multi-Frame Aggregation, and Decision Stability

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous83. Presentation Attack Detection in Depth: Physics, rPPG, Depth, NIR, Replay, Masks, and Injection
This chapter84. Face Quality, Multi-Frame Aggregation, and Decision Stability
Next85. Tracking from First Principles: Kalman Filters, SORT, DeepSORT, ByteTrack, and BoT-SORT

A camera produces a stream, not one sacred frame.

Using a single arbitrary frame throws away temporal redundancy.

84.1 Quality dimensions

Face quality can include:

  • face pixel size;
  • yaw/pitch/roll;
  • blur;
  • focus;
  • occlusion;
  • exposure;
  • contrast;
  • eye visibility;
  • landmark confidence;
  • compression artifacts;
  • motion;
  • sensor saturation.

Quality is task-specific. An image visually attractive to a human may still be poor for recognition.

84.2 Hard quality gates

Example:

face width < 100 px -> reject frame
|yaw| > 35°        -> reject frame
blur score poor    -> reject frame
severe clipping    -> reject frame

The exact thresholds must come from evaluation, not intuition.

84.3 Soft quality weighting

Instead of discarding every imperfect frame, assign a quality weight q_i.

For embeddings e_i:

eagg=∑iqiei∥∑iqiei∥ e_{agg}= \frac{\sum_i q_ie_i} {\left\|\sum_iq_ie_i\right\|}

High-quality frames contribute more.

84.4 Similarity aggregation

Alternative: compare each frame to the reference separately:

si=cos(ei,eref) s_i=\cos(e_i,e_{ref})

Then aggregate:

  • median;
  • trimmed mean;
  • top-k mean;
  • quality-weighted mean.

Avoid naïve max similarity because one anomalously high frame could dominate.

84.5 Temporal consistency

A genuine tracked face should evolve smoothly.

Possible checks:

  • bounding-box motion continuity;
  • landmark continuity;
  • embedding consistency;
  • depth continuity;
  • PAD consistency.

Sudden identity embedding jumps within one continuous track can indicate:

  • tracking switch;
  • occlusion;
  • injected frames;
  • recognition instability.

84.6 Decision hysteresis

Without hysteresis, a score near threshold can flip every frame:

0.621 pass
0.618 fail
0.623 pass
0.617 fail

A stable state machine can require accumulated evidence:

need 5 good frames
within last 8 frames
with stable tracking
and PAD passed

This produces better UX and security than a one-frame threshold.

84.7 Quality and fairness

Quality distributions may differ across:

  • camera placement;
  • height;
  • mobility;
  • skin reflectance under particular illumination;
  • eyewear;
  • cultural clothing;
  • demographic factors.

Therefore a quality gate itself can introduce unequal failure rates even if the recognition network is unchanged.

Evaluate the entire pipeline, not only embedding accuracy.

85. Tracking from First Principles: Kalman Filters, SORT, DeepSORT, ByteTrack, and BoT-SORT

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous84. Face Quality, Multi-Frame Aggregation, and Decision Stability
This chapter85. Tracking from First Principles: Kalman Filters, SORT, DeepSORT, ByteTrack, and BoT-SORT
Next86. Vector Search Deep Dive: IVF, PQ, HNSW, Candidate Generation, and Re-Ranking

SAM2/SAM3 provide powerful mask tracking, but classical tracking remains valuable because a payment terminal often needs something much simpler:

keep a stable identity for the same detected customer across a short video sequence.

85.1 Tracking-by-detection

The common structure:

Frame t
  |
Detector
  |
boxes
  |
associate boxes with existing tracks
  |
update track states

The tracker does not necessarily detect objects itself. It associates detector outputs over time.

85.2 State estimation

A simple box track state might contain:

x=[cx,cy,w,h,vx,vy,vw,vh]T x=[c_x,c_y,w,h,v_x,v_y,v_w,v_h]^T

where:

  • (c_x,c_y) = center;
  • (w,h) = size;
  • v_* = velocities.

The tracker predicts where the box should appear in the next frame.

85.3 Kalman filter

A linear state model:

xt=Fxt−1+wt x_t=Fx_{t-1}+w_t

Measurement:

zt=Hxt+vt z_t=Hx_t+v_t

with process noise w_t and measurement noise v_t.

The Kalman filter alternates:

PREDICT
use motion model to estimate next state

UPDATE
combine prediction with new measurement

Prediction

x̂t|t−1=Fx̂t−1|t−1 \hat{x}_{t|t-1}=F\hat{x}_{t-1|t-1}

Covariance also propagates, increasing uncertainty.

Update

The Kalman gain determines how much to trust measurement versus prediction.

If the detector is noisy, rely more on model prediction.

If state uncertainty is high and measurement is reliable, move strongly toward the measurement.

85.4 Data association

Suppose three existing tracks and three new detections appear.

Build a cost matrix using IoU or motion distance:

             det A   det B   det C
track 1       .1      .8      .9
track 2       .7      .2      .8
track 3       .8      .7      .1

Hungarian assignment finds a globally low-cost one-to-one matching.

Interesting connection:

Hungarian matching appears both in tracking association and DETR training, but the entities being matched are different.

85.5 SORT

SORT combines:

  • a detector;
  • Kalman-filter motion prediction;
  • Hungarian assignment;
  • simple IoU-based association.

The original paper deliberately emphasizes simplicity and speed.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.00763

Weakness:

If two people cross or one disappears behind another, geometry alone may cause identity switches.

85.6 DeepSORT

DeepSORT adds an appearance embedding.

Now association can consider:

motion similarity
+
appearance similarity

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/1703.07402

This helps maintain identity through occlusion because the tracker can recognize that a reappearing box visually resembles the prior track.

Important distinction:

DeepSORT’s appearance embedding is usually a re-identification embedding, not necessarily a legally/security-calibrated face biometric embedding.

Do not automatically reuse one as the other.

85.7 ByteTrack

Many trackers discard low-confidence detections immediately.

ByteTrack’s core idea is to associate almost every detection box, including lower-score boxes, in a second association stage.

Why?

An occluded real person often receives a low detector score.

Throwing it away breaks the trajectory.

ByteTrack uses existing track information to recover true low-score detections while rejecting background.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2110.06864

85.8 BoT-SORT

BoT-SORT combines stronger motion/appearance association and camera-motion compensation.

Primary paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.14651

Camera-motion compensation matters more in mobile cameras than fixed kiosks, but the broader principle remains:

tracking errors arise from both object motion and camera motion.

85.9 Tracking metrics: MOTA, IDF1, and HOTA

Tracking quality is not captured by one number. Three common metrics emphasize different failure modes.

MOTA — Multiple Object Tracking Accuracy

MOTA combines false positives, false negatives, and identity switches relative to the number of ground-truth objects. Historically it became a standard MOT summary metric, but it can be dominated by detector quality and does not isolate identity consistency cleanly.

IDF1

IDF1 measures the F1 score of correctly identified detections over trajectories. It is more directly sensitive to whether the same physical object keeps the same track identity.

For a biometric capture session, this is conceptually important because a track switch can contaminate multi-frame face aggregation even when every individual face detection is geometrically accurate.

HOTA — Higher Order Tracking Accuracy

HOTA was designed to balance detection quality and association quality rather than collapsing everything into one detector-dominated score. It separately exposes how well objects are detected and how consistently detections are associated over time.

For a payment terminal, however, generic MOT benchmark metrics are still proxies. The most useful product-level metric may be:

How often does tracking cause the wrong person's frames
to enter one biometric verification session?

That failure rate should be measured directly.

85.10 When not to use SAM tracking

If the requirement is:

keep one customer's face box stable for 1.5 seconds

then:

face detector + simple Kalman/SORT-style tracker

may be vastly cheaper than a large promptable segmentation model.

SAM becomes attractive when you need:

  • precise masks;
  • arbitrary promptable concepts;
  • rich object segmentation;
  • complex video mask propagation.

Architecture should follow the product requirement, not model prestige.

85.11 Tracking and biometric identity must remain separate

A track ID means:

track_17 = same visual trajectory

It does not mean:

track_17 = bank customer 123456

Only a separately calibrated biometric recognition process can make that identity claim.

A secure pipeline may bind them temporarily:

track_17
  |
  +--> stable face crop stream
  |
  +--> biometric verification
  |
  v
transaction session

but the track identifier itself is not identity evidence.

Connection Bridge — Scaling Identification Introduces a Retrieval System Between the Model and the Decision

For small enrollment sets, you can compare the probe embedding with every template exactly. At large scale, that becomes a search problem. IVF, PQ, HNSW and related ANN techniques trade exactness, memory, and latency to produce a manageable candidate set.

That candidate-generation stage is not the biometric verdict. Its job is to avoid searching everything. A robust architecture can use approximate retrieval to find likely candidates, then re-rank or verify them with stronger exact comparisons and calibrated thresholds. Separating retrieval from decisioning prevents infrastructure optimizations from quietly redefining security policy.

86. Vector Search Deep Dive: IVF, PQ, HNSW, Candidate Generation, and Re-Ranking

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous85. Tracking from First Principles: Kalman Filters, SORT, DeepSORT, ByteTrack, and BoT-SORT
This chapter86. Vector Search Deep Dive: IVF, PQ, HNSW, Candidate Generation, and Re-Ranking
Next87. Edge Inference and Quantization: FP32, FP16, INT8, QAT, ONNX, TensorRT, OpenVINO, and LiteRT

A 1:1 biometric comparison is simple:

probe embedding
      vs
one reference embedding

A 1:N identification problem is different:

probe embedding
      vs
millions of enrolled embeddings

At that point, face recognition becomes partly an information-retrieval problem.

86.1 Exact nearest-neighbor search

Suppose there are N enrolled embeddings of dimension d.

For normalized embeddings, cosine similarity reduces to a dot product:
