si=qTxi s_i=q^Tx_i

Computing all similarities costs roughly:

O(Nd) O(Nd)

If:

N = 10,000,000 identities
d = 512 dimensions

then one probe requires billions of multiply/add operations if searched naïvely.

Modern GPUs can brute-force surprisingly large galleries, but latency, concurrency, memory bandwidth, and cost eventually motivate indexing.

86.2 Approximate Nearest Neighbor

ANN deliberately trades a small amount of recall for a large reduction in search cost.

Instead of guaranteeing the mathematically exact nearest vector, the index aims to return very likely nearest candidates quickly.

The production pattern is often:

ANN candidate generation
       |
       v
top 100 or top 1000
       |
       v
exact similarity re-ranking
       |
       v
biometric threshold + policy

This is safer than treating approximate-index scores as final biometric truth.

86.3 IVF: inverted file indexing

IVF partitions vector space into coarse clusters.

During index construction:

all embeddings
   |
k-means coarse quantizer
   |
cluster 1 list
cluster 2 list
cluster 3 list
...

At query time:

  1. compare query with coarse centroids;
  2. choose the nearest nprobe clusters;
  3. search only vectors inside those clusters.

If there are 4096 coarse clusters, you may search only a small fraction of the gallery.

Tradeoff:

more clusters probed
-> higher recall
-> more compute

86.4 Product Quantization

A 512-dimensional FP32 embedding requires:

512×4=2048 bytes 512\times4=2048\text{ bytes}

For 100 million templates, raw storage alone becomes huge.

Product Quantization splits the vector into sub-vectors:

512-D vector
   |
   +--> dims 1..32
   +--> dims 33..64
   +--> ...

Each subspace has a learned codebook.

Instead of storing every float, store the nearest codeword index for each sub-vector.

This can compress embeddings dramatically.

Distance can be approximated using lookup tables between the query’s sub-vectors and codebook entries.

86.5 IVF-PQ

IVF and PQ combine naturally:

coarse IVF cluster
       |
compressed PQ codes inside cluster

This reduces both search scope and memory footprint.

The cost is approximation error.

For biometric security, always evaluate how compression affects:

  • genuine-score distribution;
  • impostor-score distribution;
  • top-k identification recall;
  • false-match behavior at the target operating threshold.

86.6 HNSW

Hierarchical Navigable Small World indexing treats vectors as nodes in a graph.

Nearby vectors are connected.

Higher layers are sparse long-range navigation graphs; lower layers are denser.

Conceptually:

Layer 3:       A ---------------- M

Layer 2:       A ------ F ------- M
                     /      \
Layer 1:    A-B-C-D-E-F-G-H-I-J-K-L-M

Search begins at the top, greedily navigates toward the query, then descends to denser layers.

This is analogous to entering a city via highways, then arterial roads, then neighborhood streets.

86.7 HNSW parameters

Important conceptual parameters:

M

Number of graph connections per node.

Higher M:

  • more memory;
  • potentially better recall;
  • more search/build work.

efConstruction

How broadly graph construction searches for neighbors.

Higher values generally improve index quality at build-time cost.

efSearch

How many candidate nodes are explored during query.

Higher:

better recall
slower query

86.8 Candidate generation versus biometric decision

This separation is critical:

ANN index says:
"these 100 identities are geometrically nearby"

biometric matcher says:
"candidate X exceeds calibrated similarity threshold"

risk engine says:
"this transaction may proceed / needs step-up"

Do not conflate retrieval recall with biometric acceptance.

86.9 Open-set identification

Closed-set assumption:

the correct identity is definitely in the gallery.

Open-set assumption:

the person may not be enrolled at all.

Payment systems are inherently open-set in many environments. Random bystanders can appear.

Therefore the system must support:

no acceptable identity

not merely return the nearest enrolled person.

Nearest neighbor always returns someone. That does not mean the person matches.

86.10 Gallery growth and false matches

As gallery size grows, the probability of some impostor being unusually similar increases.

This is an extreme-value problem.

Therefore a threshold calibrated for 1:1 verification cannot automatically be reused for 1:N identification at huge scale.

Evaluate using the actual gallery size and population distribution expected in deployment.

86.11 Multi-stage identity search

A safer large-scale design may use:

transaction/account context
       |
reduce candidate population
       |
ANN search
       |
exact re-rank
       |
biometric threshold
       |
second factor / transaction binding

Example candidate narrowing signals might include merchant/account context or an explicit user-presented identifier, provided privacy and security requirements allow it.

The best optimization is often to avoid unconstrained 1:N search entirely.

Connection Bridge — Deployment Changes the Model You Thought You Had

A trained checkpoint is only one representation of the model. Export, graph optimization, operator fusion, reduced precision, calibration, runtime kernels, and hardware scheduling can alter latency and sometimes output distributions. In ordinary vision, a tiny numerical shift may be irrelevant. In biometrics, if that shift occurs near a security threshold, it can change false-match or false-non-match behavior.

So deployment should be treated as part of validation, not as a packaging step after validation. Every material optimization—especially quantization—deserves biometric re-evaluation at the operating points the product actually uses.

87. Edge Inference and Quantization: FP32, FP16, INT8, QAT, ONNX, TensorRT, OpenVINO, and LiteRT

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous86. Vector Search Deep Dive: IVF, PQ, HNSW, Candidate Generation, and Re-Ranking
This chapter87. Edge Inference and Quantization: FP32, FP16, INT8, QAT, ONNX, TensorRT, OpenVINO, and LiteRT
Next88. Compression and Distillation: Pruning, Teacher-Student Learning, and Efficient Biometrics

A research model that runs at 40 ms on an A100 is not automatically deployable in a payment terminal.

Deployment asks different questions:

  • which operators are supported?
  • what precision does the hardware accelerate?
  • how much RAM/VRAM exists?
  • what is cold-start latency?
  • can preprocessing remain zero-copy?
  • can the model be updated safely?

87.1 Numeric precision

FP32

32-bit floating point.

Advantages:

  • wide dynamic range;
  • stable and familiar;
  • easiest baseline.

Costs:

  • larger memory;
  • higher bandwidth;
  • slower on hardware optimized for reduced precision.

FP16

16-bit floating point.

Roughly halves storage/bandwidth compared with FP32 and can exploit tensor-core-like acceleration.

BF16

Also 16 bits, but allocates more exponent range and fewer mantissa bits than FP16.

Useful during training because it preserves FP32-like dynamic range.

INT8

8-bit integer quantization.

Can reduce model size and accelerate inference significantly on supported hardware.

But it requires mapping real values into a discrete integer range.

87.2 Quantization equation

A common affine quantization form is:

xreal=s(xq−z) x_{real}=s(x_q-z)

where:

  • x_q = integer value;
  • s = scale;
  • z = zero point.

Equivalently:

xq≈round(xreals+z) x_q\approx round\left(\frac{x_{real}}{s}+z\right)

Current TensorRT documentation describes INT8 Q/DQ using clipping, scaling, and integer rounding; LiteRT similarly documents per-tensor/per-axis integer representations.

TensorRT quantization reference: https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/tensorrt/latest/inference-library/quantized-types-schemes.html

LiteRT post-training integer quantization: https://developers.google.com/ai-edge/litert/models/post_training_integer_quant

87.3 Per-tensor versus per-channel quantization

Per-tensor

One scale for the entire tensor.

Simple but coarse.

Per-channel / per-axis

Separate scales for weight channels.

Useful because convolution filters can have very different numeric ranges.

Per-channel weight quantization often preserves accuracy better.

87.4 Post-training quantization

PTQ starts with a trained floating-point model.

Then calibration data estimates activation ranges.

FP32 model
   |
representative calibration dataset
   |
choose scales / zero points
   |
INT8 model

Advantages:

  • simple;
  • no retraining;
  • fast experimentation.

Risk:

  • sensitive layers may lose accuracy.

87.5 Quantization-aware training

QAT simulates quantization during training.

The forward path includes fake quantization effects, while gradients use approximations such as straight-through estimators.

training sees quantization noise
       |
network adapts weights
       |
final real INT8 deployment

QAT is useful when PTQ causes unacceptable degradation.

87.6 Calibration dataset quality

For PTQ, calibration data should represent real activation distributions.

If calibration uses only bright frontal faces but deployment includes:

  • dark scenes;
  • NIR variation;
  • severe pose;
  • multiple faces;

activation ranges may be poorly estimated.

Quantization therefore has a data-design problem too.

87.7 Quantization and biometric thresholds

This is crucial.

Suppose FP32 embeddings yield similarity:

same identity: 0.631

After INT8 quantization:

same image: 0.619

A tiny embedding drift can alter decision behavior near threshold.

Therefore do not validate only top-1 accuracy after quantization.

Recompute:

  • FMR;
  • FNMR;
  • TAR at target FMR;
  • threshold calibration;
  • demographic slices;
  • PAD metrics if the PAD model is quantized.

87.8 ONNX

ONNX defines a portable graph format composed of:

  • inputs;
  • outputs;
  • nodes/operators;
  • initializers/weights;
  • attributes;
  • opset versions.

Official introduction: https://onnx.ai/onnx/intro/

Typical deployment flow:

PyTorch
  |
export
  v
ONNX graph
  |
  +--> ONNX Runtime
  +--> TensorRT
  +--> OpenVINO
  +--> vendor compiler

ONNX is not itself an accelerator. It is an interchange representation and operator specification ecosystem.

87.9 Opset compatibility

An ONNX operator can evolve.

The opset version defines operator semantics.

A model export can fail at deployment because:

training framework emits operator version X
runtime supports only Y

This is why production ML needs tested export pipelines, not ad-hoc notebook conversion.

87.10 TensorRT

TensorRT compiles neural-network graphs for NVIDIA GPUs.

It can:

  • select optimized kernels;
  • fuse operations;
  • use reduced precision;
  • optimize memory reuse;
  • specialize engines for shapes/profiles.

An exported TensorRT engine is often hardware/version specific, so reproducible engine builds and compatibility management matter.

87.11 OpenVINO

OpenVINO targets Intel CPU/GPU/NPU-style deployment environments.

It supports graph optimization and low-precision inference, including INT8 models produced with quantization tooling such as NNCF.

This can be attractive for x86 kiosk terminals without discrete NVIDIA GPUs.

87.12 LiteRT / mobile runtimes

LiteRT—evolved from TensorFlow Lite—targets constrained mobile/edge inference and supports quantized execution paths.

For smartphone-based biometric activation, this class of runtime matters more than server-oriented accelerators.

87.13 CoreML and platform-native runtimes

Apple platforms can execute models through CoreML/ANE paths.

Android devices may use LiteRT delegates or vendor NN APIs.

The architectural lesson:

edge inference is a compiler/runtime/hardware problem as much as a neural-network problem.

87.14 Operator fusion

At inference, a graph may contain:

Conv -> BatchNorm -> SiLU

A compiler can sometimes fuse these into a smaller number of kernels.

This reduces:

  • memory round trips;
  • launch overhead;
  • intermediate tensors.

Architecture modules that look equal in FLOPs can have very different real latency depending on fusion support.

87.15 Why FLOPs are not latency

Two models with 10 GFLOPs can run very differently.

Latency depends on:

  • memory bandwidth;
  • kernel fusion;
  • tensor shapes;
  • operator implementation;
  • batch size;
  • hardware occupancy;
  • cache behavior;
  • dynamic control flow.

Always benchmark on the actual target device.

87.16 Edge versus cloud biometric inference

Edge

Pros:

  • lower network latency;
  • stronger privacy potential;
  • sensor and inference can share trust boundary;
  • continues during network degradation.

Cons:

  • limited compute;
  • harder model updates;
  • heterogeneous hardware.

Cloud

Pros:

  • centralized model management;
  • large compute;
  • easier fleet-wide updates.

Cons:

  • raw/derived biometric transmission;
  • network dependency;
  • larger centralized attack surface;
  • sensor-to-verifier integrity becomes critical.

NIST’s current SP 800-63B guidance prefers local biometric comparison over central comparison in its applicable authentication context because central verification creates larger-scale attack concerns.

That is not automatically a legal requirement for every payment system, but it is a sound architecture principle to evaluate.

88. Compression and Distillation: Pruning, Teacher-Student Learning, and Efficient Biometrics

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous87. Edge Inference and Quantization: FP32, FP16, INT8, QAT, ONNX, TensorRT, OpenVINO, and LiteRT
This chapter88. Compression and Distillation: Pruning, Teacher-Student Learning, and Efficient Biometrics
Next89. Training Data Engineering: Identity Splits, Leakage, Hard Negatives, Domain Shift, and Synthetic Data

Quantization changes numeric representation. Compression more broadly asks:

Can we reduce model cost while preserving useful behavior?

88.1 Unstructured pruning

Set individual small-magnitude weights to zero.

Example:

[0.24, 0.001, -0.73, 0.0002]
          |
prune     |
          v
[0.24, 0, -0.73, 0]

This creates sparsity.

But generic hardware may not run sparse tensors faster unless sparsity has a supported pattern.

88.2 Structured pruning

Remove entire:

  • channels;
  • filters;
  • attention heads;
  • blocks.

This changes dense tensor shapes, so ordinary hardware can benefit directly.

The challenge is deciding which structures are expendable without harming accuracy.

88.3 Knowledge distillation

A large teacher network guides a smaller student.

large teacher
  ResNet-100
       |
       | soft targets / embeddings / feature maps
       v
small student
  MobileFaceNet

The student can learn richer information than hard identity labels alone provide.

88.4 Logit distillation

For classification, teacher soft probabilities encode class relationships:

teacher:
identity A 0.80
identity B 0.15
identity C 0.04
...

The student learns this distribution, often using a temperature-scaled KL-divergence objective.

88.5 Embedding distillation

For face recognition, it can be more natural to align student and teacher embeddings:

Ldistill=∥fs(x)−ft(x)∥22 L_{distill}=\|f_s(x)-f_t(x)\|_2^2

or use angular/cosine objectives.

88.6 Feature-map distillation

Intermediate teacher representations can supervise student feature maps after projection.

This is useful when the student needs to mimic not only final output but internal spatial reasoning.

88.7 Distilling a PAD model

A heavy multimodal teacher could use RGB + depth + NIR during training, while a deployment student uses fewer modalities.

However, the student cannot magically recover physical information absent from its sensors.

Distillation transfers statistical knowledge, not new physical observability.

88.8 Compression validation

After any compression:

Do not ask only:
"Did accuracy drop 0.2%?"

Ask:
"Did security operating points move?"

For biometrics:

  • similarity distributions;
  • FMR/FNMR;
  • TAR at low FMR;
  • demographic slices;
  • low-quality subsets;
  • attack subsets;
  • latency tails.

A compressed model is a new model release and deserves full validation.

89. Training Data Engineering: Identity Splits, Leakage, Hard Negatives, Domain Shift, and Synthetic Data

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous88. Compression and Distillation: Pruning, Teacher-Student Learning, and Efficient Biometrics
This chapter89. Training Data Engineering: Identity Splits, Leakage, Hard Negatives, Domain Shift, and Synthetic Data
Next90. Adversarial ML and Pipeline Attacks Against Biometric Payment

In biometric ML, data design can invalidate an entire experiment even when the model code is perfect.

89.1 Identity-disjoint splits

Suppose one person has 100 photographs.

Bad split:

80 photos -> train
10 photos -> validation
10 photos -> test

The test set contains identities seen in training.

For evaluating generalizable face embedding learning, this may create optimistic results depending on the task.

A stronger protocol is often identity-disjoint:

train identities       !=
validation identities  !=
test identities

This tests whether the model learned transferable identity representation rather than memorizing known people.

89.2 Capture-session leakage

Even if identities are correctly split for certain experiments, other leakage can occur.

Example:

same camera
same background
same burst sequence
same compression artifacts

A model may exploit environmental shortcuts.

For PAD this is especially dangerous: it may recognize the attack device instead of attack physics.

89.3 Near-duplicate leakage

Video frames milliseconds apart are nearly identical.

Randomly splitting frames into train/test creates artificial performance.

Split by:

  • subject;
  • session;
  • device;
  • environment;
  • attack instrument;

depending on what generalization you need to measure.

89.4 Hard-negative mining in face recognition

Most random identity pairs are easy.

If embeddings are already well separated:

Razi vs random stranger
cosine = 0.05

provides little information near the decision boundary.

Hard negatives are different identities that appear close in embedding space:

Razi vs visually similar stranger
cosine = 0.48

Training on these examples improves discrimination where mistakes actually happen.

89.5 Hard positives

Same identity can also be hard:

  • large age gap;
  • heavy pose;
  • glasses/no glasses;
  • facial hair change;
  • low light;
  • low resolution.

A good dataset contains intra-class diversity, not just many near-duplicate images.

89.6 Label noise

Face datasets can contain:

  • wrong identity labels;
  • duplicate identities under different names;
  • celebrity lookalikes merged incorrectly;
  • non-face crops.

Margin-based losses can aggressively fit incorrect labels, so cleaning matters.

Techniques include:

  • clustering;
  • teacher-model consistency checks;
  • manual review of suspicious clusters;
  • robust loss design.

89.7 Long-tail identity distribution

Web-scale face datasets often have highly uneven images per identity.

Some identities have thousands of images; others have a handful.

This affects classifier weight learning and sampling.

Balanced identity sampling can avoid training being dominated by a few frequent identities.

89.8 Domain shift

Train:

high-quality web photos

Deploy:

fixed kiosk camera
fluorescent mall lighting
motion blur
fisheye perspective

This is domain shift.

A model with excellent benchmark performance may fail operationally.

89.9 Domain adaptation strategy

Possible responses:

  • collect target-domain data;
  • fine-tune carefully;
  • use augmentation approximating target conditions;
  • self-supervised adaptation;
  • camera-specific normalization/calibration;
  • robust quality gates.

But adaptation must not destroy generalization or create demographic regressions.

89.10 Augmentation

Useful transformations may include:

  • brightness/contrast;
  • color jitter;
  • blur;
  • compression;
  • random crop;
  • mild perspective;
  • occlusion;
  • synthetic motion blur;
  • sensor noise.

The guiding rule:

augmentation should represent plausible deployment variation, not arbitrary visual corruption.

If a transformation changes identity semantics or creates physically impossible artifacts, it may hurt.

89.11 Synthetic face data

Synthetic data can expand:

  • pose coverage;
  • lighting variation;
  • attack simulation;
  • privacy-preserving experimentation.

But synthetic generators have their own biases and artifacts.

A PAD model can accidentally learn “synthetic-generator fingerprints.”

Synthetic data should supplement, not automatically replace, high-quality real evaluation data.

89.12 Dataset versioning

Every model release should record:

dataset version
subject count
identity distribution
capture devices
split rules
attack species
augmentation config
label-cleaning version

Without this, model comparisons become scientifically weak.

Connection Bridge — Attackers Target the Cheapest Assumption, Not the Most Impressive Model

Once the full pipeline exists, an attacker does not need to defeat ArcFace mathematically if it is easier to inject a precomputed embedding, replace a camera feed, tamper with thresholds, replay a signed-looking message, or exploit an enrollment weakness. This is why threat modeling must span model, sensor, runtime, transport, storage, policy, and human process.

The next template-protection chapter follows naturally: when a biometric reference is long-lived and non-secret in the human sense—you show your face publicly—security has to come from protected transformations, keys, trusted execution, revocability strategies, and transaction binding rather than pretending the biometric itself is a password.

90. Adversarial ML and Pipeline Attacks Against Biometric Payment

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous89. Training Data Engineering: Identity Splits, Leakage, Hard Negatives, Domain Shift, and Synthetic Data
This chapter90. Adversarial ML and Pipeline Attacks Against Biometric Payment
Next91. Biometric Template Protection and Cryptographic Binding

A biometric payment system is attacked as a system, not as an isolated neural network.

90.1 Evasion attacks

An attacker perturbs input to alter model behavior.

Examples:

  • adversarial glasses;
  • printed patterns;
  • patches;
  • imperceptible digital perturbations in an injected stream.

The exact threat differs depending on whether the attacker controls physical presentation or the digital pipeline.

90.2 Adversarial examples

For classifier f(x), seek perturbation δ such that:

f(x+δ)≠f(x) f(x+\delta)\neq f(x)

while keeping δ small under some norm or physical constraint.

Biometric attacks can target:

  • face detection;
  • landmark alignment;
  • embedding similarity;
  • PAD.

90.3 Embedding attacks

An attacker may optimize an image so its embedding approaches a target identity:

minx∥f(x)−etarget∥ \min_x \|f(x)-e_{target}\|

Physical realizability makes this harder, but digital injection drastically expands the threat.

90.4 Model extraction

If an API exposes too much information, an attacker can query it repeatedly to approximate behavior or decision boundaries.

Defenses include:

  • rate limiting;
  • restricted score exposure;
  • monitoring query patterns;
  • keeping internal embeddings/private thresholds inaccessible.

90.5 Template inversion

A face embedding is not a raw photograph, but that does not make it harmless.

Research has shown that learned representations can leak reconstructive or identity information depending on model and attacker capability.

Therefore embeddings should be treated as sensitive biometric data.

90.6 Database compromise

If templates are centrally stored:

biometric DB compromise

can create long-lived harm because biometrics cannot simply be reset like passwords.

This motivates encryption, segmentation, hardware-backed keys, cancellable transforms, and minimizing centralized raw biometric storage.

90.7 Replay at protocol level

Even a genuine biometric result can be replayed if it is not bound to a fresh transaction.

Bad design:

"Razi verified = true"

sent as a reusable token.

Better:

biometric/authenticator result
cryptographically bound to:
- transaction nonce
- merchant/terminal
- amount or transaction details
- timestamp/expiry

This prevents a previous successful authentication from authorizing an unrelated payment.

90.8 Sensor injection

Threat:

attacker compromises OS/driver
        |
feeds synthetic frames
        |
PAD sees perfect-looking live video

Defenses can include:

  • sensor authentication;
  • secure camera paths;
  • signed/attested capture metadata;
  • TEE-backed processing;
  • challenge signals generated below the compromised application layer.

NIST SP 800-63B explicitly calls out sensor/endpoint integrity and injection-attack concerns in biometric authentication guidance.

90.9 Embedding injection

Even stronger attack:

skip camera
skip detector
skip PAD
inject target embedding directly

Therefore services must authenticate the provenance of intermediate data.

The embedding service should not accept arbitrary client-supplied embeddings unless the architecture intentionally trusts that client and can attest it.

90.10 Model-file tampering

Replace PAD model with one that always returns bona_fide=1.

Defenses:

  • signed model artifacts;
  • verified boot;
  • measured boot / attestation;
  • immutable deployment manifests;
  • rollback protection.

90.11 Threshold tampering

An attacker does not need to steal the model if they can change:

match threshold 0.55 -> 0.10

Security-sensitive configuration must be protected and audited like code.

90.12 Supply-chain attacks

Risks include:

  • poisoned model dependencies;
  • malicious pretrained weights;
  • compromised build pipelines;
  • unsafe serialization formats;
  • dependency confusion.

Model provenance belongs in software supply-chain security.

90.13 Data poisoning

An attacker who can influence enrollment or training data may insert samples that create a backdoor or corrupt identity boundaries.

Enrollment is therefore a high-trust ceremony, not just “upload a selfie.”

90.14 Threat-model matrix

Layer Example attack Example control
Sensor replay/virtual camera authenticated sensor, secure capture path
Detection adversarial patch robust training, scene policy
PAD unseen mask/display multimodal PAD, attack testing
Embedding adversarial impersonation calibrated thresholds, quality gates
Template DB theft encryption, HSM-backed keys, segmentation
API score probing rate limit, minimize score exposure
Config threshold modification signed config, RBAC, audit
Protocol replay verified session nonce/transaction binding
Payment account takeover independent risk + MFA controls

The important lesson is defense-in-depth.

91. Biometric Template Protection and Cryptographic Binding

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous90. Adversarial ML and Pipeline Attacks Against Biometric Payment
This chapter91. Biometric Template Protection and Cryptographic Binding
Next92. Latency, Throughput, Observability, Drift, and Production SLOs

A password can be changed.

Your face cannot be reissued easily.

That makes biometric-template protection unusually important.

91.1 Raw image versus template

A biometric system may store:

raw enrollment images
aligned crops
embeddings/templates
quality metadata
model/version metadata

Minimize what is stored.

Raw images are useful for reprocessing and audit but create much higher privacy/security exposure.

If raw images are not required, do not keep them by default merely because storage is cheap.

91.2 Encryption at rest

Templates should be encrypted using modern authenticated encryption.

Keys should not live beside the encrypted database in plaintext.

A stronger pattern:

application
   |
requests decrypt operation
   |
KMS/HSM controls key use
   |
plaintext exists only in bounded processing context

91.3 HSM

A Hardware Security Module protects cryptographic keys and performs sensitive operations inside a hardened boundary.

HSMs are particularly valuable for:

  • root/key-encryption keys;
  • signing transaction assertions;
  • wrapping data-encryption keys;
  • audit-controlled cryptographic operations.

An HSM does not magically protect a plaintext embedding after the application decrypts it. System design must minimize plaintext exposure too.

91.4 Envelope encryption

Common pattern:

Template
  |
encrypt with Data Encryption Key (DEK)
  |
Encrypted template

DEK
  |
wrapped with Key Encryption Key (KEK) in KMS/HSM

Each template or tenant can have independent data keys while the root key remains inside hardware-controlled infrastructure.

91.5 Trusted Execution Environments

A TEE isolates code and data from the normal OS to some degree.

Possible uses:

  • local biometric comparison;
  • protected template storage;
  • transaction-signing key access;
  • attested execution.

But TEEs have platform-specific threat models and have experienced side-channel vulnerabilities historically. Treat them as one security boundary, not magic.

91.6 Cancellable biometrics

A cancellable biometric transforms the template with a revocable transformation.

If compromised:

change transform parameters
-> generate a different protected representation

The ideal properties are:

  • difficult to invert;
  • unlinkable across applications;
  • preserves matching utility;
  • revocable.

This is attractive because the underlying face cannot be changed.

91.7 Biometric cryptosystems

Research includes fuzzy commitment, fuzzy vault, and other constructions that bind cryptographic material to noisy biometric measurements.

The challenge is fundamental:

cryptographic keys require exact bits
biometrics are noisy measurements

So error-tolerant helper data is needed.

These systems are more complex than ordinary template comparison and must be evaluated carefully for leakage and practicality.

91.8 Application unlinkability

If the same raw embedding is shared across many services, it becomes a cross-service identifier.

Prefer architecture where different relying contexts cannot trivially correlate the same biometric template.

Techniques may include:

  • application-specific protected templates;
  • local verification;
  • device-bound authenticators;
  • derived cancellable representations.

91.9 Transaction binding

Biometric verification should authorize a specific cryptographic action, not emit a generic permanent identity flag.

A strong pattern resembles:

1. terminal creates transaction challenge/nonce
2. user biometric activates trusted authenticator
3. authenticator signs challenge + transaction context
4. backend verifies signature and freshness
5. payment risk/authorization evaluates signed assertion

Now the biometric is an activation factor for a cryptographic authenticator rather than the sole bearer credential.

This aligns with the security principle in NIST SP 800-63B that a biometric characteristic is not an authenticator by itself and should be paired with a physical authenticator in the authentication context covered by that standard.

91.10 Why this is stronger than central face search

Central face search:

face -> central embedding match -> identity -> pay

has a very large biometric trust surface.

Device/terminal-bound cryptographic activation:

biometric locally unlocks non-exportable key
key signs exact payment challenge

creates a stronger proof of possession and transaction intent.

The tradeoff is product UX and provisioning complexity.

Connection Bridge — Production Quality Is a Time Series, Not a Benchmark Number

Offline accuracy can tell you whether a model was good on a test set. A payment service needs to know whether the system remains good tomorrow. Camera firmware changes, lighting environments shift, devices age, fraud behavior adapts, model versions roll out, and network conditions vary. Observability turns the architecture from a static diagram into something measurable over time.

The difficult part is doing this without violating biometric privacy. Production telemetry should emphasize quality distributions, score histograms, error classes, latency, device state, decision outcomes, and carefully governed samples—not casual logging of raw faces. SLOs make the reliability contract explicit; drift detection tells you when the assumptions behind those SLOs are changing.

92. Latency, Throughput, Observability, Drift, and Production SLOs

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous91. Biometric Template Protection and Cryptographic Binding
This chapter92. Latency, Throughput, Observability, Drift, and Production SLOs
Next93. One Payment, End to End: A Worked Transaction with Tensors, Timing, Decisions, and Failure Modes

A payment system is interactive infrastructure.

Average model latency is not enough.

92.1 Latency decomposition

Example budget:

camera exposure/capture         15 ms
frame transfer                   5 ms
face detector                    7 ms
landmarks + alignment            2 ms
PAD                              18 ms
face embedding                   6 ms
match/retrieval                  4 ms
risk-engine request             25 ms
payment authorization network  180 ms
--------------------------------------
nominal total                  262 ms

This is illustrative, not a target.

The important practice is measuring each stage separately.

92.2 p50, p95, p99

If 99 transactions are fast and one takes 5 seconds, average latency may still look fine.

Percentiles expose tail behavior.

Example:

p50 = 280 ms
p95 = 620 ms
p99 = 1.9 s

For user experience, p95/p99 can matter more than mean.

92.3 Pipeline versus sequential sum

Not all stages need to run sequentially.

While tracking frame t, preprocessing of frame t+1 can begin.

Some risk queries can run concurrently with biometric aggregation.

Pipeline design can reduce end-to-end latency without making individual models faster.

92.4 Batching

Cloud inference benefits from batching because GPUs process large tensors efficiently.

But batching adds waiting time:

request arrives
wait for batch
execute fast

Interactive payment flows often need small dynamic batches or no batching at edge.

Throughput optimization and latency optimization are not identical.

92.5 Cold start

A model may need to:

  • load weights;
  • compile kernels;
  • allocate GPU memory;
  • initialize runtime engine.

If terminals sleep aggressively, cold start can dominate first-transaction latency.

Mitigations:

  • warm resident process;
  • serialized optimized engine;
  • keep-alive;
  • staged initialization at kiosk boot.

92.6 Observability layers

You need metrics for:

Sensor

  • frame rate;
  • exposure;
  • dropped frames;
  • camera disconnects.

Detection

  • face-present rate;
  • faces per frame;
  • box confidence;
  • face pixel size.

Quality

  • blur distribution;
  • pose distribution;
  • rejection reason.

PAD

  • score distribution;
  • attack/bona-fide decisions;
  • retry rate.

Recognition

  • genuine-like score distribution;
  • failure-to-enroll;
  • failure-to-acquire;
  • match retries.

System

  • stage latency;
  • GPU/CPU utilization;
  • memory;
  • queue depth;
  • error codes.

Business/risk

  • step-up rate;
  • abandonment;
  • fraud outcomes;
  • chargeback linkage where legally/operationally appropriate.

92.7 Do not log raw biometrics casually

Observability can become a privacy leak.

Bad:

"for debugging, store every face crop"

Better:

  • structured numeric quality metrics;
  • anonymized model version;
  • hashed transaction/session IDs;
  • bounded, access-controlled forensic capture only under explicit policy.

92.8 Drift

Model drift may appear as:

average face size declines
PAD scores shift
match scores shift
failure-to-acquire rises

Causes:

  • new camera firmware;
  • lens degradation;
  • store lighting changes;
  • demographic/population change;
  • seasonal clothing;
  • model/runtime update;
  • compression pipeline change.

Monitoring distributions can reveal failures before aggregate fraud metrics explode.

92.9 Data drift versus concept drift

Data drift

Input distribution changed.

Example: new terminal camera.

Concept drift

Relationship between input and desired output changed.

For biometrics, identity concept itself is relatively stable, but attack strategies evolve. PAD experiences a form of adversarial concept drift: attackers invent new instruments.

92.10 Model-version observability

Every decision should be attributable to:

face-detector version
PAD version
recognition version
runtime/quantization version
threshold-policy version
risk-model version

Otherwise an incident cannot be reproduced.

92.11 Shadow deployment

Before activating a new model:

old model -> makes real decision
new model -> runs silently

Compare:

  • latency;
  • score distributions;
  • disagreements;
  • demographic slices;
  • failure rates.

This is safer than immediately replacing a security-critical biometric model.

92.12 Canary rollout

Deploy to a small fraction of terminals first.

Observe.

Then gradually expand.

Rollback must be fast and cryptographically controlled.

92.13 SLOs should be end-to-end

A component SLO such as:

face embedding p99 < 10 ms

is useful but insufficient.

A user cares about:

transaction ready-to-authorize p99
successful capture rate
false rejection / retry burden
security outcome

Optimize the system, not one model benchmark.

Connection Bridge — The Whole Story in One Transaction

This chapter is where all the branches reconverge. The value of the walkthrough is not the exact model choice; it is seeing how representations change as the transaction moves through the system: photons become pixels, pixels become feature maps, a face becomes aligned pixels, aligned pixels become an embedding, the embedding becomes a similarity measurement, and that measurement becomes only one input into risk and authorization.

While reading it, repeatedly ask: what is the type of evidence at this boundary, how trustworthy is it, and what happens if it is wrong? That question is the connective tissue of the entire book.

93. One Payment, End to End: A Worked Transaction with Tensors, Timing, Decisions, and Failure Modes

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous92. Latency, Throughput, Observability, Drift, and Production SLOs
This chapter93. One Payment, End to End: A Worked Transaction with Tensors, Timing, Decisions, and Failure Modes
Next94. How to Read Papers, Architecture Diagrams, YAML, and Source Code Without Guessing

This chapter connects all previous components into one concrete transaction.

The numbers are illustrative architecture examples, not recommended production thresholds or latency guarantees. A real system must calibrate them empirically using its hardware, user population, threat model, payment rules, and applicable regulation.

The scenario:

A customer stands at a biometric payment terminal. The system already knows which account or payment instrument is being claimed through a QR, wallet session, card tap, device-bound token, or other transaction context. Face biometrics are used as part of verification, not as an unconstrained global identity search.

This is intentionally a 1:1 verification design because it is easier to secure and reason about than “walk up and search every customer in the database.”

93.1 Stage 0 — Transaction intent exists before face recognition

A dangerous architecture begins with:

camera sees face
   |
find identity
   |
pay from that person's account

A stronger architecture begins with a transaction/session:

merchant/terminal
      |
creates transaction
      |
amount + merchant + nonce + expiry
      |
customer claims/binds account or authenticator
      |
biometric verifies/activates that claim

This separates:

"Who is this face most similar to?"

from:

"Does the person authorizing THIS transaction match the enrolled biometric bound to THIS authenticator/account?"

That distinction dramatically reduces identification ambiguity.

93.2 Transaction challenge

Backend creates something conceptually like:

{
  "transaction_id": "T-...",
  "merchant_id": "M-...",
  "amount": 125000,
  "currency": "IDR",
  "nonce": "random-128-bit-value",
  "expires_at": "...",
  "terminal_id": "..."
}

The values shown are conceptual only.

The nonce prevents replay.

The biometric operation should ultimately be cryptographically bound to this specific challenge or to an authenticator action that is itself bound to it.

93.3 Stage 1 — Camera capture

Assume camera frame:

1920 × 1080 RGB
8 bits/channel
30 FPS

Raw tensor layout after acquisition may be:

HWC = [1080, 1920, 3]
uint8

The runtime may convert to:

CHW = [3, 1080, 1920]

or directly process a hardware-native image buffer.

Failure modes

  • camera unavailable;
  • dropped frames;
  • severe underexposure;
  • lens obstruction;
  • injected/virtual camera;
  • stale replay frame;
  • unsynchronized RGB/depth feeds.

Security check

Before trusting content, the system should establish what it can about sensor provenance and endpoint integrity.

Pixels are evidence only if the capture path is trustworthy enough for the risk being authorized.

93.4 Stage 2 — Preprocessing for detection

Suppose a face detector expects 640×640.

The 1920×1080 image is letterboxed rather than naïvely stretched:

1920x1080
   |
scale preserving aspect ratio
   |
640x360 content
   |
pad top/bottom
   |
640x640 detector input

Tensor:

[1, 3, 640, 640]

Normalize, for example:

x′=x/255 x'=x/255

if required by the detector.

The exact normalization must match training.

93.5 Stage 3 — Face/person localization

Possible detector:

  • SCRFD;
  • RetinaFace;
  • specialized YOLO face detector;
  • another validated face detector.

Suppose it returns:

face box:
x1 = 711
y1 = 212
x2 = 1230
y2 = 854
confidence = 0.996

and landmarks:

left_eye
right_eye
nose
left_mouth
right_mouth

Quality signal

Face dimensions:

width  = 519 px
height = 642 px

This is likely enough spatial detail for recognition, subject to blur/exposure/pose.

Multi-person policy

Suppose detector sees three faces:

Face A -> centered in transaction zone
Face B -> behind customer
Face C -> edge of frame

The terminal needs a transaction zone policy.

Do not simply choose highest confidence.

Possible signals:

  • geometric position;
  • track continuity;
  • depth/proximity;
  • explicit user positioning;
  • device interaction location.

If ambiguity remains, stop rather than silently selecting a bystander.

93.6 Stage 4 — Tracking

Create:

track_id = 17

Kalman/SORT/ByteTrack-style tracking maintains the target across frames.

State might be:

xt=[cx,cy,w,h,vx,vy,…] x_t=[c_x,c_y,w,h,v_x,v_y,\ldots]

The purpose is not biometric identity.

It is to guarantee that subsequent quality, PAD, and embedding frames belong to the same visual subject.

Failure

If track 17 swaps to the person behind the customer, later biometric aggregation becomes contaminated.

Therefore embedding consistency can also act as a track-quality signal.

93.7 Stage 5 — Face quality gate

For each frame, estimate:

face_size
blur
pose yaw/pitch/roll
exposure
occlusion
landmark confidence

Example:

frame 101:
face width     520 px
blur quality   high
|yaw|          8°
|pitch|        4°
landmark conf  0.99
-> accept

frame 102:
motion blur severe
-> reject

frame 103:
face width     525 px
|yaw|          11°
-> accept

The system does not force every frame through recognition.

93.8 Stage 6 — Alignment

Using five landmarks, estimate a similarity transform mapping detected face geometry to canonical coordinates.

A 2D similarity transform can be written:

[x′y′]=sR[xy]+t \begin{bmatrix} x'\\y'\end{bmatrix} = sR \begin{bmatrix}x\\y\end{bmatrix}+t

where:

  • s = scale;
  • R = rotation matrix;
  • t = translation.

Canonical output may be:

112 × 112 × 3

for a common face-recognition setup.

Tensor:

[1, 3, 112, 112]

The face network now receives eyes/nose/mouth in consistent approximate positions.

93.9 Stage 7 — PAD

Assume multimodal capture:

RGB crop
NIR crop
depth crop
short temporal window

PAD model returns multiple internal signals or one fused score.

Illustrative:

RGB texture       0.94 bona fide
NIR material      0.97
Depth geometry    0.99
Temporal/rPPG     0.86
Fusion            0.965

Do not treat those numbers as universal probabilities unless the system is explicitly calibrated that way.

PAD outcome categories

Better than simple boolean:

PASS
RECAPTURE
STEP_UP
DENY
SENSOR_INTEGRITY_ERROR

This preserves information about why verification failed.

93.10 Stage 8 — Face embedding

Aligned crop passes through recognition backbone:

[1,3,112,112]
   |
IResNet-100 / MobileFaceNet / other validated backbone
   |
512-D vector

Output before normalization:

z∈ℝ512 z\in\mathbb{R}^{512}

L2 normalize:

e=z∥z∥2 e=\frac{z}{\|z\|_2}

Now:

∥e∥2=1 \|e\|_2=1

93.11 Stage 9 — Multi-frame embedding aggregation

Suppose accepted frames produce:

e101, e103, e104, e106, e107

Quality weights:

q101 = .95
q103 = .91
q104 = .97
q106 = .88
q107 = .96

Aggregate:

eprobe=normalize(∑iqiei) e_{probe}=normalize\left(\sum_iq_ie_i\right)

This reduces one-frame noise.

93.12 Stage 10 — Retrieve enrolled reference

Because this is 1:1 verification, account/authenticator context provides the reference template.

reference_template = e_ref

No global ANN search is necessary.

This is a major architectural simplification.

93.13 Stage 11 — Similarity

For normalized vectors:

s=eprobeTeref s=e_{probe}^Te_{ref}

Suppose:

similarity = 0.67

Whether that is a match depends entirely on the calibrated model, dataset, preprocessing, operating point, and deployment population.

Never copy a threshold from a blog post.

93.14 Stage 12 — Biometric decision object

Instead of returning:

true

return structured evidence:

{
  "match_score": 0.67,
  "quality_summary": "high",
  "pad_result": "pass",
  "stable_frames": 5,
  "track_integrity": "stable",
  "sensor_attested": true,
  "face_model_version": "...",
  "pad_model_version": "..."
}

Again, values are illustrative.

This gives the risk engine context.

93.15 Stage 13 — Risk policy

Inputs may include:

biometric evidence
transaction amount
merchant risk
account status
terminal integrity
velocity
recent failed attempts
known device/authenticator
geographic consistency

Possible policy:

LOW RISK + strong biometric
-> proceed

MEDIUM RISK
-> require explicit device confirmation/PIN

HIGH RISK or PAD anomaly
-> deny / alternate flow

The policy should be explainable and auditable.

93.16 Stage 14 — Cryptographic authorization

Best case: the biometric activates a cryptographic authenticator.

verified biometric
      |
unlocks/activates private key in secure hardware
      |
sign(transaction nonce + amount + merchant + context)
      |
backend verifies signature

Now authorization proves more than facial similarity.

It proves access to a bound cryptographic key activated under biometric policy.

93.17 Stage 15 — Payment authorization

Only after authentication/risk conditions are satisfied does payment infrastructure process authorization.

The AI stack should never directly call:

transfer_money()

on the basis of a face score.

A proper separation:

AI produces evidence
authentication binds identity/intent
risk evaluates context
payment service enforces authorization

93.18 End-to-end latency example

Illustrative:

Stage Latency
sensor capture 15 ms
detector 7 ms
tracking/quality 2 ms
alignment 1 ms
PAD 15 ms
embedding 5 ms
comparison <1 ms
risk/auth service 25 ms
payment network 180 ms
nominal compute/network path ~250 ms

But multi-frame evidence may intentionally require several frames over 150–300 ms.

Therefore “model inference latency” is not equal to user-perceived transaction latency.

93.19 Failure containment table

Failure Unsafe behavior Safer behavior
detector finds no face lower threshold recapture / alternate authentication
two faces ambiguous pick highest confidence require clear transaction-zone subject
poor quality still match recapture
PAD uncertain ignore PAD step-up / deny based on policy
embedding near threshold round upward step-up
sensor attestation fails trust pixels anyway treat capture as untrusted
network timeout authorize optimistically fail according to payment risk policy
model unavailable skip biometric silently alternate authenticated path
track switch aggregate all frames reset biometric session
config mismatch use default threshold block incompatible model/policy versions

93.20 Why the architecture is a chain of uncertainty

Each stage produces uncertainty:

detector confidence
landmark uncertainty
quality uncertainty
PAD uncertainty
embedding similarity uncertainty
risk uncertainty

The system-design task is not to pretend uncertainty disappears.

It is to contain it.

The core production principle is:

No single probabilistic component gets unilateral authority over an irreversible financial action.

Connection Bridge — From Consuming Architectures to Reconstructing Them Yourself

The practical endpoint of learning all these names is not memorization. It is being able to open an unfamiliar repository or paper and recover the same story yourself: identify the input representation, trace tensor shapes, locate feature-building stages, find fusion paths, distinguish training-only modules from inference modules, inspect the loss and assignment code, and map outputs to the downstream decision.

Once you can do that, a new YOLO version or SAM successor becomes much less intimidating. The labels change faster than the underlying questions.

94. How to Read Papers, Architecture Diagrams, YAML, and Source Code Without Guessing

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous93. One Payment, End to End: A Worked Transaction with Tensors, Timing, Decisions, and Failure Modes
This chapter94. How to Read Papers, Architecture Diagrams, YAML, and Source Code Without Guessing
Next95. Architecture Alternatives for a Real Biometric Payment Product

The goal of studying architecture is eventually to stop needing simplified explanations.

When a new model appears, you should be able to reconstruct it from the paper and repository.

94.1 Start with the task definition

Before architecture diagrams, write down:

Input:
Output:
Training objective:
Inference objective:
Evaluation metric:

Example YOLO detector:

Input: RGB image
Output: boxes + classes + scores
Training: dense/assigned detection losses
Inference: dense or end-to-end detections
Metric: COCO AP + latency

Example ArcFace recognizer:

Input: aligned face
Output: embedding
Training: identity classifier with angular margin
Inference: embedding only
Metric: verification/identification performance

This prevents category confusion.

94.2 Separate backbone, neck, and head

For detectors:

Backbone = extract features
Neck     = combine scales/context
Head     = task outputs

When reading a new YOLO diagram, color-code mentally:

feature extraction
feature fusion
prediction

Do not memorize module names before knowing their role.

94.3 Expand every named block recursively

If paper says:

C2PSA

ask:

What is C2PSA made of?
-> split
-> PSA blocks
-> attention
-> FFN
-> residuals
-> concat/fuse

What is attention made of?
-> Q/K/V projections
-> score matrix
-> softmax
-> weighted value aggregation

Keep expanding until every leaf is an operation you understand.

94.4 Trace tensor shapes

This is the fastest way to catch misunderstanding.

Suppose:

Input [1,3,640,640]
Conv stride 2, C=64

Then:

[1,64,320,320]

Another stride 2:

[1,128,160,160]

If an FPN concatenates an upsampled 40×40 tensor with a 40×40 skip, dimensions must align spatially.

Shape tracing turns a pretty diagram into a computational graph.

94.5 Read parameter counts as sanity checks

For a convolution:

Params=CoutCinK2 Params=C_{out}C_{in}K^2

If documentation claims a tiny module but you compute enormous parameter count, inspect hidden-width scaling or grouped/depthwise convolution.

94.6 Understand YAML format

Ultralytics model YAML uses layer descriptions of the form:

[from, repeats, module, args]

Example:

[-1, 1, Conv, [64, 3, 2]]

Read as approximately:

input from previous layer
repeat once
module = Conv
out channels = 64
kernel = 3
stride = 2

A multi-input concat:

[[-1, 6], 1, Concat, [1]]

means:

concatenate previous output and layer 6 along channel dimension

Do not assume repeats=3 always means three separate graph nodes. Parser logic may pass repeat count into a CSP module as an internal repeat parameter.

94.7 Depth and width scaling

Model families often define base channels/repeats then scale them.

Conceptually:

nano   -> narrow + shallow
small  -> wider/deeper
medium -> wider/deeper
large  -> ...

If YAML says 512 channels, actual instantiated channels may differ after width scaling.

This is why reading only the YAML text without parser/scaling rules can be misleading.

94.8 Follow source definitions

When documentation says C3k2, locate class definition.

Read:

  1. constructor;
  2. child modules;
  3. forward();
  4. tensor split/concat logic;
  5. conditions/flags;
  6. inherited parent class.

The forward() method is often the shortest route to truth.

94.9 Training-only versus inference-only modules

Look for:

if self.training:

or auxiliary outputs used only in loss computation.

A paper architecture diagram may show training branches that are removed after export.

This distinction is essential when estimating deployment cost.

94.10 Read the loss before believing the architecture story

The loss tells you what behavior the model is actually rewarded for.

For detection ask:

  • how are positives assigned?
  • what classification target?
  • what box loss?
  • auxiliary losses?
  • one-to-one or one-to-many?

For recognition:

  • normalized features?
  • normalized classifier weights?
  • margin type?
  • scale parameter?
  • quality adaptation?

For segmentation:

  • mask BCE?
  • Dice?
  • focal?
  • matching?

94.11 Read the ablations

An ablation table asks:

baseline
+ component A
+ component B
+ component C

This is where you learn whether the fancy named component actually contributed.

But be careful:

  • effects may interact;
  • training recipe may change;
  • benchmark noise exists;
  • reported latency hardware matters.

94.12 Compare training data

Never compare two models without asking:

pretraining dataset?
training epochs?
input resolution?
augmentation?
extra unlabeled data?
ensemble?
TTA?

Architecture is only one variable.

94.13 Compare latency honestly

Latency claims are meaningful only with:

  • device;
  • runtime;
  • precision;
  • batch size;
  • image size;
  • preprocessing/postprocessing inclusion;
  • warm/cold state.

3 ms on TensorRT FP16 A100 does not predict CPU kiosk behavior.

94.14 Read DETR diagrams

When you see:

encoder
object queries
decoder

ask:

What are encoder tokens?
What positional encoding?
Where do queries originate?
Do queries carry reference points/boxes?
Is attention full or deformable?
How many decoder layers?
How are boxes refined?
How is matching performed?
Is denoising training used?

That checklist distinguishes DETR generations.

94.15 Read SAM diagrams

Ask:

image encoder/perception encoder?
prompt modalities?
prompt encoder?
fusion stage?
detector?
mask decoder?
memory encoder?
memory bank?
tracker?
presence head?

SAM1, SAM2, and SAM3 differ primarily in what prompts/tasks/temporal structure they support around these roles.

94.16 Read face-recognition papers

Ask:

backbone?
embedding dimension?
training identities/images?
loss?
normalization?
alignment protocol?
test protocol?
quality filtering?
threshold protocol?

A new loss can look superior partly because of different training data.

94.17 Reproduce the computational graph on paper

A good exercise:

Input
[1,3,640,640]
   |
Conv s2
[1,64,320,320]
   |
...

Write the shape after every major block.

For attention, write:

X: [B,N,D]
Q: [B,H,N,d_h]
K: [B,H,N,d_h]
V: [B,H,N,d_h]
A: [B,H,N,N]

If you can do that, you understand the model far more deeply than someone who memorized the acronym.

94.18 The three-source rule for new architectures

For serious understanding, use:

  1. paper — intended idea;
  2. official source code/config — actual implementation;
  3. official docs/model card — deployment/training behavior.

These can disagree subtly.

When they do, code and released checkpoint behavior usually determine what you actually run.

Connection Bridge — Architecture Is a Set of Tradeoffs, Not a Canonical Stack

After studying powerful models, it is tempting to assemble the most sophisticated component at every stage. Real systems usually benefit from the opposite discipline: pick the simplest component that satisfies the measured requirement, then spend complexity where it buys security, robustness, or latency.

A kiosk doing 1:1 verification may not need SAM. A constrained edge device may prefer SCRFD plus a compact recognition backbone. A high-risk environment may justify depth or NIR hardware. A large 1:N system may need ANN retrieval and aggressive candidate re-ranking. The correct architecture depends on threat model, population size, device capability, payment value, privacy constraints, and acceptable fallback behavior.

95. Architecture Alternatives for a Real Biometric Payment Product

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous94. How to Read Papers, Architecture Diagrams, YAML, and Source Code Without Guessing
This chapter95. Architecture Alternatives for a Real Biometric Payment Product
Next96. Research Questions Worth Investigating in Biometric Payment R&D

There is no single correct biometric payment architecture.

The best design depends on product constraints, fraud model, regulation, hardware, user flow, and what “biometric payment” actually means.

95.1 Architecture A — Face as sole 1:N identifier

walk up
  |
camera
  |
face embedding
  |
search entire customer gallery
  |
choose account
  |
authorize payment

Advantages

  • magical/frictionless UX;
  • no explicit account presentation.

Problems

  • huge open-set identification risk;
  • gallery-size effects;
  • bystander/privacy concerns;
  • difficult consent semantics;
  • false-match impact is financially severe;
  • central biometric database becomes extremely sensitive;
  • spoof/injection consequences are large.

This is the architecture I would scrutinize hardest.

95.2 Architecture B — account claim + 1:1 face verification

QR / wallet / card / account token
       |
claimed account
       |
face verification
       |
transaction authorization

Advantages

  • one biometric comparison;
  • simpler threshold calibration;
  • no global gallery search;
  • clear user intent;
  • lower bystander ambiguity.

Tradeoff

The user must present some account context.

From a security-engineering perspective, this is often a much cleaner baseline.

95.3 Architecture C — biometric activates device-bound key

user device / trusted terminal
      |
local face biometric
      |
unlock non-exportable private key
      |
sign exact transaction challenge
      |
payment backend verifies signature

Advantages

  • biometrics remain local;
  • cryptographic possession proves authenticator binding;
  • transaction can be explicitly signed;
  • compromised central biometric DB is less central to the design.

Costs

  • provisioning/recovery complexity;
  • device/terminal secure hardware dependency;
  • cross-device UX.

This pattern resembles how modern platform biometrics are often strongest: biometric as local authenticator activation, not remote bearer credential.

95.4 Architecture D — terminal-bound customer enrollment

Some closed ecosystems—office cafeteria, campus, membership venue—may enroll users to a limited terminal fleet.

Small gallery changes the risk model:

N = 500 users

is different from:

N = 50 million users

But small scale does not remove privacy/PAD/security obligations.

95.5 Architecture E — multimodal biometric

Combine face with another modality:

face
+
voice? fingerprint? palm? device possession?

Do not assume two biometrics automatically create strong MFA. Two biological measurements can share capture/injection risks and may not satisfy authenticator-factor definitions in every standard.

Combining biometric + cryptographic possession is often conceptually stronger.

95.6 Architecture F — face only as fraud/risk signal

Face need not be the authentication factor.

Example:

primary payment authentication
      +
face/account consistency signal
      +
risk engine

This reduces the consequence of one biometric mistake.

Depending on privacy/legal constraints, this may still be sensitive processing and requires strong governance.

95.7 YOLO versus specialized face detector

YOLO

Use when you need rich scene understanding:

  • people;
  • phones;
  • screens;
  • masks/objects;
  • terminal-zone context.

SCRFD/RetinaFace

Use when the key task is efficient face localization + landmarks.

A real system may use both:

scene YOLO
   +
face-specific detector

but justify the extra compute.

95.8 SAM3 in the architecture

SAM3 is useful if the product requires open-vocabulary segmentation/tracking such as:

  • segment all people in a crowded scene;
  • identify/track a concept by text/exemplar;
  • obtain precise masks for privacy redaction or scene interaction.

For ordinary face verification, SAM3 is likely overkill.

This is an important architecture discipline:

use the smallest model that solves the required subproblem reliably.

95.9 Recognition backbone choice

Large IResNet

Pros:

  • strong recognition accuracy;
  • mature ecosystem.

Cons:

  • more compute.

MobileFaceNet / efficient student

Pros:

  • edge-friendly;
  • lower latency/power.

Cons:

  • possible accuracy loss under difficult conditions.

Choice should be based on operating-point performance on target captures, not generic benchmark rank.

95.10 Sensor package choice

RGB only

Cheapest, simplest.

Requires strong software PAD and sensor-integrity controls.

RGB + NIR

Adds controlled illumination/material cues.

RGB + depth/NIR

Stronger physical observability but higher BOM, calibration, and maintenance.

A payment terminal is a hardware-software system. Sensor cost may be cheaper than trying to solve impossible physics with a larger neural model.

95.11 Edge/cloud split options

All local

capture -> PAD -> match locally -> signed assertion

Best privacy/trust potential if hardware supports it.

Hybrid

capture/PAD local
embedding encrypted to backend
central comparison

Central

frames -> cloud
all AI central

Simplest fleet model management but highest network/privacy/trust burden.

Choose intentionally.

95.12 Recommended R&D baseline architecture

For a serious prototype, a defensible starting point is:

Account/transaction claim
        |
Authenticated terminal camera
        |
Face detector + landmarks
        |
Quality gate
        |
PAD
        |
Aligned face
        |
Face embedding
        |
1:1 comparison
        |
Multi-frame stability
        |
Risk policy
        |
Cryptographic transaction binding
        |
Payment authorization

Then experiment with richer models only where evidence shows the baseline fails.

96. Research Questions Worth Investigating in Biometric Payment R&D

WHERE THIS CHAPTER SITS
Previous95. Architecture Alternatives for a Real Biometric Payment Product
This chapter96. Research Questions Worth Investigating in Biometric Payment R&D
Next97. Additional Primary Sources for the Advanced Chapters

If you were building this as an AI R&D program rather than a demo, these are the questions that actually deserve experiments.

96.1 How much does dedicated face detection outperform YOLO face detection on target hardware?

Measure:

  • recall by face size;
  • landmark accuracy;
  • CPU/GPU latency;
  • difficult lighting;
  • multiple faces.

Do not assume specialized is always better; test.

96.2 Does precise segmentation improve PAD?

Hypothesis:

A face/hair/skin mask from segmentation could reduce background leakage in PAD.

Counter-hypothesis:

Segmentation cost adds latency and may introduce another failure mode without enough PAD gain.

Ablate:

PAD raw crop
vs
PAD face mask
vs
PAD skin-region mask

96.3 Does SAM3 add value over lightweight tracking?

Compare:

SAM3 tracker
ByteTrack
DeepSORT
simple face-track Kalman

Metrics:

  • ID switches;
  • latency;
  • GPU memory;
  • occlusion recovery;
  • impact on final biometric error.

The final metric matters more than tracking benchmark scores.

96.4 What is the minimum useful face resolution?

Experiment by downsampling target-domain faces.

Plot:

face width pixels
vs
TAR at target FMR

This directly informs camera/FOV/UX design.

96.5 What capture quality metric best predicts recognition failure?

Compare:

  • generic blur metrics;
  • landmark confidence;
  • MagFace-like quality;
  • dedicated FIQA model;
  • embedding norm where meaningful;
  • learned failure predictor.

Goal:

reject only captures likely to produce unreliable matching.

96.6 How many frames are enough?

Evaluate:

1 frame
3 frames
5 frames
10 frames

Plot error versus latency.

At some point additional frames provide little gain.

96.7 Quality-weighted aggregation versus best-frame selection

Compare:

highest-quality single frame
mean embedding
quality-weighted mean
top-k score mean
temporal model

Evaluate on motion/pose variation.

96.8 ArcFace versus AdaFace under kiosk degradation

ArcFace may dominate clean conditions while quality-adaptive losses can behave differently under blur/low resolution.

Train/evaluate under:

  • strong compression;
  • low light;
  • blur;
  • pose;
  • low resolution.

Do not assume newer loss is automatically better for your domain.

96.9 Can one model jointly estimate face quality and identity safely?

Shared representation could reduce compute.

But coupling can create failure correlation.

Experiment:

shared backbone + quality head
vs
independent quality model

Measure whether quality remains predictive under attack conditions.

96.10 What PAD modality gives the best marginal security per hardware cost?

Compare:

RGB only
RGB + NIR
RGB + depth
RGB + NIR + depth

Against attack matrix:

print
phone replay
OLED replay
3D mask
silicone mask
injection

Injection will expose a key truth: more physical modalities do not help if the attacker bypasses the sensor stack.

96.11 Can sensor attestation be bound to inference?

Research architecture:

camera hardware attestation
      |
secure capture hash/metadata
      |
TEE inference
      |
signed biometric assertion

Goal: prove not only what the model decided, but that it processed data from an approved physical capture path.

96.12 How should thresholds adapt to transaction risk?

One temptation is dynamic biometric thresholds:

low amount -> loose threshold
high amount -> strict threshold

This can complicate calibration and fairness.

A cleaner design may keep a fixed biometric operating threshold and vary step-up policy instead.

Research both and quantify error implications.

96.13 Does 1:N identification remain acceptable at realistic gallery scale?

If considering frictionless face-only identification, evaluate with the actual expected N.

Measure:

  • false positive identification rate;
  • rank-1/rank-k;
  • open-set rejection;
  • latency;
  • demographic effects.

Do not extrapolate from a 10k gallery to 50 million users.

96.14 ANN compression effect on security operating point

Compare:

exact FP32 search
HNSW
IVF
IVF-PQ
compressed template storage

Measure not only recall@k but final false-match behavior after re-ranking.

96.15 Quantization drift

Run identical biometric evaluation for:

FP32
FP16
INT8 PTQ
INT8 QAT

Track score distribution shifts.

An INT8 model can be “99.9% similar in outputs” while still causing disproportionate threshold flips near the operating boundary.

96.16 Model fusion versus independent models

Potential unified network:

shared face backbone
  +--> landmarks
  +--> quality
  +--> PAD
  +--> embedding

Advantages:

  • lower compute;
  • shared features.

Risks:

  • correlated errors;
  • harder certification/evaluation;
  • one compromise affects all functions.

Independent models cost more but give cleaner failure isolation.

This is a genuine system-design tradeoff.

96.17 Attack generalization

Train PAD without attack device X.

Then test X.

Repeat leave-one-attack-family-out.

The objective is not benchmark memorization; it is unknown-attack robustness.

96.18 Deepfake injection detection

Evaluate:

  • camera attestation;
  • frame timing anomalies;
  • sensor noise fingerprints;
  • challenge-response;
  • cryptographic capture binding;
  • software virtual-camera detection.

Do not expect RGB deepfake classifiers alone to carry the entire burden.

96.19 Demographic and accessibility evaluation

Measure pipeline failure rates by relevant populations and conditions.

Include:

  • skin tone where applicable to sensor/recognition behavior;
  • age ranges;
  • eyewear;
  • mobility limitations;
  • height/camera angle;
  • religious/cultural face coverings where legally/ethically appropriate;
  • users unable or unwilling to use biometrics.

The alternative path must be a first-class product path, not a broken fallback.

96.20 Failure containment experiments

Inject failures intentionally:

detector timeout
PAD service crash
stale camera frame
network loss
wrong model version
corrupt embedding
track switch
HSM unavailable

Verify the system fails safely.

This is as important as model accuracy.

96.21 The research mindset

For every new component, ask:

What exact failure is this component supposed to reduce?

What measurement proves it reduced that failure?

What new failure modes did it introduce?

Can a cheaper/simpler component achieve the same system-level result?

That is the difference between architecture collecting and R&D.

  2. NIST SP 800-63B — Authentication and Authenticator Management
    https://pages.nist.gov/800-63-4/sp800-63b.html

Current NIST guidance in its applicable digital-authentication scope states that biometric characteristics are not authenticators by themselves, requires facial PAD, gives explicit biometric performance requirements, discusses injection attacks and sensor integrity, and prefers local biometric comparison over central comparison.

A final note on sources

For fast-moving model families, especially YOLO and SAM, architecture details can change between released versions. When this document conflicts with a future release:

  1. read the specific checkpoint/model card;
  2. inspect the exact release’s configuration and source code;
  3. confirm training/inference branch differences;
  4. record the version used in your experiment.

A model family name is not a stable architecture specification.

Connection Bridge — The Pattern Behind All the Patterns

Across nearly a hundred sections, the same engineering pattern keeps recurring: represent the problem in a form the next stage can solve, then explicitly contain what is lost or uncertain in that representation. Convolution compresses pixels into features. Detection compresses objects into boxes. Alignment reduces pose variation. Embeddings compress faces into identity geometry. ANN compresses a population search into candidates. Risk compresses many uncertain signals into policy. Authorization turns policy into a bounded financial action.

Every compression gains efficiency and loses information. Good architecture is largely the discipline of knowing what was lost, whether the loss matters, and which independent controls catch the failure when it does.

Final Synthesis — How to Think About the Entire System

After all the individual architectures, the most useful abstraction is still the system decomposition:

PHYSICAL WORLD
    |
    v
SENSOR PHYSICS
camera / RGB / NIR / depth / timing
    |
    v
CAPTURE TRUST
attestation / secure path / freshness
    |
    v
PERCEPTION
face/person detection, scene understanding, tracking
    |
    v
QUALITY + GEOMETRY
landmarks, alignment, pose, blur, exposure
    |
    v
PRESENTATION ATTACK DETECTION
physical authenticity evidence
    |
    v
IDENTITY REPRESENTATION
ResNet/IResNet/MobileFaceNet/ViT-style backbone
trained with ArcFace/AdaFace/etc.
    |
    v
EMBEDDING GEOMETRY
normalized representation on hypersphere
    |
    v
MATCHING / RETRIEVAL
1:1 cosine comparison or carefully designed 1:N search
    |
    v
BIOMETRIC EVIDENCE
score + quality + PAD + temporal consistency
    |
    v
AUTHENTICATION / CRYPTOGRAPHIC BINDING
prove possession + transaction intent
    |
    v
RISK POLICY
amount, velocity, device, terminal, account, anomalies
    |
    v
PAYMENT AUTHORIZATION
irreversible financial action

The model families now fit into this map naturally:

YOLO / RetinaFace / SCRFD
    -> localization

FPN / PAN / CSP / C2f / C3k2 / C2PSA
    -> feature extraction and fusion mechanics

DETR / DINO / RT-DETR
    -> query-based set prediction

SAM1 / SAM2 / SAM3
    -> promptable masks, concepts, tracking, memory

ResNet / IResNet / MobileFaceNet
    -> identity feature backbones

ArcFace / CurricularFace / MagFace / AdaFace
    -> embedding-space training objectives

HNSW / IVF / PQ
    -> large-gallery retrieval

Kalman / SORT / DeepSORT / ByteTrack / BoT-SORT
    -> temporal association

PAD models + RGB/NIR/depth/rPPG
    -> presentation-attack evidence

ONNX / TensorRT / OpenVINO / LiteRT
    -> runtime deployment

HSM / TEE / attestation / signatures
    -> cryptographic trust and transaction binding

The deepest lesson is not a model name.

It is this:

A secure biometric payment system is a controlled composition of noisy estimators, physical sensors, cryptographic identities, and financial policy. The architecture succeeds when each component is given only the authority its evidence justifies, and when every failure is contained before it can become an unauthorized payment.
